## Capítulo 1 — Investigations Management (págs. 1–78)

**Central idea:** An investigation is a lawful, methodical fact-finding process that leads to a reasonable conclusion. Managing it well takes five qualities, a structure that fits the company (in-house, outsourced, or mixed), and control at every phase of the life cycle. In the private sector, the interest of the company governs, not public punishment.

### 1.1 The Nature of Investigation Management

- **Investigation**, according to the ANSI/ASIS INV.1-2015 standard (ANSI: American National Standards Institute; ASIS: ASIS International):
  - It is a fact-finding process.
  - It gathers and documents information in a logical, methodical, and lawful way.
  - Its aim is to reach, objectively, a reasonable conclusion based on the facts found.
- The investigator may not be asked for conclusions. Their results and interviews guide others, for example legal counsel.
- The definition applies equally in the public and private sectors. It covers everything from background checks to administrative inquiries and criminal matters.
- **Management**, according to Merriam-Webster (2020): the act of conducting or supervising something. Also: “judicious use of means to accomplish an end.”

#### Purposes of an investigation (Webster University 2020)

Especially in business, the purpose falls into one or more of these 8 categories:

| # | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Thoroughly document incidents, for example those reported to security personnel or officers. |
| 2 | Identify the cause of undesirable situations when malicious activity is suspected. Examples: notable losses, a sudden drop in revenue or market share, unexplained shrinkage, inventory discrepancies, defalcation. |
| 3 | Document and correlate the facts of any situation or allegation of misconduct or inappropriate behavior. |
| 4 | Identify, interview, or monitor suspects in a crime or misconduct. |
| 5 | Gather information that proves or refutes an allegation, or that implicates or exonerates a suspect. |
| 6 | Allow a trust-level or suitability determination. It may concern a person (e.g., personnel security clearance) or an organization (e.g., a potential partner or acquisition target). |
| 7 | Conduct threat assessments to prevent workplace violence, whether internal or from third parties. |
| 8 | Gather crime data and other material in a site inspection, to mitigate the company’s liability and risks. |

- **Defalcation**, according to 1.1: failing to account properly for money held as a fiduciary.
- Exam note: the book defines “defalcation” in two ways. In 1.1 it is failing to account for money held in a fiduciary capacity. In 1.9.1 it is misappropriating entrusted property, which is the proper definition of embezzlement.

#### Purpose, at two levels

| Level | What the purpose does |
| --- | --- |
| Case level | Sets the context of the case. Keeps focused those who work it and those who manage it. |
| Strategic level | Dictates the planning, organization, equipment, staffing, and preparation needed. |

- Unit heads and security directors must anticipate all possible purposes. They concentrate resources on the most frequent cases, but think ahead about the others.

### 1.2 Qualities of an Effective Investigation

- There are five attributes:
  1. objectivity;
  2. thoroughness;
  3. relevance;
  4. accuracy;
  5. timeliness.
- Management must ensure them at the strategic level and in each case. It does so with two things:
  - a system that facilitates them;
  - good case review and quality control.
- The investigative process is science and art. Much of the art lies in these qualities.

| Quality | Short definition (book’s box) |
| --- | --- |
| Objectivity | Remain impartial and use a rational investigative hypothesis, without introducing prejudice. |
| Thoroughness | Follow all relevant leads to their logical conclusion. Corroborate all key findings. |
| Relevance | Decide whether information applies to the case, with just the right breadth and depth. Without accumulating so much that it confuses, bogs down, or hides the truth. |
| Accuracy | The credibility of a source. It may be human, physical or electronic evidence, or the result of an observation or surveillance. |
| Timeliness | Finish quickly, but not too quickly. Resist outside pressures that rush or slow the case and harm its resolution. |

#### 1.2.1 Objectivity

- **Definition:** the ability to remain objective and focused.
  - Information, evidence, and interviews are reported without internal or external bias.
  - Prejudices and unfounded hypotheses are avoided.
- It requires setting aside feelings, opinions, and outside influences. It is among the hardest things, even for an expert, because it goes against human nature.
- **Working hypothesis:**
  - It can be formed at the start or later, and change one or more times.
  - It is a valid tool as long as it respects objectivity.
  - A logical hypothesis starts from understanding the information, the evidence, and the mission context.
  - If it is not based on evidence, confirmed information, and known facts, it usually leads down the wrong path.
- Typical mistake: trying to prove a theory instead of following every lead to its logical conclusion.
- Do not jump to conclusions without independently corroborating the facts. This holds even in the face of seemingly overwhelming evidence. The facts must speak for themselves.
- Each investigator must recognize their biases and neutralize them, including when forming the hypothesis. Findings form impressions, not the other way around.
- **Approach and demeanor:**
  - The first thing is to project objectivity.
  - This is achieved by watching your words, facial expressions, and body language.
  - If you act like someone who knows what they are doing, others will assume it.
- **An unsolved murder case:**
  - Investigators assumed the culprit was a prison escapee, because his father lived next door to the victim.
  - While chasing him, they ignored leads and physical evidence.
  - When he was captured, it turned out he was in a county jail for public intoxication when the crime occurred.
  - Years later, a cold case investigator followed the physical evidence.
  - A bloody fingerprint matched the victim’s boyfriend. Time-clock records disproved his alibi.
  - The boyfriend confessed, gave details that confirmed it, and asked why it had taken so long.
  - Lesson: a prior hypothesis blinds the investigator; follow the evidence.
  - Note: the book tells this case twice (pp. 3 and 4), with details that complement each other.

#### 1.2.2 Thoroughness

- Review all leads, and the key ones more than once, to check their consistency.
- Corroborate what is important with different sources and, where possible, of different types. Types the book cites (open list):
  - interviewees or witnesses;
  - subject matter experts;
  - physical evidence;
  - electronic evidence;
  - public records;
  - surveillance results;
  - open sources;
  - databases.
- Also corroborate the statements of witnesses and of the subject of interest.
- **A case with 13 victims:** for six months, a suspect in jail gave detailed alibis for each date. The investigator showed that all were false. The suspect confessed when confronted with his deception.
- Some leads generate others, secondary and tertiary. They seem superfluous, but may be valuable directly or as corroboration.
- Two things must be balanced: following every possible lead, but without accumulating so much information that it confuses or hinders.
- That is why independent case management matters, in both the public and private sectors.
- There is often pressure to cut the investigation short for lack of time or resources, especially in business.
- Document all leads and all information. What seems irrelevant today may be important later.

#### 1.2.3 Relevance

- Information must relate to the subject of the investigation and be at the right level of detail. Deciding how much to dig is another difficult part of the job.
- It has two determinants:

| Determinant | What it decides | Book example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spectrum of details | How wide a net is cast | Robbery in an office building: do you investigate construction at the building next door? Answer: “maybe.” Child sexual abuse case in a neighborhood: the dates coincided with the start and end of a local construction project. The suspect came from the crew and confessed. |
| Depth of details | How deep you dig | Asked “What did you do yesterday?”, one person answers with excessive detail (bed, teeth, face, shaving, cereal, plate to the sink). Another says only “I got up, went to work, came back.” The right answer is in between. |

- **Scripting an alibi:** an employee with access to the safe volunteered, without being asked, a minute-by-minute log of where he had been. The sheets bore a date prior to the theft.
- With some interviewees it is hard to get the right depth:
  - the less intelligent;
  - the very intelligent (e.g., scientists and engineers);
  - the nervous;
  - those in a hurry;
  - those hiding something;
  - the very quiet;
  - the very talkative.
- The skilled interviewer steers the conversation to the right detail without diminishing the value of the information. They use open-ended questions to keep information flowing.
- **Cause and effect:**
  - Bad credit history in a background investigation: it is not enough to report it; its cause must be found and verified.
    - It may be identity theft.
    - Or debts inherited on the death of a close relative, until the estate is settled.
    - If it is already resolved, the weakness disappears.
  - The candidate’s criminal record: it is wise to discuss it with the candidate, for three reasons.
    - Court and police records show the conviction, not the facts behind it.
    - Records may be incomplete.
    - Government guidelines call for assessing the relevance of the conviction and possible rehabilitation.
  - If the candidate admits and describes a disqualifying crime, they are confessing by self-disclosure.

#### 1.2.4 Accuracy

- According to the National Research Council (2014), eyewitnesses are key to identifying culprits, but they make mistakes. Their memory can be altered even by the police procedures that test it.
- **Innocence Project (2020):** erroneous witness identifications contributed to more than 70% of wrongful convictions in the United States later overturned with post-conviction DNA evidence.
- The mental processes that organize what the senses take in make errors.
  - In the clear air of Arizona, a mountain seems to be half a mile away and is three miles away.
  - In a training exercise, one observer described a suspect as short, chubby, with a green golf shirt, light brown hair, and a mustache.
  - Another observer described him as thin, of medium height, with a blue T-shirt, dark hair, and a mustache.
- In business, many employee tips about losses, sexual harassment, and other incidents are credible. But employees also give many false or misunderstood tips.
- **Verification rules:**
  - Check frequently, as in a financial audit, which assesses the accuracy, timeliness, and completeness of records.
  - If something can be physically measured, it must be measured.
  - If an informant is the only source of a key fact, it must at least be tested for consistency.
  - Look for inherent contradictions in the information itself. Example: a car trip that, given distance and time, would require traveling at 135 miles per hour.
  - Examine the informant’s motives, because they can bias how complete and accurate their information is.
- **Accept nothing at first glance:**
  - Date and time stamps from computers, photo or video cameras, and cell phones fail if the internal clock is set wrong.
  - An access control record showing someone passing through a door is suspect until two things are checked.
    - Whether the system can be tampered with or spoofed.
    - Whether another person used that person’s card.
- **Background and preemployment:** some believe the listed references the candidate gives are useless, because they are friends. Experience says otherwise: they often provide unfavorable information. That fact forces you to think about the interviewee’s motives.

#### 1.2.5 Timeliness

- You must act without delay to take advantage of memory and evidence before they are lost. Three rules:
  1. Open the investigation as soon as possible.
  2. Complete it as quickly as possible.
  3. Do not close it prematurely.
- They seem contradictory; the key is balance.
- ASIS International standard (2015): investigate as soon as possible, respecting the requirements of the jurisdiction. This prevents human, physical, or electronic evidence from degrading.
- Witnesses report better with the event fresh. Over time:
  - memory fades;
  - it is contaminated with rationalizations, as they think about the consequences of their answers;
  - it is colored by news, talks with other witnesses, and other influences.
- Surveillance video can be lost if there is a delay. Many systems record a fixed number of days and then overwrite.
- Finishing quickly serves to:
  - save resources;
  - return operations to normal quickly;
  - deter other potential offenders;
  - show professionalism, which increases the effectiveness of the investigation or security team.
- You must not rush at the expense of quality, thoroughness, or accuracy.
  - Typical pressures to close: lack of time or resources, publicity, and political factors. Where possible, do not give in.
  - The unit head or security director may have to explain to senior management the harms of rushing, including greater legal risk.
- Not all cases are solved, either quickly or ever. If management believes otherwise, it expects too much of the investigative unit (IU) or the security department.
- Remedy: regular, timely communication between security or investigation managers and the organization’s decision makers.

### 1.3 Public Sector versus Private Sector Investigations

- Tools, techniques, and processes are similar. The fundamental difference is in whom each serves.

| Aspect | Public sector (generally, law enforcement) | Private sector |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Aim | Serve and protect society at large | Protect the interests of the company that employs it |
| How it is measured | Number of arrests and convictions | Recovery, restitution, litigation mitigation, and risk reduction |
| What it seeks in an internal crime | Prosecute and imprison the offender | Some combination of accepting restitution and taking administrative or employment action |
| Standard of proof | Often higher | Dictated by the outcome sought in the case |
| Scope | A specific act or criminal violation | Broader: policy violations and underlying system problems that call for changing processes or policies |

- **Public strategies for a series of thefts:**
  - proactive, with surveillance;
  - reactive, assigning more personnel to solve the cases. Crime scene investigation is a reactive measure.
- **Public agencies with an investigative mission:**
  - national and local law enforcement;
  - regional or special-purpose units (airport, transit, or park authorities);
  - intelligence and counterintelligence agencies;
  - inspector general offices;
  - regulatory agencies that enforce rules or administrative laws.
- **Private actors**, in two categories:
  1. In-house investigative or security units within a company or organization.
  2. Companies that provide investigative services or special expertise on a fee basis.
- The chapter focuses on in-house units, but mentions outside providers where applicable.
- **The role of private investigation is growing because:**
  - the police with jurisdiction often decide not to investigate because of their workload and other priorities;
  - public resources are limited and some crimes are of no interest to conventional police;
  - the police are barred from investigating civil or internal company matters, unless there is a specific crime.
- The difference affects four things: legal authority, resource allocation, program management, and outcomes.
- **Burden of proof:**
  - In US criminal courts, the prosecutor must prove beyond a reasonable doubt.
  - In civil matters, preponderance of evidence decides.
  - Prosecuting someone usually requires more proof than disciplining or firing them.
- Even if restitution and dismissal are chosen over criminal prosecution, the investigation must be done professionally and reach a reasonable conclusion.

#### Six differences between public and private investigation (ASIS International investigations standard, 2015)

1. Powers of arrest.
2. Search and seizure.
3. Testimony.
4. Prosecution.
5. Due process.
6. Consequences.

#### Acceptable outcomes and the role of legal counsel

- At the start of the private case, the company must define which outcome or outcomes it accepts. To do so it considers:
  - the legal requirements of the jurisdiction;
  - current or under-negotiation union contracts;
  - public and investor relations;
  - what the company did before in similar cases.
- It may be wise to have clear policies on outcomes and the measures that follow. Legal counsel is always consulted.
- A formal relationship must be created between the investigation and legal counsel, in-house or outside.
  - This protects the results, exhibits, and data from discovery by the opposing party in a trial.
  - The information becomes **attorney work product**.
  - The investigator communicates only through the assigned attorney, or with those the attorney approves.

#### Other features of private units

- Corporate investigators usually focus on one or a few types of case.
  - Example: at one company, 90% of the time goes to theft.
  - Other units focus on safety violations, employee misconduct, sexual harassment, or property damage.
- Even so, they must be ready for unusual cases with little notice. There are two routes:
  - maintain some capability of their own (expertise, training, equipment);
  - have outside resources to call on.
- IU leaders must make their expertise known and build relationships throughout the company. Otherwise the company will assign investigations to human resources or legal counsel and will not use the IU.
- Corporate IUs also handle external matters. This happens at internet providers, telecommunications companies, insurers, and utilities, whose services attract fraud or illegal use.
- Internal problems arise from missing or weak internal controls.
- Many companies separate the internal-case unit from the external-case one. Advantages:
  - focus on a single activity;
  - special expertise;
  - liaison networks;
  - contacts with prosecutors;
  - less confusion about outcomes and processes.
- Some organizations hire outside firms for their investigations, internal or external.
- In many places, the public sector sometimes hires a private firm for specific expertise (e.g., forensic accounting) within a police investigation. This may signal more public-private cooperation.
- Private IUs also use outside experts, for example in the face of acts or threats of workplace violence. Assessing the threat and interviewing the person of interest calls for experience the IU almost never has.

### 1.4 Management Issues in Investigations

- Management is key to the success of security and criminal investigations in business.
- Managing investigations uses the five basic management functions. All five apply at the three levels.

**Figure 1.1 — Investigative management functions at each level (source: Webster University 2020)**

| Five functions (apply at all three levels) | Three levels |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Plan · 2. Organize · 3. Direct · 4. Coordinate · 5. Control | Strategic · Operational · Case |

| Level | What it covers | Topics it addresses |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Strategic | High-level management of the whole unit and its relationship with senior management. Legal counsel should take part here, to focus cases according to company policy and civil or criminal law. Investigators are formally hired here, to have the protection of attorney work product. | Who leads the function. Where it fits in the structure. What its strategic goals and objectives are. Where it focuses its efforts. How financial and personnel resources are assigned to it. |
| Operational | The technical aspects of the function and how it works within its department. | Caseload, case evaluation, quality control, investigative policies and procedures, report formats, liaison, team composition, supplies and equipment, evidence management, outside contracts. |
| Case | Each individual investigation (Webster University 2020). | The assigned investigators, investigative techniques, and case management protocols. |

- The person responsible for the function may be the IU head, the chief security officer (CSO), the security director, or the director of investigations. The book allows any other title.
- That person must think at once about the three levels and the factors that cut across them.

#### Goals, objectives, and metrics

| Concept | Features |
| --- | --- |
| Goals | Long-term, overall end states. They may be expressed in general terms. They must be consistent with the organization’s strategic direction. They are included in the IU’s mission statement. |
| Objectives | They come from the goals, but are more specific and may be more immediate. They are generally measurable. They serve to measure the unit’s progress, success, or achievements. |
| Metrics | The specific and measurable. They serve to evaluate people, teams, and organizations. They must be tied to the general goals, not be an end in themselves. |

- Exam note: the book calls “specific, measurable goals” “metrics.” Just before, it says goals are general and that what is measurable are objectives.
- Every investigative strategy must be compatible with the organization’s strategic business plan.
- An investigation’s objectives are tactical and respond to what is expected in the short term.
  - The IU head consults the tactical business plan so they are compatible.
  - Example: a complaint arises within an undercover project. If an investigation is opened, its objectives must be consistent with those of the project.
  - Changes to operational objectives are considered carefully and treated as “change orders,” as in project management.

#### Ethics

- It is a theme that cuts across the three levels.
- Organizational measures with great impact:
  - publish written standards of ethical conduct;
  - adopt a zero-tolerance policy toward ethical lapses;
  - sanction those who commit them.
- Without sound ethical standards, the effectiveness of the IU and of each investigator suffers. Both depend on their credibility and respectability to gather, corroborate, and integrate facts.
- Almost all human sources provide information out of some degree of trust in the investigator or agency:
  - victims;
  - witnesses;
  - first responders;
  - liaison contacts;
  - subject matter experts;
  - informants.
- A reputation for lacking ethics reduces the quantity, accuracy, and candor of information. It also lowers respect for the unit within the company, and with it how the unit is used and how its members are treated.

**Unethical or dishonest conduct that is unacceptable (9):**

1. Opening, closing, speeding up, or slowing down cases for convenience, because of a relationship with the victim, the suspect, or another key player, or for personal gain.
2. Mishandling evidence to influence the outcome.
3. Mishandling evidence or information through incompetence.
4. Fabricating evidence or information.
5. Making improper threats or promises when interviewing a suspected employee.
6. Leaking or compromising sensitive investigative information.
7. Using techniques that are unscientific, unreliable, inappropriate, or needlessly heavy-handed.
8. Mistreating liaison contacts, for example by giving them misleading or false information, or taking advantage of the relationship.
9. Lying in a judicial or administrative proceeding.

- Any of these behaviors merits the most severe punishment.
- It taints all of the offender’s prior cases: everything they did in their post before being discovered becomes suspect. In addition, it can be demanded as evidence at trial (discoverable) and is often admitted.
- Lack of ethics often leads to replacing IU leaders.
  - Case: a senior vice president of corporate investigations was fired. It came out that some memoranda addressed to local management were not made known in time to the other employees. The jury found the company negligent for failure to warn.
  - Pending: the case appears cut off in the text; it is not clear what specific ethical lapse was charged.
- Lack of ethics also exposes the organization to civil or criminal liability.
- IU heads must make ethics a pillar of their operations, procedures, and relationships, and instill it in staff.
- A case is resolved sooner by the constant pursuit of truth than by aggressive tactics or deception.

### 1.5 Organizing for Investigations

- If there is no IU, or one is being expanded or redesigned, one of the first decisions is how to organize the function.

| Type of organization | Logical option |
| --- | --- |
| Small, with few investigative needs | Outsource to an outside agency or service. Many have consultants or private detectives on retainer to respond quickly. You pay only per case, and only when needed. |
| Large, or with constant need | A full-time investigator or team may be justified. |

- Most medium and large companies have enough work to keep an investigator or a team busy. All the more so because investigations and regulatory compliance are increasingly complex.
- **Matters that call for investigation in business:**
  - personnel background investigations (BIs);
  - product security or product tampering;
  - white-collar crimes: procurement fraud, other types of fraud, and embezzlement;
  - employee misconduct;
  - theft and pilferage;
  - information loss;
  - due diligence;
  - abuse of information technology (IT) systems;
  - regulatory compliance.
- **Compliance investigation:** its main purpose is to provide guidance on possible violations of regulatory requirements.

#### Cost, budget, and return

- Cost almost always weighs heavily in deciding whether to outsource or have in-house capability. Cost elements:
  - personnel expenses;
  - contracts and retainers;
  - equipment;
  - consumables;
  - overhead;
  - labor;
  - office expenses.
- Return on investment (ROI) can be shown with restitution figures.
- Cost-benefit analysis helps decide whether to outsource the unit.
- **Beware of restitution as the main proof of value:**
  - Some suspects agree to pay to avoid criminal prosecution and later claim they were coerced.
  - The investigator must not touch restitution until the subject confesses or the facts allow a reliable conclusion.
- **The budget is hard to predict:** one or two unexpected large cases drive up costs.
- **Recommended bases for estimating it:**
  - historical data;
  - quantitative estimates of the risk avoided;
  - support for the organization’s goals;
  - anticipated changes.
- Changes in the company (growth, new facilities, international expansion, new markets) and changes in regulation can move the budget a great deal.
- Without a history, you must benchmark against companies similar in size, location, and mission.
- These costs must also be added:
  - equipment;
  - supplies;
  - outside services;
  - local and out-of-area travel;
  - administrative support;
  - contract or personnel management.
- Section 1.9.2 expands on the topic of funding.
- Outsourced investigations can be charged to the business area where they are done or for which they are done. That cost burdens the user’s budget, and only for the actual service.
- The IU must also set spending limits for outside consultants. Setting timelines and benchmarks allows information to be reviewed and a cost-benefit analysis to be done.

**Figure 1.2 — Investigative services: outsourced versus in-house (source: Innovative Protection Solutions, LLC, 2020)**

|  | Outsourcing | Internal unit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | 1. Staff can be expanded or reduced according to actual need. 2. Equipment, supplies, and training become the contractor’s responsibility. 3. Specialists and experts are called in when needed. 4. May be more cost-effective. 5. Reduces overhead. 6. Investigators may have a broader base of expertise and experience. 7. Gives the investigation an independent, external, and objective image. | 1. The company fully controls the process and the people. 2. Allows closer liaison with local agencies and internal contacts. 3. Better protects sensitive information and keeps it within the organization. 4. Investigators usually know the organization, its mission, its culture, and its structure much better. |
| Disadvantages | 1. Quality control is left almost entirely in the contractor’s hands. 2. Investigators may know less about the client’s company, mission, organization, and culture. 3. Requires contract management effort. | 1. Maintaining in-house capability is expensive. 2. Equipment must be maintained that may go unused for long periods. 3. A staff must be maintained, with its training, licenses, and personnel management. 4. Especially if there is a trial, the investigation can be tainted by being done under the company’s direction. |

### 1.6 Fitting the Investigative Mission into the Organization

- The place and use of the IU vary greatly among organizations.
  - Many companies have the IU within the security department or under the CSO.
  - Others have no in-house investigative capability.
- Ideally, the structure is decided with a needs assessment and a cost-benefit analysis.
- It is often decided arbitrarily, without criteria or without context.
- Senior security, assets protection, or risk management professionals should advise management. The advice covers the real investigative needs and the most effective structure to meet them.

#### Most common structures (7)

| # | Structure | Reports to |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Separate IU, with unit head or senior investigator | Corporate security director or CSO |
| 2 | Separate IU, with unit head or senior investigator | Risk management or assets protection director |
| 3 | Separate IU, with unit head or senior investigator | Legal department |
| 4 | Specialized investigations supported by internal audit or by IT | Pending: the book does not say to whom they report |
| 5 | Independent function, such as an inspector general or equivalent | Directly to senior executive management |
| 6 | The security director investigates in person, because there is no IU or investigator | Not applicable |
| 7 | The security director oversees an outsourced capability. Calls an outside firm or investigator under a prior agreement | Not applicable |

- **Regional IUs:** in large or widely dispersed companies. Advantages:
  - they save travel time and expenses;
  - their investigators know the local scene: culture, geography, procedures, laws, and regulations;
  - they work better with local liaison contacts.
- **Units by business area.** Examples:
  - one IU for the commercial division and another for the government division;
  - one for consumer products and another for business-to-business.

#### Areas of focus

- The main need varies. In some organizations it is personnel screening. In others it is misconduct, external theft, or fraud prevention and detection.
- The main focus guides how:
  - the unit is organized;
  - staff are hired and trained;
  - the office is set up;
  - equipment and supplies are purchased;
  - communication channels are created;
  - relationships are established.
- Managers must assume, and tell management, that other types of case will arise. Example: a background-focused IU may suddenly have an assault or theft case.
- Investigators must be ready for that, mentally and in equipment, contacts, and outside vendors.
- The network of liaison contacts, outside sources, specialists, and equipment providers is adjusted to the main focus, with some flexibility.
- Whatever the structure, a specific person is named, with an alternate, to manage outside resources. This provides continuity and allows capabilities to be activated quickly.
- Investigative needs often arise with little notice and on a surge basis.

#### Reporting chain

- It is critical: it affects the outcome of cases and the effectiveness of the unit.
- In general, the shortest chain between the source of the information and the decision maker is best.
- If a trial is expected or company fault is suspected, the investigator delivers the report directly and only to the company’s legal counsel of record.
  - This lets them protect the entire investigation as attorney work product.
  - The attorney maintains attorney-client privilege when advising the company.
- **The final decision maker** may be the chief executive officer (CEO), the chief operating officer (COO), the head of legal, the president, or another executive.
  - You must identify them, win their trust, and create a formal reporting mechanism.
  - It is wise to have an alternate channel in case that person is unavailable or is implicated in the matter.
- The reporting chain does not have to follow the day-to-day organization chart. If departing from it clashes with the company culture, an inspector general with a direct, independent channel may work better.

#### Physical location

- In some cases the aim is to be near the areas dealt with most:
  - the executive office;
  - general counsel;
  - human resources;
  - internal audit;
  - IT;
  - security.
- In other cases it is better to be far from the areas that try to micromanage investigations.
- More detail in section 1.8.4.

#### Changes over time

- The function can grow or shrink with the business environment.
  - With cutbacks, a company with IUs worldwide may reduce their number.
  - Or it may eliminate regional units and concentrate everything at headquarters.
  - Outsourcing in those regions may be the solution.
- **Threat management team (TMT):** another possible response.
  - It is multidisciplinary: human resources, safety, security, legal, and other areas according to the company’s objectives.
  - Depending on the type of case, it is activated at the start to contribute different expert views.
  - It weighs in on disciplinary measures and recommends changes to procedures and policies.
  - It is very useful in workplace violence cases.
- **The company’s response to crime and unethical conduct also changes:**
  - Before, it could pursue all complaints vigorously, internal and external.
  - The new model may be limited to cases with large restitution or recovery, or high-profile ones.
  - Or it may concentrate resources on certain types, for example a heavy hand with IT abuse and a blind eye to minor thefts.
  - Government regulations may also dictate steps and actions.
- These cycles of emphasis follow the company’s financial health, the evolution of the sector, or public awareness of a problem.
  - After the attacks of September 11, 2001, on the World Trade Center and the Pentagon, the FBI (Federal Bureau of Investigation) shifted much of its focus from organized and economic crime to counterterrorism.
  - After the Enron and WorldCom scandals (2001 and 2002), companies gave much more weight to fraud and financial mismanagement.
- Those responsible must give the function agility to meet its objectives in a changing environment.

### 1.7 Outsourcing Investigations

- For a corporate investigative function, outsourcing can mean two things:
  1. Outsourcing the whole function to an outside agency under contract.
  2. Contracting only selected or specialized support services, when needed.
- The decision and approach are usually based on a cost-benefit analysis and anticipated needs.
- **Reasons to outsource:**
  - almost all organizations watch costs;
  - many investigate only occasionally;
  - some prefer an independent source;
  - a corporate policy that encourages outsourcing peripheral services.
- Contracting for the full-scope service is less common than contracting as-needed.
- Outsourcing specialized and expert services is almost always an important part of the budget.

#### Specialized services that commonly support corporate investigations (19)

1. Computer forensics.
2. Forensic auditing.
3. Handwriting analysis.
4. Questioned document examination.
5. Statement analysis.
6. Workplace violence assessments and intervention.
7. Harassment and sexual harassment complaints.
8. Forensic psychology.
9. Forensic polygraph, where legal.
10. Technical surveillance or countersurveillance, to deter criminal activity.
11. Investigative project management: specialized projects, undercover work, labor disputes (open list in the book).
12. Criminal behavioral science or profiling.
13. Physical evidence collection.
14. Laboratory services.
15. Audio or video enhancement.
16. Criminal intelligence analysis.
17. Brand integrity investigations that require geographic density.
18. International investigations that call for local knowledge or contacts.
19. Investigations where there is a perceived conflict of interest.

#### Choosing a vendor

- There are two key decisions:
  - the size of the preferred vendor;
  - whether services will be distinct or bundled.

| Vendor | Advantages and warnings |
| --- | --- |
| Large, national or international in scope | Brings many resources to the project. |
| Small or local | More personal service and local experience. May be the recommended choice for specialized or expert support. Be wary if it claims to be expert in everything: it is extremely rare to master computer forensics, ballistics, background checks, and executive kidnapping all at once. |
| Both | There may be differences in cost and availability. |

- At any size, clear criteria must be set and applied. It is recommended to pre-approve vendors with a vetting process.

**Questions to identify and select a vendor (18):**

1. Does it have a reputation for ethical and honest service?
2. Has it demonstrated the technical expertise the client needs?
3. Is it licensed in all the areas to which the investigation might lead?
4. Are its investigators trained and certified in the techniques and equipment they will use?
5. Has it served other companies in the same sector as the client?
6. Has it served other companies in the same geographic area?
7. Does it have staff and physical resources for short-notice, long-term, and high-demand needs?
8. Does it have a staff of investigators with talent, experience, and stability?
9. How does it bill? Includes peripheral services, on-demand and subcontracted, and reimbursable expenses.
10. Does it maintain professional relationships with liaison contacts, agencies, and other firms (including possible subcontractors) that improve its effectiveness?
11. What mechanisms does it use to ensure quality and receive feedback on the contract?
12. What formal and informal mechanisms does it use to communicate with the client, in routine and extraordinary matters?
13. Is it known for protecting its clients’ information and interests?
14. If it provides the full service, does it have its own capability for specialized and one-off support? For example: surveillance, technical services, computer forensics.
15. What is the professional background of its principal partners and key staff?
16. What is its history of acquisitions and mergers?
17. What professional, civic, and business associations does it belong to?
18. Does it have sufficient professional liability insurance?

#### 1.7.1 Identifying Potential Vendors

- Recommendations from colleagues and peers (word of mouth). It is a form of benchmarking that provides candid and valuable information.
- Credible professional associations or networks. Example: Intellenet (International Intelligence Network), which lists vetted detectives and investigative firms.
- Private detective associations in most US states, Canadian provinces, and countries of the world.
  - They often impose on their members an ethical code and training and licensing requirements.
  - They often have a vetting process.
  - They help find qualified vendors by region.
- **When contracting:** the IU head must ensure that key clauses pass down to the vendor’s consultants or subcontractors. Especially on these topics:
  - evidence handling;
  - information protection;
  - public disclosure of information;
  - licensing requirements;
  - availability to testify;
  - legal liability.
- Ultimate responsibility for ensuring that contracted investigators have training and licenses rests with the contracting organization.
- It must be checked thoroughly during selection. Some unscrupulous firms interpret lightly the requirements for licensing, training, and weapons qualification.

### 1.8 Establishing an IU

- There is no typical structure for the investigative function in business.
- Factors that define it: the sector, and the mission, size, and scope of the company.
- When creating or redesigning the function, the IU head starts from what was covered in this chapter.
- They work with senior management so that the decision is not driven by emotion, improvisation, or lack of information.
- The structure and operation of the function can greatly influence the financial and operational health of the organization.

#### 1.8.1 Functional Charters and Policy Statements

There are three levels of defining the unit:

| Level | Document | Content and features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Functional charter | Gives credibility and authority to the IU. It is issued by the CEO or an equivalent position. It is brief, often a page or less. It sets the purpose and direction of the function and its strategic goals. It defines who is responsible, the nature and main emphasis of the function, and its essential internal relationships. |
| 2 | Policy statement | Describes how investigations are initiated and conducted. It addresses reporting channels, coordination mechanisms, and final resolution (disposition). It also governs other departments: for example, it explains to human resources how to request background investigations. |
| 3 | Objectives | Unlike the previous two, they are reviewed often, normally every year. They include performance metrics and improvement goals for the next period. |

- Doing these three documents well at the start greatly helps the unit’s startup and long-term effectiveness.
- The next step is to obtain the resources, based on those documents. There are three categories: human, physical, and other.

#### 1.8.2 Human Resources

- **Size of the unit.** It is estimated with these factors:
  - the expected caseload;
  - the type of cases;
  - the geographic area covered;
  - the administrative and other support needs.
- If it cannot be projected or compared, the best approach is to start small, outsource when needed, and grow over time.
- **The type of case weighs heavily on size.**
  - An investigator can handle many more background cases than complex procurement fraud.
  - A fraud-focused unit needs more investigators, with more experience, and more administrative support.
- **The investigator’s three “I”s:**
  1. information;
  2. interrogation;
  3. instrumentation.
- Even so, the complexity and profile of the cases dictate the training and experience required.
- Many positions call for special fields: computer investigation, contract fraud, or financial crimes.
- The company’s internal unit is called a proprietary IU.

| Investigator from the public sector or police | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Situation | Historically, a large percentage of corporate investigative staff comes from there. |
| Advantages | Experience, excellent training, and a proven performance record. Eases liaison with police and other first responders. |
| Risk | May not fit the company. Must move well in a business environment and deal with senior management and with staff at all levels and areas. |
| Differences from business | The lines of authority and jurisdiction. In part, the objectives of the investigation. The type of relationships and what coordination demands. |
| Skill required | Using tools such as scatter graphs to find weaknesses in manual or computer systems. |
| Conclusion | The transition is sometimes difficult and depends on the candidate’s personality. |

#### 1.8.3 Investigator Qualifications (Webster University 2020)

Professional qualifications and personal traits are valued.

**Professional qualifications (6)**

| Qualification | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Education | Many positions require at least a bachelor’s degree. Even if not in the field, it indicates intelligence, maturity, discipline, and broad knowledge. It helps in understanding culture and society, which gives perspective on the real facts. It shows the capacity to keep learning. Specialists need specific training, often graduate degrees (e.g., forensic science, behavioral psychology). |
| Training | There are many courses on general techniques and on specific aspects. They are given by police agencies, universities, and commercial providers. The level, currency, and source (agency or school) of the training are weighed. |
| Certification | Examples: Professional Certified Investigator (PCI), Certified Protection Professional (CPP), Certified Fraud Examiner (CFE), Certified Forensic Interviewer (CFI). They prove knowledge, commitment to the field, and effort to stay current. They should weigh heavily in hiring and promotion. |
| Experience, general | Rule of thumb: two years doing investigations, preferably of several types. It must include interviews and interrogations, evidence handling, liaison, surveillance, records searches, photography, reports, and case presentation. Patrolling as a police officer does not count as investigating, unless the person did specific, detailed investigative work. |
| Experience, specialized | Experience in the sector, in the business environment, or in a specialty adds value, sometimes a great deal. It brings lessons learned and best practices that improve the unit. Specialist positions require it; detailed information must be requested from the candidate. |
| Communications skills | Drawing out information (the core of every investigation) from people, cooperative or not, with different views and levels. Presenting orally and in writing to executives, lawyers, prosecutors, police, and security professionals. Being concise and convincing at once, balancing facts and conclusions. It is partly a personal trait, but is treated as a professional qualification. |

**Personal traits (10)**

| Trait | What it means |
| --- | --- |
| High ethical standards | A demonstrated record of honesty and professional ethics. It marks their whole relationship with the unit and with those they deal with on the organization’s behalf. |
| Persistence | Pressing on despite dead ends and frustrations. |
| Balance | Pursuing success firmly without skipping rules or protocols. That way they do not jeopardize the legal basis of the case or raise the company’s legal risk. |
| Maturity | A mature, realistic view of themselves and their surroundings. It allows putting in context the information, facts, and situations that affect lives, careers, and the organization. |
| Ability to deal effectively with people | Despite technology, people are the center of almost every investigation. They must deal with all kinds of people, in any role. |
| Self-motivating and self-starting | There is almost no direct supervision, apart from legal and regulatory. They are expected to work alone, on their own drive. |
| Ability to multitask | Every case has many elements and sources, and they usually carry several cases at once. |
| Professional demeanor | Maintaining it when dealing with people, gathering and analyzing information, and presenting facts and conclusions. Otherwise they lose effectiveness and damage the credibility of the unit and the organization. |
| Observational skills | Observing people, places, activities, and situations; the most important thing is curiosity. It feeds the information base and guides next steps. Observing, interpreting, and relating well often makes good investigators. |
| Flexibility | Moving well in very different environments and situations. Knowing when to yield and when to insist. |

#### 1.8.4 Physical Resources

**Office space**

- Open or shared offices are a serious problem, especially next to other departments.
- If possible, the team should have private or semiprivate offices.
- Private spaces are also needed for meetings, interviews, and calls, and locked spaces for sensitive files and evidence.
- **Interview room**, aspects to consider:
  - size and comfort;
  - furniture;
  - layout;
  - possible recording equipment;
  - whether it has windows, which may or may not be desirable.
- Access with privacy for the person arriving for the interview is advisable.
- If there are frequent meetings with outside people, the room may be outside the controlled area (e.g., next to the outer lobby). That way visitors do not sign in at visitor control.
  - It is practical for outside informants, liaison contacts, outside vendors, and police.
  - Human resources can share it for exit interviews or meetings with terminated employees, without giving them access to the controlled area.
- **Case board:** complex cases are made easier with a manual storyboard (e.g., whiteboard) or software. It serves to track leads, show links (link analysis), and plan next steps.
  - That information must be protected from the view of anyone not on the team.
  - A “war room” may serve: a separate, secure, private space for teamwork.

**Evidence storage**

- Not all units need it, but temporary or long-term storage must be planned.
- Only a few people have keys, combinations, or codes.
- Most have individual locked containers.
- A separately zoned intrusion detection system is often advisable.
- Access control is key for practical reasons and for the chain of custody.
- Provision must be made for evidence with special storage needs:
  - very large items;
  - items that require climate control;
  - evidence that must be stored outdoors.

**Records and file storage**

- Many files are highly sensitive or confidential and have practical and legal access limits.
- Retention periods vary widely. They must be stored securely and in an orderly way, to keep or destroy them on time (retention and destruction or disposition).
- All documents and evidence are kept in their original format until all legal action, including appeals, ends.
- **Electronic or cloud storage:**
  - it takes up less space and usually makes retrieval easier;
  - secure backup copies must be off-site and instantly accessible;
  - they are useful if the data, the system, or the IU’s site fails (e.g., cyberattack, natural disaster, or other catastrophe);
  - for that reason secure cloud services are preferred.
- Even if everything is digitized, sometimes the originals must be kept. Anything that may be evidence (e.g., original photos and written statements) is not destroyed.

**Investigative supplies and equipment**

- It varies greatly. Some units only interview and report; others need to collect evidence, technical equipment, and consumables.
- Most need at least a basic evidence collection kit and audio and video recording equipment.
  - A voice-activated digital recorder and a digital camera are usually enough.
- **Beware of the smartphone:**
  - the device itself can become evidence and be out of use for a long time;
  - investigative material must be kept strictly separate from personal or organizational content.
- The unit must assess its needs realistically. Sometimes it can borrow equipment from the audiovisual department.
- Original audio, video, or IT evidence must not be left in doubt by copying, enhancing, or manipulating it.
- Staff must master the equipment before really using it.
  - Test it in various conditions: light levels, microphone position, background noise, indoors and outdoors.
  - Practice using it.
- It must be set in advance how audiovisual and electronic evidence, recordings, and removable media are labeled, logged, and stored.

**Physical investigative aids**

- A crime lab is not needed, but some simple aids are. Many corporate IUs have:
  - a latent fingerprint kit;
  - evidence bags and containers;
  - latex gloves;
  - materials to mark or identify evidence.
- Marking materials: substances that glow under ultraviolet light, barcode labels, quick response (QR) codes, etching pens. Also radio frequency identification (RFID) and global positioning system (GPS) tracking devices.
- Police suppliers sell kits that combine several aids at a reasonable cost.

**Other equipment**

- **Computing:** laptops, tablets, cell phones, and special peripherals.
  - Besides administrative work, they serve to manage digital records, do computer forensics, and store electronic evidence.
- **Interview recording:** turnkey digital systems on the market, for police and investigators. There are fixed ones, installed in a room, and portable ones.
- Separate equipment may be needed to process cases, handle evidence, and analyze, in addition to office equipment.
- **Covert surveillance:** photographic equipment, vehicles, and communication systems.
  - It is no longer expensive, and wireless technology makes it easier to install.
- **Drones:** increasingly common. They serve for surveillance, assessments, aerial photos for reports, and other support.
- Basic equipment must be ready to deploy quickly.
  - It must be maintained.
  - Whoever uses it needs full training and practice.
  - Before each deployment, the legal requirements for surveillance and recording are checked, up to date.
- **Vehicle:** preferably low-profile and without company logos.
- Secure communication equipment may be useful. Procedures for its use and for communications security must be set.

#### 1.8.5 Additional Resources (6)

| Resource | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Training | Requires much time and money. Includes informal in-house training, courses, and conferences. Specialists need more, but everyone must update periodically. Topics: investigative techniques, legal matters, and making use of available technology. |
| Relationships | Internal and external liaison contacts are a resource. They must be cultivated on purpose, especially when creating the function. |
| Communications | Effective channels within the organization and with third parties (e.g., police and other liaison contacts). Clear, realistic procedures for sharing information, reporting cases, requesting support, and assigning resources. Formal and informal channels, for routine matters and for crises. |
| Financial resources | Well-planned budgets, with the best possible projections. Sometimes it is wise to collaborate with other departments to take advantage of their budgets (e.g., facilities, security, safety, legal, risk management). |
| Information assets | Information is the lifeblood of the mission. They are created at the start and maintained always: recruiting, handling, and closing informants; liaison contacts; internal and external databases; access to key people in the organization; open source information; operational data. Also an employee hotline for complaints, reports, and tips. It is a cheap way to learn of misconduct and receive anonymous tips. |
| Geographic assets | Many organizations cover wide areas or the whole world. It is wise to have sources, liaison contacts, backup vendors, and other resources in each area. Agreeing on those relationships and having equipment, supplies, and perhaps staff ready greatly improves operations. |

### 1.9 Managing Investigations

- The book announces four key management areas, at the strategic and day-to-day level:
  1. investigative functions;
  2. investigative resources;
  3. unit management;
  4. case management.
- Exam note: within 1.9 only three areas are developed (1.9.1 to 1.9.3). Case management appears separately, as section 1.11.

#### 1.9.1 Investigative Functions

- Most IUs focus on one function or a group of functions.
- They range from simple to complex. For example, from documenting how the security guard responded to a workplace incident to complex procurement fraud.
- They are called types of investigation. The unit’s incident management system is usually organized by type of incident.

**Typical types of investigation in business (25)**

| # | Type |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Incident or accident |
| 2 | Employee misconduct |
| 3 | Misuse or abuse of computers or IT systems |
| 4 | Substance abuse |
| 5 | Due diligence |
| 6 | Regulatory compliance violation |
| 7 | Lifestyle or financial inquiries of executives and staff |
| 8 | Personnel security or background |
| 9 | Theft, pilferage, or misappropriation |
| 10 | Lapping: crediting one account with money taken from another account |
| 11 | Assaults and crimes against persons |
| 12 | Property damage and vandalism |
| 13 | Inventory discrepancies or unexplained shrinkage |
| 14 | Sabotage |
| 15 | Industrial espionage |
| 16 | Copyright and proprietary information violations |
| 17 | Embezzlement or defalcation: misappropriation of property by the person to whom it was entrusted |
| 18 | Fraud: general, procurement, insurance, travel, accounting (open list in the book) |
| 19 | Product tampering, real or hoax |
| 20 | Diverted or counterfeit product |
| 21 | Skimming: keeping part of the cash |
| 22 | Communicating threats |
| 23 | Harassment, including sexual harassment |
| 24 | Workplace violence, real or potential |
| 25 | Litigation support. Varies depending on whether the company is plaintiff or defendant. |

- **Other uses by sector**, where the IU supports the core mission:
  - a real estate company uses it to locate unknown owners or do difficult property title searches;
  - others use it for market research, competitive intelligence, and other corporate functions.
- In many organizations, the IU is a corporate resource serving business objectives.
- IU managers (and security directors) must understand how the unit fits and how management sees it (the big picture).
- Ideally the IU manager or security director defines that fit. Their role may be direct or subtle, but they must influence as much as possible without losing sight of the business.
- Section 1.13 expands on several important types:
  - incident investigations, the most common type in many organizations;
  - compliance investigations;
  - background investigations;
  - substance abuse investigations.
- **Due diligence** (see chapter 3): a very important type, but often neglected.
  - Before a new business relationship, the prudent organization verifies what the future partner says.
  - It is usually requested as negotiations close and must be finished on time.
  - The skilled investigator quickly confirms the subject’s assets, liabilities, legal problems, possible conflicts of interest, and hidden affiliations or identities.

#### 1.9.2 Investigative Resources

- **Main resources of an IU (5):**
  1. people;
  2. information;
  3. credibility;
  4. physical assets;
  5. financial assets.
- The main functional focus dictates the structure and the distribution of resources. It defines:
  - what kind of investigators are hired;
  - what mechanisms are created to obtain information;
  - how and where the unit is structured;
  - what equipment and supplies it needs;
  - to a large extent, what the IU requests in budget.
- Funding is hard to project: one or a few large cases and new regulations or laws alter it. Justifying the budget is an arduous task.
- The best route is to demonstrate value, or that the unit pays for itself.

**Supports for justifying the budget (5):**

1. A correct focus, which supports the mission and the strategic and business goals.
2. An accurate and detailed record of investigative costs.
3. Effective cost control and efficiency measures.
4. Demonstrating the benefits of restitution and recovery.
5. Estimating in money the risk avoided (quantitative estimation of risk avoidance).

- Controlling operating and overhead costs well greatly improves the response to funding requests.
- Costs can be tracked by type of case, location, business area, or other variables.
- Recoveries and restitutions are recorded and reported to senior management. That way they show the financial benefit and support the ROI argument.
- Often the IU demonstrates ROI through civil recovery. It recovers the losses and also the costs of the investigation.
- Personnel costs and outside services are usually the largest items. Overhead is usually the easiest to control.
- To save money, managers rely on liaison contacts and on other departments: IT, facilities, accounting and finance, legal, and risk management.

#### 1.9.3 Unit Management

- The IU manager must balance administrative and operational duties.
- Figure 1.3 shows the investigative life cycle and many of the functions under the manager’s charge.

**The four phases of the cycle, according to the text**

| Phase | Content |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Initiation | Ways to initiate a case: incident report, complaint, discrepancy report, or investigation request. Includes the reason for the case and the mechanism by which it is opened. |
| 2. Investigation | Examples of case types on two tracks: incident, allegation, or situation; and level of trust. |
| 3. Reporting | Findings are reported and their later use is anticipated. Almost all the information is formally retained, often in an automated database, in field, summary, or full format. |
| 4. Use of results | Use in the specific case: disciplinary action, referral to the police or the judicial system, or adjudication. Aggregate or later use: statistical analysis, program evaluation, strategic planning, and budget forecasting. |

**Figure 1.3 — The Investigative Life Cycle**

| Phase | Incident, allegation, or situation track | Level of trust track |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Initiation | Triggers: Incident Report, Complaint, Audit or Discrepancy. Origin: Incident, Allegation, Situation (known or unknown subject). | Need for Level of Trust; Request for Investigation |
| Investigation | Criminal Investigation; Administrative Inquiry; Misconduct Investigation; Incident Investigation; Compliance Investigation | Background; Due Diligence; Financial/Lifestyle |
| Reporting and recording | Report of Investigation; Retention and/or Database | Retention and/or Database |
| Use in the case | Action/Case Disposition | Adjudication/Determination |
| Later uses | Trend Analysis; Statistical Reports; Program Management Evaluation; Strategic Planning; Resource Allocation; Criminal Intel. Analysis | Access Authorization; Visit Requests; Concurrent Clearances; Corporate Decisions; Reinvestigation Alert |

- Pending: the figure’s text was damaged and was read by position. The arrows and the exact track of “Report of Investigation,” “Action/Case Disposition,” and “Adjudication/Determination” remain to be verified against the original figure.
- The IU manager designs and applies systems to manage each of the four phases.
- It is easy to sink into the day-to-day, but the management and administration of the unit must not be neglected.

**Initiation**

- A convenient mechanism is needed for employees and others to provide information worth investigating or that supports an open case.
- An important part of credible information comes from these sources:
  - whistleblowers;
  - employee tips, anonymous or not;
  - complaints that arrive through supervisors.
- That information covers incidents and conditions that cause losses, legal liability, and other problems, including possible violence.
- Dismissing anonymous complaints goes against good practice. Even so, false reports, retaliation, and misinformation must be considered.
- **Legal framework for whistleblower protection:**
  - Laws protect against retaliation, but vary from country to country.
  - The United Nations Convention against Corruption (2003) made that protection part of international law (National Whistleblower Center 2020).
  - In the United States, two rules require an internal reporting system without retaliation, as part of an effective compliance and ethics program:

| Rule | Whom it binds |
| --- | --- |
| Sarbanes-Oxley Act of 2002 | Publicly traded companies |
| Federal Sentencing Guidelines | Companies convicted of criminal activity |

- Many organizations have channels to report fraud, waste, abuse, threats, security violations, and other adverse situations.
- It is also important to have an awareness program.
- Companies must have a process to evaluate each communication and investigate those that call for follow-up.
- They must do everything possible to prevent retaliation against the whistleblower. That is why it is advisable for all reports to be anonymous.
- In some organizations, the channel is shared with the areas that record safety risks, IT threats, or discrimination complaints.

**Investigation**

- The manager must implement a case management system and administrative controls.
- **Automated systems:** nearly all organizations use them, and many integrate with the security incident management system.
  - Well configured and with proper permissions, they integrate security, investigation, human resources, and facilities data.
  - They make it easier to collect and retain data, write and share reports, and coordinate with legal and finance.
- **Case assignment and workload management:**
  - Programs help; in small units it can be done by hand.
  - In large or dispersed units, assignment is based on caseload, experience, specialization, geography, or a mix of factors.
  - It must be done carefully and deliberately.
  - Poor assignment demotivates investigators and lowers the IU’s effectiveness. Some are overloaded and others have little work.
  - It can also happen that a specialist handles general cases while their cases go to others.
- **Supervision and controls:**
  - Keeping the investigative assignment ledger is the task of the controller or the manager.
  - The operational and administrative aspects of each case (e.g., costs) are reviewed at least once a month.

**Minimum checks and balances they must ensure (11):**

1. That investigation requests are documented and answered.
2. That there is proper authorization to open a case.
3. That each investigator carries an appropriate number of cases, and that the right investigator carries the right case.
4. That the status of each case is reported regularly, inside and outside as appropriate.
5. That leads are followed and recorded as appropriate.
6. That asset values (estimated loss, recoveries) are recorded and updated.
7. That liaison contacts are recorded and managed.
8. That periodic written reports are provided as planned.
9. That the case’s disposition and objectives are updated regularly.
10. That the case’s resources are recorded and managed.
11. That post-case information (disposition, files) is recorded.

- **The controls must allow three things:**
  - that all investigations are authorized;
  - that all the work assigned to each investigator is known;
  - that the results and pending tasks of each case are quickly known.
- At the start of a new IU, formal controls may not seem necessary. As the unit, its activity, or staff turnover grows, a formal system is needed. That way the maximum benefit is drawn from the accumulated data.
- The system should try to answer two questions:
  - how much a competent investigator should accomplish in a workday;
  - how long an investigation should take.
- **What affects the investigator’s output:**
  - their temperament, competence, and work pace;
  - the complexity of the case;
  - the availability of informants and other sources.
- Often it cannot be predicted what will be achieved in a day. That is why the skilled investigator opens at once all the leads they can handle. That way they generate information to use when the opportunity arises, in the least time.
- **Measure of effectiveness: the time it takes to close a case.**
  - It varies enormously, but with statistics averages are drawn by type of lead or case.
  - The average is used to schedule, budget, and assign cases.
  - It does not work in all environments, but it helps in some, both in case management and in strategic planning.

**Reporting**

- The formats, frequency, distribution, and other reporting requirements must be in the policies and procedures, and carefully followed.
- Formal documentation is usually the first thing neglected when caseload rises and time gets tight.
- Poor documentation and careless records can lead to failure. It is tedious, but it is an important management responsibility.

**Follow-up**

- Management and IU managers must understand that this phase goes beyond deciding disciplinary, administrative, or judicial measures.
- Sometimes the findings point, besides individual guilt, to system failures or business practices that caused the problem. Among them, at times, security vulnerabilities. Those failures call for corrective measures.
- **Synergy:** using the results to resolve the case and also to correct the conditions that allowed the incident.
- The results are kept in files or databases, according to the records retention policy. They serve for:
  - statistics and trend analysis;
  - evaluating the effectiveness of investigative, security, or assets protection programs;
  - supporting resource allocation decisions;
  - refining policies and procedures;
  - feeding criminal intelligence analysis, in general or in future cases. For example, with the same people, organizations, departments, places, or modus operandi.

### 1.10 Other Management Matters

Three key topics remain for running the investigative unit (IU): setting objectives, motivating staff, and training them.

#### 1.10.1 Setting Objectives

- It is a responsibility that is often neglected. It serves to operate well and to demonstrate value to the organization.
- Objectives must be **specific and measurable**.
- They are set for different aspects of the unit: financial management, case resolution, overall unit performance, information flow, and inquiries/complaints/leads handled. The book leaves the list open.
- They are measured, documented, and updated periodically: **at least once a year**.

#### 1.10.2 Motivation

Situations that affect the investigator’s self-image and attitude (9):

| # | Situation |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Testifying at trial or in administrative proceedings: intense questioning, reprimands, and challenges from lawyers or other officials. |
| 2 | Investing a lot of time in cases that later turn out to be based on false allegations, reports, or data. A sense of wasted effort. |
| 3 | Unsolved cases, cases closed before being resolved, or with a resolution the investigator considers inappropriate. |
| 4 | Very long cases, sometimes years (e.g., fraud and white-collar crime): a sense of not making progress. |
| 5 | Ethical temptations: dealing with dishonest people who prosper through their conduct, bribes, use of inside information for personal gain, abuse of authority, improper or illegal techniques (open list in the book). |
| 6 | Uncooperative or frustrating witnesses, interviewees, and liaison contacts. |
| 7 | Senior management’s decision not to pursue a matter (before or after investigating), when the investigator sees potential. |
| 8 | Reluctance of the police or the prosecutor to accept a case after much effort. |
| 9 | Cynicism: carrying work pressures over into personal life. |

- The IU manager must detect signs of frustration, without acting as an “armchair psychologist.”

Measures to motivate and maintain peak performance (7):

1. Require periodic breaks or vacations.
2. Send to training courses (this also gives time away from cases).
3. Organize social events in the office.
4. Form teams of investigators so they motivate each other.
5. Recognize personal and professional achievements.
6. Support participation in professional and community organizations.
7. Use a participative management style.

#### 1.10.3 Training

- It benefits the unit and the investigator. It takes time and money, but is essential in a professional unit.
- **Internal and periodic:** review policies, procedures, protocols, and topics that affect the organization.
- **External:** courses and seminars on specialized topics and on advanced general techniques.
- Specialized topics must be tied to the position or the industry. The book cites 16:

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Computer investigations and forensics | Surveillance technology | Evidence handling | White-collar crime |
| Forensic auditing | Criminal intelligence analysis | Environmental investigations | Safety investigations |
| Accident investigation | Sexual harassment | Workplace violence | Insurance fraud |
| Interviewing and interrogation | General management | Communication | Self-improvement |

### 1.11 Case Management

- Besides the unit’s objectives, each case needs its own.
- Even if the objective seems obvious, it is wise to identify and document three things:
  - the **basis or foundation of the case** (case predication);
  - a **primary objective**;
  - normally, **secondary objectives**.
- This keeps the investigation focused and guides the steps.
- **Detailed investigative plan:** required in complex or nonroutine investigations and in **all** proactive ones. It is coordinated with all relevant parties. It is approved by the IU manager or a higher authority.
- Objectives may change during the case. Every change is documented.

#### 1.11.1 Case Files

It is opened at the start of each investigation, with the IU manager’s approval.

Initial contents of the file (6):

1. Original complaint form or other initiating mechanism.
2. Assigned case or file number.
3. Name of the assigned investigators.
4. Complaint or subject information.
5. Initial leads.
6. Record of management’s approval.

Steps that opening sets in motion (5):

1. Assign a case or file number.
2. Record the case in the case management system and in other databases as appropriate.
3. Notify management and others, as appropriate.
4. Coordinate with staff, as appropriate.
5. Try to corroborate the initial information.

Administrative data of the file, tracked by hand or with an automated system (11):

| # | Data |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Type or category of case |
| 2 | Case title or subject (if known) |
| 3 | File number |
| 4 | Location of the incident that originated it, if any |
| 5 | Value of assets lost or stolen |
| 6 | Assigned investigators |
| 7 | Opening date (and place, if applicable) |
| 8 | Deadline for the report or other action |
| 9 | Dates of interim reports |
| 10 | Date of the final or closing report |
| 11 | Value of assets recovered |

#### 1.11.2 Following the Progress of a Case

- **Case chronology form,** automated or by hand. It records activity that does not warrant an interim report. Examples:
  - setting appointments to interview witnesses;
  - setting appointments to review documentary evidence with records custodians;
  - organizing the information to define pending actions.
- The chronology plus the interim reports serve two purposes:
  - they demonstrate the investigator’s diligence;
  - they allow the manager to evaluate the efficiency and effectiveness of staff.
- **Automated system:** generates reports of open cases by deadline, by assigned investigator, or by other factors. The manager reviews the status of any case at any time.
- Interim reports are reviewed in electronic or paper format. Periodic oral meetings on case status are also recommended.
- **Large IUs:** may have an operations coordinator who reviews cases, guides, and recommends steps.
- It is wise to use supervisors and colleagues as sounding boards or for brainstorming. Investigation is partly creative and the best ideas usually come from collaborating.

File custody. Even a small IU or security department needs a formal system.

| Format | Controls |
| --- | --- |
| Paper | Secure container (safe or high-quality locking file cabinet). One or more people designated as responsible. Checkout card at the place of the removed file: who took it out and when. That way it is located when the manager or another team member asks for it. |
| Electronic (ongoing and closed) | Access permissions and audit trail. They are set by the systems administrator or another official designated by the IU manager. |

Exam note: the book says that “no one” may add or remove a file for any reason. Right after, it requires a card recording who removed it. It is an inconsistency in the text itself.

Tasks of the lead investigator during the case (7):

1. Record the liaison contacts and the information they provide.
2. Keep the surveillance logs, physical and technical.
3. Keep the evidence logs, with receipts and chain of custody documents.
4. File original written statements and documentary evidence.
5. When applicable, record costs per case. Sometimes they are charged to another corporate or outside account.
6. Keep the suspense log: report deadlines, status reviews, leads, and other milestones. Ensure delegated tasks are completed on time.
7. Report to regulatory agencies when mandatory.

These steps bring three benefits: legal credibility of the case, greater likelihood of success, and more efficiency.

#### 1.11.3 Case Reporting and Information

- Results are normally communicated in a **formal report of investigation**.
- There may be a final report, a series of interim reports, and periodic and final summaries or synopses.
- **Interim report rule:** each one includes all the information from the previous interim. The reader does not have to go back to the earlier ones: the latest report contains everything known to date.
- Two things must be balanced:
  - sharing what is relevant with those who need it to support the investigation or decide;
  - protecting the information (integrity and privacy of those involved).
- Most IUs have dissemination policies, but gray areas arise. Dissemination of sensitive information is coordinated with the IU manager or the appropriate authority.
- **Case initiated by an attorney** with attorney work product privilege: nothing is disclosed unless the attorney orders it.
- Every disseminated report, on paper or electronic, indicates:
  - its sensitivity level;
  - the dissemination controls;
  - the disposition and retention instructions.
- Recipients are warned, when applicable, that they may not forward or share the information.
- **Distribution log:** to whom it was sent and when, or who accessed it electronically and when. Also file downloads or modifications.

### 1.12 Other Investigative Management Issues

#### 1.12.1 Legal Issues

- The book is based on the United States, but says that similar issues apply in most jurisdictions.
- Legal matters are considered for two reasons:
  1. to protect the company against legal liability;
  2. to ensure an appropriate conclusion if the case goes to trial or another legal forum.

Other legal considerations (4):

1. Comply with the laws on private investigator licensing and on the conduct of private investigations.
2. Meet regulatory reporting requirements in all applicable jurisdictions.
3. Possible civil lawsuits (sometimes criminal complaints) for defamation, false imprisonment, false arrest, retaliation, and harassment.
4. Possible subpoenas to testify, to hand over the written records of the investigation, or both.
