- The IU head must ensure that the organization knows the applicable laws, regulations, and corporate policies. The task grows if there are locations in several jurisdictions or countries.
- **Venue:** may not be the expected one. It may be:
  - where the corporate headquarters is;
  - where the incident occurred (perhaps off company property);
  - where the complainant is;
  - another place tied to the incident.
- Laws, regulations, and restrictions may vary among those jurisdictions.

**Local Licensing Requirements**

- Most jurisdictions require paid investigators to be licensed. Requirements vary greatly among states and countries.
- They are checked before hiring investigators and before sending them to other jurisdictions.
- **Outsiders** (outsourced investigators, consultants, or experts): the license of the individual **and** of the firm that employs them is verified. It cannot be assumed.
- Violations can cause significant legal liability, fines, and the dismissal of a case that was good.
- With operations in several jurisdictions, the law of each one where an investigation is conducted is reviewed.

#### 1.12.2 Liaison

- Professional relationships are invaluable for an effective IU. Establishing them, nurturing them, and using them well is a key management responsibility.
- Types of contacts: internal and external, formal and informal, individual and organizational.
- More detail in Chapter 8.

**Figure 1.4. Suggested Liaison Contacts**

| Internal — 15 | External — 15 |
| --- | --- |
| Executive management | Local law enforcement |
| Facilities | Other first responders |
| Human resources | Local FBI, including InfraGard representatives\* |
| Information technology | Other federal or regional investigative agencies |
| Legal counsel | Military |
| Operations | Expert consultants or firms |
| Public relations | Crime laboratory services |
| Purchasing | Outside employee assistance program providers |
| Safety/occupational health | Equipment or supply vendors |
| Security, if separate dept. | Local Chamber of Commerce |
| Labor relations | Other business associations |
| Employee councils | Professional associations |
| Department or division contacts within the company | Company suppliers and vendors |
| Accounting and finance | Local community contacts |
| Other informants | Other informants |

\* Figure note: most FBI (Federal Bureau of Investigation) field offices have representatives for corporate relations, computer crime, or economic espionage.

Exam note: “Other informants” appears in both columns. In the external column there are two nearly identical entries: “Equipment or supply vendors” and “Company suppliers and vendors.” The assignment to each column was taken from the position of the text on the page.

#### 1.12.3 Criminal Intelligence Analysis

- It is advisable, or essential, in organizations with frequent, large, or complex investigations.
- For INTERPOL, information is the lifeblood of police work. Timely and accurate analysis is key to understanding how crime and criminal enterprises work and what drives them.

**Two types of analysis according to INTERPOL (2020):**

|  | Operational / tactical | Strategic |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scope | Supports a specific investigation | Addresses a general criminal threat or trend |
| Aim | A specific police or investigative result: arrest, seizure, forfeiture of assets or proceeds of crime, or dismantling of a criminal group | Inform high-level decision makers |
| Typical support | 1) Identify links between suspects and their involvement in crimes. 2) Identify key investigative or information gaps. 3) Develop profiles of known or suspected criminals. | Early warning of threats. Helps set priorities in the face of emerging criminal problems. Examples: allocate resources to types of crime; strengthen training in a technique. |
| Benefit | Immediate, in the case | Long-term |

**Application in the private sector:**

- It is just as important for corporate IUs.
- It supports the security risk management process. Its usual use is threat analysis, but it also reveals vulnerabilities and impacts of crime or other loss events.
- Example: comparing the impact of theft or loss of intellectual property among several research projects or business units.
- It can be strategic or tactical (operational).
- The strategic type informs resource allocation, focus or priorities by risk category, and long-term planning. It also directly supports investigative operations.
- An important aspect of the strategic type is a formal process very similar to the National Security Intelligence Cycle.

**Figure 1.5. The Intelligence Collection Cycle**

- It can be used by police (at all levels), investigative organizations, and corporate IUs with large, complex, or interconnected investigations.
- Pending: the figure is a graphic with no extractable text. The five steps below come from the text.
- The book’s example for the whole cycle: a company concerned about an organized retail crime (ORC) network that exploits its warehouses.

Exam note: the same cycle gets three names. It is “very similar” to the National Security Intelligence Cycle, the figure calls it the “Intelligence Collection Cycle,” and the text calls it the “criminal intelligence cycle.”

| Step | What it is | Key points | ORC example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Requirements | What information must be gathered or what questions must be answered. Those questions are called interrogatories. | They are set by the whole organization or by a business unit, region, or project. The more specific they are, the more useful the result, but they may cost more or take longer. | Tactics against warehouses, changes in modus operandi, data on specific groups, where they operate, which groups act together and how they communicate. |
| 2. Collection | Gathering data from various sources and types of source to cover the requirements after analysis. | Witting and unwitting participants. Possible collectors: competitive intelligence function, online, in-person, print, and other open source intelligence (OSINT), direct observation or surveillance, internal market research, specialized outside firms. Information is inside and outside the organization: do not neglect either. Best is a variety of sources: different perspectives, origins, sectors, and eras (old and recent). | Police or task forces, media, crime and threat databases, supply chain security experts, already victimized companies or facilities, past or current members of ORC groups. Warehouse risk assessments. Physical surveillance at or near warehouses. A mix of internal collectors, a contracted intelligence collection firm, and a private investigation firm. Define the geographic scope from the start. |
| 3. Integration/Analysis | The heart of the process: turns information and data into actionable intelligence. | Information is received, cross-referenced, and sorted: assigned to requirements or to general-use files. The credibility of sources (people, organizations, publications, media) and the reliability of the information are assessed. Analysts and collectors communicate back and forth. | Everything is analyzed and shared with those who prepare intelligence products for “consumers” and stakeholders. It is checked whether the requirements were met and how to share what goes beyond them. |
| 4. Dissemination | Who receives what, when, in what format, and how it is delivered. | Sometimes the requirements set it; it is commonly left open. It can be simple or very complex. It is affected by whether there are third-party recipients. Classification: in the private sector, normally “confidential” or “proprietary” (or equivalent). Retention: how and how long, for both the initial data and the finished intelligence. Laws, regulations, and policies govern, and vary among jurisdictions and international borders (physical or logical). | Recipients: senior management (C-suite), asset owners, warehouse management, human resources, personnel security, physical security, operations, information technology (IT) and, more limitedly, trusted transport and supply chain providers. Nondisclosure agreements (NDAs) required. Possible coordination with police or security system providers. |
| 5. Evaluation | Periodic review to improve the process. It is easy to neglect. | Three questions: 1) Were the requirements met? It is evaluated when one is closed or on an interim basis. 2) Is the function meeting its goals and objectives, and efficiently? 3) How can the process be improved? | Does the IU know what it needs to stop the current threat? What else will be needed now or in the future? Is it ready to coordinate with police, legal counsel, or others? How can it improve in the short and long term? |

Integration and analysis activities (5):

1. Corroborate the information and compare it with other information of a similar nature.
2. Break down each input, study the meaning and relevance of each part, and recompose it into a coherent piece of data.
3. Combine fragments of information like a jigsaw puzzle.
4. Put the information in context and make sense of it according to the organization’s mission and intelligence needs.
5. Decide the best use of the intelligence and recommend how to disseminate and apply it.

Internal analysis teams and fusion centers:

- Many companies have internal intelligence analysis teams: from high-tech research and pharmaceuticals to cruise lines and theme parks.
- That cell can function as a **mini-intelligence fusion center**.
- **Fusion center:** normally a multijurisdictional police working group. It receives threat, organizational, and other information from many sources and agencies, and cross-references and analyzes it.
- It usually has a focus (terrorism, drug smuggling, counterfeiting, or human trafficking), but may combine several.
- In a company it may include business units, departments, corporate functions, internal stakeholders, and even key suppliers or partners.
- Advantages: broader sources, more perspectives for analysis, and dissemination, with protections, to a larger audience. A more useful result for the organization.

Sources that are also customers:

- The police and asset owners can be both “source” and “customer” or recipient of the finished intelligence. It is common and logical.
- Processing in the analysis phase avoids simply returning what the source gave and adds value for all parties.

**Tactical focus and link analysis**

- Tactical analysis is usually tied to an ongoing investigation or operation.
- It also serves to visualize complex threat trends and interdependencies relevant to the organization’s risk posture.
- **Most-used tool: link analysis.**
  - It is very old. It probably originated in the early detective agencies, such as Pinkerton.
  - It has been promoted by professional associations, especially the International Association of Law Enforcement Intelligence Analysts (IALEIA).
  - It used to be done with whiteboards and sticky notes. Today it uses specialized software and data analytics.
- **Definition:** it represents graphically and logically the connections among people, places, and activities. It seeks to reveal patterns or other information useful for investigating.
- **Uses:** solving crimes (the best known) and, proactively, threat analysis, predictive analysis, and study of criminal or adversary organizations.

Elements that can be data points in a link analysis (11):

| # | Element |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Personal connections (2nd-, 3rd-, or 4th-degree, for example) |
| 2 | Communications among individuals, groups, or organizations |
| 3 | Travel patterns (local, national, or international) |
| 4 | Events attended (social, professional, educational, organizational) |
| 5 | Internet and social media connections |
| 6 | Employment |
| 7 | Locations |
| 8 | Vehicles |
| 9 | Memberships (formal or informal groups, criminal or not) |
| 10 | Activities |
| 11 | Financial patterns or transactions |

- A complete analysis tracks these elements for a crime, threat, group, or situation. It also detects changes and trends over time.
- General objective: “connect the dots” and obtain actionable intelligence.
- Applications of strategic and tactical analysis:
  - executive protection (static, local movements, and travel);
  - crime prevention programs;
  - threat assessment;
  - fraud detection.
- Both can reveal systemic weaknesses that facilitate crime or aggravate risk. For example, in security or business practices, decision making, project management, or facility design.
- **Social media:** both types depend heavily on them, to detect and track threats and as a source of leads.
  - In the strategic type they are used mainly in collection. They should also be used in analysis, to give context or corroborate.
  - In the tactical type they are invaluable for identifying suspects, connections, and threat actors, and for uncovering illicit activity.
- Data mining, data analytics, and link analysis tools are also valuable.

#### 1.12.4 Protecting Investigative Information

- It receives less attention than it should, especially in the private sector.
- Once information is compiled, its content and the result are permanent.

Harms if information is compromised:

1. It affects the future effectiveness of investigators or the IU.
2. It exposes the organization to civil liability.
3. It damages the reputation and careers of people: subjects, witnesses, interviewees, managers, colleagues (open list in the book).
4. It damages important liaison relationships.
5. It fuels rumors that harm the investigation, the stock price, market share, and corporate reputation.

Legal framework:

- In many jurisdictions, electronic and documented information requires extraordinary protection.
- There are regulatory sanctions for failing to protect personal information obtained in investigations and surveillance.
- In most European countries, Australia, and Canada, strict laws protect the privacy of personal information.
- **European Union General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), implemented in 2018:**
  - restricts what personal information is collected, shared, and accessed;
  - affects security professionals and investigators, even in preemployment background investigations;
  - applies even if the company or the investigator is not in the European Union.

Cases where protecting information is especially important (5):

1. Subjects who are senior executives or high-profile people.
2. Joint task forces.
3. Large compliance or regulatory investigations.
4. Sensitive topics: terrorism, child pornography, workplace violence, major fraud, or product safety.
5. Personal information protected by privacy laws.

- **Joint task forces:** it is especially hard to protect information. Each agency has different policies, definitions, perspectives, and attitudes about what is sensitive and how to protect it.
- Protecting critical information is key in every investigation. Even in background investigations (BIs).
- A well-known principle of BIs: the investigator does not reveal one interviewee’s unfavorable information to subsequent interviewees.
- Besides formal protocols (such as government classification systems), there are two effective means: operational security and operations security.

|  | Operational security | Operations security (OPSEC) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Definition | The total set of security and planning measures for the success of a specific operation and the safety of investigators | A simple, systematic method of applying safeguards to protect critical information |
| Content | Cover stories; backstopping (actions that sustain fictitious covers or identities); communication protocols; agent infiltration plans; countersurveillance; covert meet security (measures to hide a secret meeting); carding (keeping data on information sources); technical monitoring | A five-step cyclical process (see below) |
| When it applies | In the public and private sectors, in special activities such as undercover or sting operations. It is expanded in Chapter 2. | In general, to protect critical information |

**The five cyclical steps of OPSEC:**

1. Identify the assets (the critical information).
2. Define the threat (collectors, capabilities, motivations).
3. Assess the vulnerabilities.
4. Analyze the risk (impact, priority, existing countermeasures; open list in the book).
5. Develop and implement countermeasures.

Step 1. Define the critical information:

- It is a key step but often forgotten.
- It consists of deciding realistically what information is sensitive at each phase and deserves protection.
- In joint task forces, a deliberate process is carried out at the start. All agencies must agree on what is critical information.

Step 2. Define the threat:

- Identify, within reason, all possible collectors or adversaries. They may use legal or illegal means.
- Likely adversaries (5):
  1. people or organizations with an interest in the outcome;
  2. friends or sympathizers of the subject;
  3. as-yet-unidentified accomplices (people or organizations);
  4. the media and plain public curiosity (especially in high-profile cases);
  5. possible copycats or others who commit similar offenses.
- Rule of thumb: never underestimate how far the adversary will go.
- Many criminals use the same surveillance and human collection techniques as the police and legitimate investigators:
  - they monitor communications: radio, landline, social media, and cell phones (open list in the book);
  - they collect information from courts and proceedings (public records);
  - they use social engineering;
  - they exploit public relations offices;
  - they use open sources, such as news and the Internet;
  - they go through the trash;
  - they attack computers and mobile devices, sometimes even Internet of Things (IoT) devices.

Step 3. Vulnerabilities:

| Direct | Indirect |
| --- | --- |
| Physical or electronic interception of information | Telltale signs, such as more activity in the security department right before an important operation |

- Both must be considered. The investigator asks whether they show predictable patterns or do something the adversary can use to frustrate the operation.

Step 4. Risk analysis:

- It is based on four factors: the nature of the critical information, credible threats, identified vulnerabilities, and the consequences of a compromise.
- It assigns levels or priorities to information risks. They are then addressed with countermeasures.

Step 5. Countermeasures:

- **Countermeasure:** anything that negates or reduces the adversary’s ability to exploit information or vulnerabilities.
- It can be something as simple as changing behaviors or routines.
- Other countermeasures (12):

| # | Countermeasure |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Restrict conversations where others can hear |
| 2 | Limit the information visible on social media (Twitter, Instagram, Facebook, LinkedIn) |
| 3 | Limit the use of cell phones, radio, and wireless communications |
| 4 | Encrypt whenever possible |
| 5 | Avoid conversations in public places, such as restaurants or airports |
| 6 | Carefully choose surveillance vehicles, techniques, and participants |
| 7 | Coordinate closely with the public relations offices of the participating agencies and companies |
| 8 | Apply the “need-to-know” principle in the IU, agency, or company |
| 9 | Protect written information and offices |
| 10 | Immediately destroy trash containing sensitive information |
| 11 | Apply good cybersecurity practices on systems with sensitive information |
| 12 | Include information protection clauses in security or investigation service contracts |

**Figure 1.6. Process Flow Chart for Protecting Investigative Information** (© 2020 Innovative Protection Solutions, LLC, used with permission)

Pending: the arrows are graphics. The path was reconstructed from the position of the YES/NO labels on the page; it should be confirmed against the figure.

| Step | Question or action (figure text) | If YES | If NO |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Is the information sensitive? | Step 2 | Monitor for changes in information value |
| 2 | Is the information public knowledge? | Step 3 | Step 4 |
| 3 | Protect it anyway? | Step 4 | Monitor for changes in information value |
| 4 | Are there adversaries? | Step 5 | Monitor for development of potential adversaries |
| 5 | Do they have motivation? | Step 6 | Continue to monitor to determine if motivation and/or capability develops |
| 6 | Do they have capability? | Step 7 | Continue to monitor to determine if motivation and/or capability develops |
| 7 | Identify capabilities to collect against target investigation/activity | Step 8 | — |
| 8 | Select/implement countermeasures based on specific collection capabilities | Step 9 | — |
| 9 | Determine effectiveness of countermeasures | Step 10 | — |
| 10 | Restart the process | — | — |

- Protective measures must not hinder the investigation, but reinforce it.
- All participants and anyone with access must apply cost-effective countermeasures.
- Benefits: they protect evidence, favor good outcomes (convictions or restitution), and protect personal safety.
- **Summary, at the start of every investigation or operation:**
  1. define the critical information;
  2. assess the adversary’s capability and motivation to collect it;
  3. determine their likely collection methods;
  4. apply countermeasures to prevent, neutralize, or mitigate that collection.

#### 1.12.5 Working in Multicultural or International Environments

Because of globalization, public and private investigators must know how to work in multicultural environments. Examples (6):

1. Investigation in a domestic company with subjects or witnesses of another ethnic origin or nationality.
2. Investigation that takes place in another country or follows leads there.
3. Investigation with partners, subsidiaries, joint ventures, vendors, or suppliers located or incorporated in another country.
4. Due diligence on companies or persons linked in some way to another country.
5. Investigation with international or multinational organizations.
6. Joint task force with agencies from more than one country.

Key considerations:

| Factor | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Laws | They vary among countries and among jurisdictions within the same country. Agreements and rules of regional entities may have an influence: European Union, free trade zones, and others. The investigator must know and respect local laws, regulations, and customs. |
| Liaison | It is approached with cultural factors in mind. Abroad, liaison with local police, security, and intelligence is common and often necessary. The relationship can range from very close and trusting to very fragile. Even so, it is usually wise to work with them, in general and in specific cases. A close relationship can bring enormous benefits and speed resolution. **Before** consulting foreign agencies, it is critical to discreetly research their reputation and practices. In multinational task forces, the team’s internal liaison is also essential: knowing each member’s tactics, legal limits, and culture improves effectiveness and reduces wasted and duplicated effort. Liaison as a force multiplier is expanded in Chapter 8. |
| Cultures and Approaches | Some techniques and strategies fit better in some cultures than others. Example: paying for information is an insult in some cultures and almost obligatory in others. Legal counsel must be consulted before offering payments. Interview techniques, the relevance of certain types of evidence, surveillance, and technical surveillance also depend on culture. |

Other differences among nations:

- They interpret crime and technology very differently. Not all countries see computer crimes as a serious threat.
- Rules of evidence and the technical capability of the police vary. That affects their ability and willingness to help in technology cases.
- They have different perspectives on drug crimes and their investigation.
- Political, diplomatic, or economic relations matter. If they are not cordial, their representatives may not cooperate against cross-border criminals.
- **Cultural differences are the hardest problem in international investigations.** The investigator must understand cultural diversity in general and the cultural and legal issues of the specific country or ethnic group.

#### 1.12.6 Ensuring an Appropriate Case Conclusion

- The general objective is a successful outcome or resolution of the case, whether the suspect turns out to be implicated or is exonerated.
- **Basic qualities of an investigation (5):** objectivity, thoroughness, relevance, accuracy, and timeliness.
- Management must also attend to three groups of issues:

| Type | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Legal | Licensing and legal liability |
| Technical | Appropriate investigative techniques and protection of information |
| Management | Effective coordination, documentation, and presentation of the case |

- Balancing these issues well helps resolve the case and protect the organization’s assets.
- On closing any case, the findings are evaluated to see other uses or benefits for the organization. Detail in Chapter 8.

### 1.13 Types of Investigations

- Most IUs focus on one or a few types of case, those that make up the bulk of their caseload. The list of typical types is in section 1.9.1.
- Some types deserve separate treatment. Background investigations are a distinct function because of their focus, resources, reporting, case resolution, and legal issues.
- In all cases, an effective and reliable investigation is timely and accurate.
- For each type, the book explains what distinguishes it, what special techniques apply, and what investigators and managers should know.

#### 1.13.1 Incident Investigations

- **Initiating event in the private sector:** normally, a security force incident report. Appendix 1A has two formats used in commercial and government facilities.
- Other initiating events: direct complaints, whistleblower reports, and security audits or assessments. That is why they are also called **complaint investigations**.
- **It is the most common type** in many companies and organizations.
- It is needed in almost every organization, although many have no formal IU. That is why it is usually done by a security supervisor or the security director, not a full-time professional investigator.
- A detailed, direct procedural guide is recommended: checklists, forms, and an incident investigation manual.
- That guide is adapted to the organization: to its needs, its culture, and the applicable legal factors.
- **Comparison of approach:**
  - Incident investigations, like BIs, call for an especially methodical and systematic approach.
  - Other types call for more creative problem solving or a flexible approach depending on the situation.
- With no IU or full-time investigator: simple procedures (protocols) to investigate and report.
- With an IU: guidelines that delimit responsibilities, perhaps within the investigative policy statement.

**Sample guideline from the book:**

| Investigated by the senior security supervisor, or done under their authority | Referred to the IU |
| --- | --- |
| Theft (estimated loss < $10,000) | Theft (estimated loss > $10,000) |
| Unknown loss (< $10,000) | Unknown loss (> $10,000) |
| Simple assault | Aggravated assault |
| Safety hazard | Property damage or vandalism (> $5,000) |
| Vehicle accident | Employee misconduct |
| Property damage or vandalism (< $5,000) | Executive misconduct |
| Unsecured area | Workplace violence (real or potential) |
|  | Sexual harassment |
|  | Sexual assault |
|  | Proprietary information loss |
|  | Fraud and economic crime |
|  | Contract irregularities |
|  | Unsecured area or forced entry |

Supervisor: 7 cases. IU: 13 cases.

Exam note: the guideline uses “< $10,000” and “> $10,000” (and the same with $5,000). It does not say who investigates a loss exactly equal to the threshold. Also, “unsecured area” appears in both columns; in the IU’s it is joined with “forced entry.”

- These thresholds can also serve to refer matters to the police or the legal area.
- Even if there is police interest, it can be very costly to prove that a specific crime occurred or to open a criminal investigation.
- It depends on the jurisdiction. Factors: local police priorities, capacity, workload, and prior experience.
- In many jurisdictions, police investigative units are overloaded. They do not investigate unless there is a clear threat to public order or a loss above a certain threshold.
- Each company must set its own loss thresholds. They serve to decide whether to investigate and with what parameters.
- **Criminal and civil:** the same facts that prove a crime can prove a civil tort. A for-profit company may be interested in civil remedies as much as in criminal convictions. In both cases a thorough and accurate investigation is needed.
- **Embezzlement example:**
  - cash disbursements are the most common form of workplace embezzlement;
  - that is why the accounts payable ledger is reviewed: dates and amounts paid to vendors.
- The results can be crucial in administrative or personnel proceedings.
- **Separation of duties:** the IU works with the departments so that an accounts payable employee cannot add or delete names in the master vendor file.

**Incident investigation process**

- The protocols are approved by senior management to give them credibility and authority, whether or not there is an IU or full-time investigator.
- The protocol includes a **decision logic matrix**. It serves to know quickly three things:
  1. who is responsible for the investigation;
  2. who is notified;
  3. whether an immediate referral to the police or the legal department is warranted.
- After that, the protocol describes the investigative steps.

Recommended contents of a protocol (sample from the book, 11 steps):

1. Assess the credibility of the source and of the information.
2. Determine whether there is physical evidence worth collecting. That is, is there a scene to process?
3. Conduct initial witness interviews:
   - priority to those directly involved or with perishable information;
   - priority to those who may not be available later: visitors, employees about to travel or be reassigned, informants passing through the area (open list in the book);
   - ask for recommendations on whom else to interview, with their identification or contact data;
   - document those who refused to give statements or were unavailable (witnesses, bystanders, others).
4. Document other relevant situational or environmental conditions.
5. List all security personnel, corporate personnel, and officials who took part in the response, assessment, and initial steps.
6. Conduct third-party interviews: people or agencies who did not witness the incident or were not present, but may know something.
7. Consult security and investigation indexes or databases.
8. Make and document all notifications of the incident.
9. Detect information gaps and identify sources or methods to fill them: interviews, evidence collection, surveillance, observation, documentary research, records checks (open list in the book).
10. Recommend follow-up or referral actions.
11. Complete the documentation and the database entry.

Training and complaint handling:

- Officers and security staff are trained in the protocols, including information protection.
- **Anonymous reports or complaint calls:** the informant must be made to feel that their information is important, appreciated, and will be investigated. Otherwise, valuable future reports are discouraged.
- At the same time, sensitive information is not given to the complainant or to others. The complaint may lead to an undercover operation or other activity that must be confidential.

Report:

- It is usually a standardized form.
- It must be filed or recorded properly in a database, so it can be retrieved later.
- It must indicate whether follow-up is required or recommended. Also whether referral to the IU, an outside investigative firm, or the police is advisable or mandatory.

#### 1.13.2 Misconduct Investigations

- It is an important subcategory of incident investigations.
- Exam note: the book writes “constructive incident investigations.” It is a possible typo.
- **Definition:** generally internal investigation. It is conducted when an employee or other person closely tied to the organization is suspected of having violated:
  - a written corporate policy;
  - a directive;
  - their conditions of employment;
  - or a federal, state, or local law.
- **Key feature:** they leave the employer highly exposed to lawsuits from employees or former employees who feel they were treated unfairly.
- Alleged causes: discrimination, wrongful termination, sexual harassment, defamation, and false arrest.
- **Best preparation:** coordinate as early as possible with the human resources director and corporate legal counsel (or equivalents). It applies to almost every private investigation, but especially to employee misconduct.

**Techniques and issues in misconduct cases**

- Both perspectives must be considered, that of the complainant and that of the subject:
  - for the employer it is a routine policy or disciplinary matter;
  - for the subject it can be traumatic: they feel their career, livelihood, and reputation are at risk.
- Experienced corporate investigators do not use techniques that could induce an innocent person to confess.
- Coercive interview techniques damage the reputation for impartiality. The IU and the investigator thereby lose credibility and trust.
- Some people are easily intimidated. Even if innocent, they may confess in the face of unfounded promises of leniency or invented, supposedly overwhelming evidence.
- Inappropriate techniques and failing to protect personal or confidential information can cause three harms: embarrassment for the company, erroneous conclusions, and lawsuits.

Suggested practices (4):

| # | Practice | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Set at the start the anticipated sanction or outcome if the accusation is proven | Avoids accusations of discrimination and the appearance of changing the sanction depending on what is found or who turns out to be implicated |
| 2 | Use internal **and** external sources to prove or rule out accusations | Using only internal sources limits the scope and may hide critical facts or data |
| 3 | Gather information from the subject’s former employers | Shows patterns of behavior, guides the investigation, strengthens the case, explains facts, and provides leads. Today’s high turnover makes it necessary to verify backgrounds thoroughly and to interview former coworkers, supervisors, colleagues, and other associates. |
| 4 | Collect electronic evidence even if it is not a system abuse case: emails, access requests, logons, file downloads, and remote access sessions | They are valuable sources |

**Side effects of the investigation**

- The possible side effects and the way to handle the case are considered.
- Surveillance: covert surveillance to gather intelligence is difficult and easy to detect if done on the move. Overt surveillance, whose aim is to prevent, can be effective.
- Exam note: this paragraph of the book mixes surveillance with side effects and ends up confusing. The effects it lists seem to refer to the investigation in general.
- Possible effects:
  - lower productivity;
  - strained relations between offices;
  - threats and intimidation;
  - pitting coworkers against each other and generating friction;
  - short- or long-term impact on the work climate and trust.
- This happens even if there is only a rumor of an internal investigation.
- Side effects cannot dictate the aggressiveness or the outcome of the investigation. But they are taken into account in four respects: overall strategy, dissemination of information, treatment of people, and professional conduct of those involved.

**Examples of misconduct cases**

- Frequent topics: conflict of interest, abuse of corporate resources, employee theft, workplace violence, and substance abuse.
- In each one it is critical that a specific corporate policy exist, and the attitude toward it. That determines detection, outcome, and legal sufficiency.
- Exam note: the book says “the existence of an attitude toward” a policy. Possible typo for “existence of, and attitude toward.”
- Features of a good policy (protect assets and the rights of the accused):
  - it is clear and well communicated: no employee or manager can credibly claim not to have known its meaning and consequences;
  - it encourages or requires reporting early warning signs from coworkers, vendors, or customers (suspicious, threatening, or inappropriate behavior);
  - it requires the employee to cooperate in any investigation, including misconduct investigations.
- The investigative process itself is also critical to deter, detect, and resolve misconduct.
- **Some investigations are prohibited by law and others are mandatory.** Example: in many jurisdictions harassment complaints must be investigated. The IU manager must know prohibitions and obligations at the international, federal, and local levels.

Before investigating, it is decided whether the conduct is legitimate grounds for discipline under local and national laws. Factors (3):

1. Whether it occurred on or off the employer’s property.
2. Whether it was during work hours or outside them.
3. Whether the offender presented themselves as a representative of the organization.

Once it is started, the company decides three things, in close coordination with legal counsel and other officials:

1. Whether there was really a fault or violation.
2. Whether it is mandatory to report it to the police.
3. How to communicate with the employee during the investigation and the resolution.

| Type of case | What the book says |
| --- | --- |
| **Conflict of Interest (CoI)** | Its definition varies according to the organization’s mission, structure, and culture. It is usually defined in the policy and in employee materials. Legally it is essential that all employees and affiliates (consultants, contractors, subcontractors, vendors, among others) know the definitions, policies, and consequences. If **the investigator** has a conflict of interest, they must disclose it to the client and not take part in any way in the assignment. |
| **Corporate Resource Abuse and Employee Theft** | It can involve a major loss. Possible abuses (5): 1) personal use of IT resources; 2) computer misuse: downloading pornography, accessing unauthorized information, spam, phishing (open list in the book); 3) personal use of company vehicles; 4) unauthorized or inappropriate use of company information; 5) inappropriate use of company aircraft. Main characteristics of internal theft (4): diversion, conversion, disguise, and divergence. The book does not define them. |
| **Workplace Violence** | It ranges from simple schoolyard-style bullying to violence with loss of life. Investigation is a central component. Peripheral topics: property damage, computer system abuse, sexual harassment, verbal or physical aggression. Warning signs usually come first, and are often recognized only afterward. **The best prevention is a preemployment background investigation program**, although it does not guarantee that someone will not become violent. It is investigated as soon as possible when signs are reported. The investigator works with threat assessment teams and shares information as far as possible. |
| **Substance Abuse** | See detail below. |

Exam note: in the conflict of interest row, “among others” reflects the book’s own “etc.”; the list of affiliates is open.

**Substance abuse**

- **NORC (2020)**, an acronym the book does not expand: employers spend almost **$9,000 a year** for each employee with an untreated substance use disorder.
- Those costs include absenteeism, lower productivity, accidents, theft, and higher medical expenses.
- It can also threaten public and workplace safety, create friction, and lead to drug dealing at work.
- **United Kingdom:** annual cost to businesses estimated at **100 billion euros** (UK Addiction Treatment Centers, 2020).
- Exam note: the text gives almost $9,000 of spending per untreated employee. The NORC 2020 reference speaks of an average saving of $8,500 for supporting each employee in recovery. Figure and concept do not match.
- Exam note: the United Kingdom figure is in euros. Possible typo, because the British currency is the pound.
- **Employee assistance program (EAP):** its use is encouraged. According to some sources, discipline for a drug or alcohol violation should **not** be postponed while the employee is in the program.
- **Canada:** substance abuse is considered a physical disability. The employer must comply with reasonable accommodation, for example EAP and recovery time.

**False allegations**

- In all types of case they are a serious nuisance. They consume resources, damage reputations, generate distrust, and lower investigators’ morale.

| Unintentional | Intentional |
| --- | --- |
| An observer misinterprets an action as a crime when there was no crime or violation | A disturbed complainant seeks attention, revenge, or to cover up their own fault by diverting attention to someone else |

- Every reasonable lead must be followed, whether or not the allegation is believed to be false.
- Security and investigation professionals must be alert to that possibility and ready to act.

#### 1.13.3 Compliance Investigations

- They share features with incident and misconduct investigations. In fact, they can fall into either of those two categories.
- Compliance weighs more and more on organizations and on those who run them.
- Having in-house capability to investigate compliance makes business sense. Sometimes the government requires it.
- The team must be trained in current regulatory issues and in investigative methods. It must know the business practices of its industry and in general well.
- Even small and medium-sized companies adopt formal compliance programs and in-house IUs to reduce their exposure to government investigations.
- In the US, the **Sarbanes-Oxley Act** and the **Federal Sentencing Guidelines for Organizational Crime** emphasize compliance programs and the visibility of executives.
- Exam note: the text says “Federal Sentencing Guidelines for Organizational Crime.” The chapter’s reference is “Federal Sentencing Guidelines, Chapter 8: Sentencing of Organizations” (U.S. Sentencing Commission 2018).
- **Purpose of a compliance program:** to protect the organization against violations of the law, failures of business ethics, and the legal penalties they entail.
- An effective program promotes three things: enforcement, monitoring, and employee training.
- It is built on a clear corporate policy, often expressed in a complete **code of conduct**.

Topics the code of conduct may cover (13):

| # | Topic |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Equal employment opportunity |
| 2 | Harassment |
| 3 | Workplace safety |
| 4 | Confidential and proprietary information |
| 5 | Copyrights, patents, and trademarks |
| 6 | Company assets and records |
| 7 | Computer systems, access to information, and authorized uses |
| 8 | Conflicts of interest |
| 9 | Government entities |
| 10 | Antitrust violations |
| 11 | Political activities and lobbying |
| 12 | Securities requirements of various jurisdictions |
| 13 | Regulatory violations |

- Noncompliance can damage the company’s results. That is why employees are trained to recognize a violation at once and know whom to report it to.

**Reporting mechanism and hotline**

- An effective reporting mechanism is a key element of every program.
- Risks of hotlines:
  - several lines confuse;
  - lack of reaction or follow-up discourages reports.
- Some companies have separate lines for: regulatory violations; fraud, waste, and abuse; security violations; safety hazards; and management issues.
- **Recommended:** one consolidated line, with well-trained professional operators and an effective system of distribution and follow-up. That way quality, valid, and useful reports are obtained.
- It can be run in-house or by an outside firm.
- **Advantage of the outside firm:** employees trust more that their voice will not be recognized.
- An effective line greatly reduces losses in most organizations.
- Its effectiveness improves with an ongoing awareness program for all employee groups and with visible backing from senior management.
- Hotlines and other mechanisms demonstrate commitment to compliance, because they uncover and deter misconduct.

**Scope by type of violation**

| Violation of internal policies or procedures | Violation of government law (e.g., insider trading) |
| --- | --- |
| It does not usually have the judicial scrutiny of a criminal investigation | Narrow scope because of legal restrictions |
| It can address broad business practice issues | It can involve serious criminal penalties |

- With credible information of a violation, a thorough investigation gathers and relates the facts.
- Companies must have in-house capability and be ready to cooperate with police or regulatory agency investigations.
- Having an effective program to detect and prevent violations **reduces the culpability** of the organization in some cases.

**U.S. Sentencing Commission**

- In **2004** it tightened the standards of the compliance and ethics program. It gave boards and executives more responsibility for its oversight and management.
- The guidelines give a reasonable time for the internal investigation. They assume the company’s cooperation will be timely and thorough.
- **Two factors that mitigate an organization’s penalty** (U.S. Sentencing Commission 2018):
  1. that an effective compliance and ethics program exists;
  2. self-reporting, cooperation, or acceptance of responsibility.

**Figure 1.7. Examples of Regulatory Agencies with Enforcement Authority** These are international and US (federal, state, and local) agencies with an enforcement interest, role, or mission in corporate compliance investigations.

| International (6) | National (15) | Local and Regional (12) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) | Nuclear agencies | Health departments |
| European Union Network for the Implementation and Enforcement of Environmental Law (IMPEL) | Communications agencies | Motor vehicle agencies |
| International Network for Environmental Compliance and Enforcement (INECE) | Aviation agencies | Courts |
| United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP) | Workplace safety agencies | Law enforcement entities |
| Secretariat for the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) | Environmental protection agencies | Construction authorities |
| International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO) | Controlled substances agencies | Zoning authorities |
|  | Labor relations agencies | Licensing authorities |
|  | Securities agencies | Food service authorities |
|  | Tax authorities | Alcohol control agencies |
|  | Customs | Animal control agencies |
|  | Postal authorities | Utilities agencies |
|  | Utilities authorities | Educational authorities |
|  | Biological protection agencies |  |
|  | Public health agencies |  |
|  | Transportation agencies |  |

Translations of Figure 1.7:

- **International:** International Atomic Energy Agency; European Union network for implementing environmental law; international network for environmental compliance; United Nations Environmental Programme; Secretariat for the Convention on Biological Diversity; International Civil Aviation Organization.
- **National:** nuclear; communications; aviation; workplace safety; environmental protection; controlled substances; labor relations; securities; tax authorities; customs; postal; utilities; biological protection; public health; transportation.
- **Local and regional:** health departments; motor vehicles; courts; law enforcement; construction; zoning; licensing; food service; alcohol control; animal control; utilities; education.

#### 1.13.4 Exploratory (Informational) Investigations

- **Definition:** they are opened to answer questions or explain a situation. They do not seek facts about a specific incident, violation, or possible crime.
- Sometimes they lead to another, more "accusatory" investigation. Most of the time they only seek to answer questions such as:
  - Why is this happening?
  - What caused or enabled it?
  - What exactly happened?

Common forms:

| Form | Content | Where it is expanded |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Background investigation of personnel or level of trust determination | Includes preemployment, in-employment, and continuous screening. Evaluates the integrity and qualifications of a person in a position of trust. Helps mitigate the insider threat. | Chapter 4 and the ASIS International Preemployment Background Screening guideline |
| Due diligence | — | Chapter 3 |
| Enigmatic conditions | Source of inventory discrepancies, adverse shrinkage trends, productivity or performance variations, accounting anomalies, and other enigmatic conditions | — |
| Reputational damage | Tracing reputational damage or disinformation on social media, online platforms, or through website or browser hijacking | — |

- The explanation may or may not be related to a crime or a security issue. The key is to answer the question.

**Situations that facilitate crime (example: human trafficking)**

- The exploratory category includes investigations that identify situations that facilitate or support criminal activity. They do not examine the criminal incident itself.
- Example: a company looks for conditions that may contribute to human trafficking.
- **Human trafficking:** exploitation of people for sexual purposes or forced labor.
- It is serious in specific sectors, especially hospitality and travel.
- Hotels, motels, resorts, and short-stay rentals are often used, sometimes with the knowledge or complicity of owners or managers.
- Investigating trafficking itself is usually a task for law enforcement (LE). Companies and other private organizations, including nonprofits, collaborate closely.
- According to open sources, the police often work in joint task forces. They rely heavily on networks with nongovernmental organizations (NGOs), schools, churches, health centers, civic groups, and businesses.
- These entities can help with:
  - victim support and care;
  - background information;
  - leads;
  - data searches;
  - surveillance;
  - awareness campaigns;
  - employee training;
  - sometimes, more direct investigative support.
- Companies can also investigate internally for two reasons:
  - to discover conditions that favor illicit or corrupt activities on their premises;
  - to mitigate the reputational risk of being associated with criminal enterprises such as trafficking.
- Companies or organizations are sometimes invited to join the joint task force and contribute their perspective and capabilities.

#### 1.13.5 Investigative Techniques in Exploratory (Informational) Inquiries

**Background investigations**

- They rely mainly on interviews and database searches: criminal record systems, local records, and verification of previous employment.
- **Main objective:** to compare what emerges in the investigation with what the subject provided, for example on the job application or in the background questionnaire.
- Inconsistencies may indicate an area of concern, a red flag, perfidy, or an attempt to conceal something.
- Privacy rules must be respected; they vary by jurisdiction and country.
- Sometimes honesty testing or polygraph is included.

**Other exploratory investigations: business knowledge required**

| Type of investigation | Knowledge needed |
| --- | --- |
| Discrepancies (inventory) | Inventory management and supply chain administration, including the organization's specific systems |
| Accounting anomalies | Financial crimes and common, lawful accounting procedures |
| Due diligence | Fiduciary relationships, contract law, mergers and acquisitions law and, where applicable, international agreements and standards |

- Conclusion: an effective investigator thoroughly understands the business (or the organization) and the investigative process.

**Common traits** of investigations of discrepancies or anomalies, due diligence, and situations that facilitate peripheral crimes:

- They require knowing the organization's software, applications, data storage, and network communication protocols.
- They require knowing the devices in use:
  - sensors;
  - barcode readers;
  - radio-frequency identification (RFID) readers;
  - point-of-sale (POS) terminals;
  - industrial control system components;
  - manufacturing or production controllers;
  - building controls;
  - access control systems.
- They are critical to the business, and their relevance to the investigation must be assessed.
- Examples of critical processes: inventory management programs, automated order fulfillment systems, and electronic financial transactions.

**Forensic accounting**

- It is usually associated with fraud and white-collar crime. It is also useful in exploratory investigations, especially of financial discrepancies, anomalies, or irregularities.
- Forensic accountants are trained in investigative techniques and automated tools.
- They reveal irregularities in complex procedures and record-keeping systems, especially in financial and compliance data.
- They also evaluate internal controls and recommend improvements.

**Root cause analysis** (see Chapter 8)

- It fits well in exploratory investigations, because they seek to know exactly why or how something happened.
- Example: a sudden increase in shrinkage may be due to a crime. It may also be due to:
  - careless paperwork by a new manager;
  - a change of computer program or a software update;
  - warehouse employee turnover;
  - a new supply chain vendor;
  - a combination of these.
- The answer is often not obvious, even after the initial investigation. A suitable root cause technique can resolve it.

### 1.14 Investigative Techniques and Tools

- The section guides managers, unit heads, and security directors on general methods.
- The techniques are subject to the policies and rules of the IU and its jurisdiction.
- Investigators must be trained in three things: basic investigation, relevant specialties, and legal limits.

#### 1.14.1 Interviewing

- An investigator's success is sometimes measured by their ability to interview or interrogate. It is covered in Chapter 5.

#### 1.14.2 Records and Database Checks

- They are a key element of almost any investigation. See Chapter 8, Online Information Sources.

#### 1.14.3 Evidence Collection and Handling

- Collecting, handling, processing, and using evidence is a key component of the investigation.
- Procedures for gathering, protecting, examining, storing, and presenting it are crucial.
- Some cases are resolved quickly with dismissal, restitution, or both. Others go through the judicial system and may take years to reach trial.
- Evidence tasks are tedious and resource-consuming, including a great deal of secure storage space.
- Physical, technical, and other types of evidence can be critical to resolving the case and to judicial or administrative decisions.
- It is expanded in Chapter 6.

#### 1.14.4 Surveillance

| Source | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Eugene Ferraro, CPP (2012) | Carefully observing people, places, or things. It does not have to be covert, although it usually is. It is probably the oldest form of investigation and perhaps the most used until the arrival of the Internet. |
| ASIS Investigations Standard (ASIS International 2015) | Direct and deliberate observation or monitoring of people, places, or things |

- **Main purpose:** to obtain information about the identity or activities of those under investigation.
- Taking notes during surveillance is essential.
- **Three broad categories:**

| Category | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Physical | Human observation or following |
| Psychological | Behavioral analysis |
| Data | Technical or electronic |

- It is widely used in public and private investigations.

#### 1.14.5 Computer Forensics

- **Definition:** the science of acquiring, preserving, retrieving, and presenting data that has been electronically processed and stored on computer media.

|  | Traditional forensic science | Computer forensics |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Where it is done | Normally in a laboratory | In forensic laboratories, data processing departments, and sometimes almost anywhere |
| What it produces | A report based on the objective results of a scientific examination | Extracts or produces information |
| Need for case details | Generally does not need them (a DNA analysis is done without knowing the name of the victim or the subject) | To be effective, it must be guided by what the investigation discovers |

- The examiner cannot review all the files. The capacity of a large disk or a central server makes it impossible in the time available.
- That is why the investigator must give the examiner the details of the case.
- The examiner can provide a prefilled list of keywords, phrases, or "terms" (in malware-type events). That makes the job much easier.
- To be effective, the examination must be a coordinated effort between investigator and examiner.

## Capítulo 2 — Undercover Investigations (págs. 83–112)

**Central idea:** In an undercover investigation, a trained investigator is placed in a workforce that does not know it. It is used to gather information and to understand the "how" and the "why" of what is happening. It is a last resort: it is expensive and slow, and it carries legal risk. It only works with a five-phase plan, an attorney from the start, strict confidentiality, and well-written reports.

### 2.1 What Is Undercover Investigation?

- **Definition (corporate undercover investigation):** it is the surreptitious placement of a well-trained and qualified investigator in an unsuspecting workforce. The investigator poses as an employee, vendor, or customer. Its main purpose is to gather information.
- The surrogate vendor or customer is usually built on a real, cooperating vendor or customer.
- By nature it is a covert operation. It is complex and does not work in every environment.
- **Main difference from other investigations:** the investigator does not hide their presence or try to go unnoticed. What is secret (sub-rosa) is their real identity or the purpose of the investigation.
- The undercover investigator is called an **operative**.
- Like interviewing, it is an **interactive investigation**. That is why the operative obtains data about the problem and also the "how" and the "why" of what the subjects of the investigation do.

### 2.2 The History of Undercover Investigations

| Date | Event |
| --- | --- |
| 1850 | Alan Pinkerton, a Scottish immigrant, founds one of the first private detective agencies in the United States in Chicago. He appears to be the first to have written about this practice. His operatives stand out as innovative and daring. |
| 1861 | At the start of the Civil War, Pinkerton is named head of the Secret Service of Union General George McClellan. |
| 1883 | He publishes *The Spy of the Rebellion*. It describes the first organized espionage network in the United States. |
| Apr 13, 1862 | Timothy Webster, his favorite undercover agent, is arrested and convicted as a spy. He is executed in Richmond (Virginia) by order of Confederate President Jefferson Davis. His career was short. |
| Civil War | According to Pinkerton, Mrs. Carrie Lawson was the first female undercover agent in the United States. She and her butler John Scobell, also a Pinkerton operative, gave key information to the North. |
| Postwar | Pinkerton is sent to Washington, D.C. to form the United States Secret Service. |
| 1865–1875 | Heyday of the Molly Maguires, a secret society of Irish Americans in the mining areas of central and eastern Pennsylvania. They rebelled against labor abuses in coal and the poverty of their towns. The mine owners controlled the police and the courts, so the Mollies murdered or intimidated police, government agents, and executives. The name is said to honor a woman who led a rebellion against landlords in Ireland in the 1840s. |
| 1875 | The Mollies create a union in a region without unions and call a strike. Franklin Gowen, president of the Reading Railroad (with large mining interests), hires Pinkerton. Pinkerton infiltrates his best agent, James McParlan. Result: ten Molly Maguires hanged. |
| 1947 | McParlan died rich, for unknown reasons. The descendants of the convicted suspected collusion and pressed Congress. McParlan's secret reports were not released until 1947. |
| 1920s–1930s | Detroit automakers use undercover agents against employee theft and alcohol at work. It became known years later, from the operatives themselves. |
| Until the 1980s | Pinkertons, Wackenhut, and Burns offered undercover, but only as a secondary service. It does not reappear as an important factor in written accounts until the 1980s. |
| 1981 | Industry recognizes, through press articles, that substance abuse has reached workers. A social problem becomes a workplace problem. |
| 1985 | Undercover becomes, and remains, a preferred method for investigating substance abuse and certain crimes at work. |

Exam note: the book writes "Alan Pinkerton" and "McParlan". Possible typo: the usual spellings are "Allan" and "McParland". It is also possibly a typo that Pinkerton founded the United States Secret Service. On the exam, answer according to the book.

### 2.3 When Should Undercover Be Used?

- It is the exception, not the rule. It is considered only when there are no other options.
- It seems simple, but it is complex and demanding. It can bring lawsuits and legal liability for the company and for the investigator.
- It demands time, patience, and resources from the company. It needs a highly structured process and a flawlessly executed plan.
- The operative does not just collect data. They may have to deal socially and professionally with the target group or person.
- Types of relationship with the target:
  - Close and continuous: daily work tasks.
  - Periodic or casual: coinciding at a recreational activity or on the same public transport.
- Risk: seeking information can lead to **entrapment** (inducing someone to commit a crime). It can also clash with the personal need for safety, immunity, and privacy.
- For all these reasons, undercover is not for everyone or for every organization.
- The form the investigation takes depends on the information sought.

**Typical situations suited to undercover (6):**

| # | Situation |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | There is constant, reliable information of employee misconduct, **conversion**, or criminal activity. But details are lacking to prevent it or identify those involved. |
| 2 | Losses are known to occur in a specific area, for example customer cash that is not recorded. It is not known how they occur, who is responsible, or whether there is **conspiracy**. |
| 3 | Management strongly suspects alcohol consumption, drug abuse, or drug dealing at work. Or it has already detected signs. |
| 4 | Alcohol or drugs affect performance and supervisors do not react or do not know how to intervene. |
| 5 | Actual practices must be compared with required policies and a routine audit is not possible. |
| 6 | Management believes that infiltrating operatives will yield important results and has already ruled out the other reasonable options. |

- **Conversion:** receiving money or goods from another and keeping or using them fraudulently for one's own benefit.
- **Conspiracy:** two or more people take part.

### 2.4 When Undercover Operations Are Not Appropriate

- Never to collect personal or confidential information.
- Never to intrude into the private life of someone who has a reasonable expectation of privacy.
- Doing so gives rise to lawsuits (actionable) against the company and its investigators.

**Do not use undercover in these 3 cases:**

1. To investigate activities permitted or protected by any government law, rule, or regulation.
2. To replace another investigation that would give the same results with less time and fewer resources.
3. To investigate protected union activities. U.S. federal law prohibits monitoring or investigating them. The same is true in many other jurisdictions.

**Unionized workforces:**

- Undercover is not ruled out just because there is a union.
- Operatives can be placed in a unionized workforce if there is no interference with the lawful and protected activities of the union or its members.
- The company must not use as operatives employees who are already unionized.

### 2.5 The Need for Undercover Investigation

- Done well, it protects assets and employees and helps reduce risks.
- It is chosen only if there are no alternatives and a significant return on investment (ROI) is expected.
- Initial question of every internal investigation: overt or covert?
- Put another way: full transparency from the start, or some covert measures to avoid problems and gather the facts effectively?
- If undercover is mishandled, it can be seen as an attempt to cover up irregularities.
- The purpose and objectives are defined in advance.

**Legal counsel:**

- It is recommended that counsel take part in planning and oversight.
- The attorney can be the one who hires the investigators.
- In general, attorney–client product and privilege protect confidentiality.
- The chief security officer (CSO) or the highest-ranking security executive must be on the selection team and on the investigative team.
- The attorney may be restrictive because of bar association rules on deceptive practices.
- Example: in 2011, the Professional Responsibility and Professional Ethics committees of the New York bar association studied the subject.
- They proposed amending the New York Rules of Professional Conduct (RPC). The amendment would expressly allow an attorney to advise and supervise undercover investigations that are otherwise lawful (New York City Bar 2019).

Exam note: the text dates the proposal to 2011, but the cited source is from 2019 (a proposed amendment to rule 8.4).

**Agency and investigator:**

- A qualified investigator from a reputable agency is chosen to find the facts and run the process.
- The CSO verifies that the agency is licensed and insured.
- The agency gives written assurance that neither it nor its investigator has a conflict of interest.
- A project management team communicates frequently. It is made up of representatives of the company, the attorney, and the agency.

**Law enforcement:**

- It gets involved only when needed.
- Sometimes the company is required to report, for example if it discovers a conspiracy or real evidence of a crime.
- After the investigation, communicating well with the workforce can be key.

**Why secrecy makes sense, at least at the start (3 reasons):**

1. Without more information, management and counsel do not know what to tell employees and customers.
2. Often the goal is precisely to find out whether there is anything to disclose.
3. If the company is only doing due diligence as a good steward, there may be no legal or business need to make it public.

- Every operation has its own problems. They must be anticipated and contingency plans prepared. With good planning and resources, many are avoided.

**Main problem: the operation being exposed or compromised.** Its becoming known during or after is usually undesirable:

| Who finds out | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Employees, for example through a management announcement before it ends | Morale and productivity drop. |
| Customers or vendors | They are upset at having been part of what was investigated. They may cut off the relationship. |
| The public, if the investigation is compromised | More resentment and bad publicity. |

- **Confidentiality:** only those with an absolute need to know take part or find out. Otherwise the investment and the people, including the investigator, are put at risk.
- **Follow-up:** close monitoring lets management and counsel "pull the plug", that is, end the operation.
- It requires time and patience. It rarely yields results at the start. The operative must integrate and earn the acceptance and trust of those being investigated.

#### Figure 2.1 — Considerations Before Approving an Undercover Investigation (Oberheiden, P.C., 2020)

| # | Question | What it implies |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Is a covert investigation viable? | Do many people know about the problem? Can they be trusted to keep it secret? Is there a legal obligation to report? If there is little chance of maintaining secrecy, it is not recommended. |
| 2 | How will confidentiality be maintained? | How is the operative introduced to the workforce? Do they go through normal hiring? Meticulous planning is needed, and everyone must understand the importance of confidentiality. |
| 3 | How long can the cover be maintained? | What triggered the investigation may become public through factors outside the company. The decision must take that into account. |
| 4 | What is needed to avoid accusations of a cover-up? | People inside and outside may be suspicious because of the secret nature. That creates labor, public image, and legal problems. The company must be able to justify its decisions during and after. |
| 5 | Who should be on the team? | No one who could be implicated in the allegations. There is usually outside counsel, senior in-house attorneys, corporate security executives, and executives of the outside agency. Every outside person is thoroughly vetted before learning of the matter. |
| 6 | Is there anything that must be notified or voluntarily disclosed? | Example: a consumer data breach may require notifying everyone affected. Some organizations have a federal obligation to self-report. Will that affect secrecy? |
| 7 | When does it become overt? | It must be decided when, if at all, employees, stakeholders, or the public are informed. If the suspicion turns out to be true, some corrective action will be needed. |

Exam note: the book describes four different compositions of the "team". In 2.5: company, attorney, and agency. In Figure 2.1: outside counsel, in-house attorneys, security executives, and agency executives. In 2.6.1 (investigative team, minimum): corporate decision maker, human resources, security, and labor attorney. In 2.6.2 (project management team): five roles. Do not confuse them.

Exam note: Figure 2.1 cites "Oberheiden, P.C., 2020". In the references that source has no year; it only says "accessed Aug 4, 2020".

### 2.6 The Five Phases of the Investigation

- No organization should fear undercover if the situation justifies it. It is complex and expensive, but it is managed well with a well-thought-out plan.

| Phase | English name | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Planning and Preparation | The team is formed, objectives are set, the agency and operative are chosen, and the other phases are planned. |
| 2 | Information Gathering | The operative works undercover. The project management team coordinates, communicates, reports, and files. |
| 3 | Verification and Analysis | After the operative is withdrawn, evidence, video, and findings are reviewed. Interviews are conducted and the information is organized by person. |
| 4 | Disciplinary and Corrective Action | The company's decision makers apply discipline. There may be a referral to law enforcement. Everything goes to the case file custodian. |
| 5 | Prevention and Education | The causes are corrected: hiring, supervision, and training. |

#### 2.6.1 Phase One: Planning and Preparation

- Planning is the key to an effective undercover investigation.

**Investigative Team and Objectives**

- Minimum members (4):
  1. A corporate decision maker.
  2. A human resources representative.
  3. A corporate security professional.
  4. An attorney who knows state and local labor law.
- The team decides the expected outcome. If misconduct is discovered, there may be discipline, prosecution, and restitution.
- Before choosing the path, 5 things are reviewed: past practices, industry precedents, the organization's policies, applicable labor contracts, and the criminal definition of the suspected conduct.
- The effect on labor relations, public opinion, culture, and reputation is also considered. And the possible ROI when there are no alternatives.
- These considerations may clash with each other. They must be analyzed and weighed before setting realistic objectives.
- Example: the risk of embezzlement is greater where the dominant value of the culture is economic.
- The objectives determine the type and structure of the investigation.
- Information unrelated to the purpose may turn up. Planning must provide a mechanism to handle it.
- There is information useful to the company that is not appropriate to investigate. Example: measuring the strength of a union campaign by identifying supporters. It could be considered an unfair labor practice.

**Possible objectives of an undercover investigation (5):**

1. Identify the true nature, scope, and perpetrators of the problem.
2. Gather information so management can apply its policies and take corrective action.
3. Collect the information in the way that least disrupts the organization and its operations.
4. Ensure the process delivers the best ROI.
5. Create better training tools for employees.

- These objectives are attainable and are the hallmark of every successful undercover investigation. They show that the investigators understand what the organization needs.

**Selecting an Agency**

- Police can conduct undercover work in companies, but they almost never have the time or resources. That is why most companies hire specialized private agencies.

| # | What the client should do | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Ask for a copy of the license, insurance, and permits | In many jurisdictions investigators and their agencies must be licensed. |
| 2 | Review the training program and the operatives' training records | The agency must do rigorous preemployment screening, periodic drug tests after hiring, and professional training. |
| 3 | Ask tough questions | About its knowledge and experience in similar investigations. |
| 4 | Ask for references and check them thoroughly | The best agencies are known in the business world and active in their industry associations. |
| 5 | Know its history of litigation and claims | Every operative must be willing to testify and to take their case through to the end. |
| 6 | Ask for sample reports | And examine them closely. |
| 7 | Require a certificate of insurance naming the organization as an additional insured | Coverage must be occurrence, not claims-made. |
| 8 | Ask for law enforcement references and cases in which the police helped | Prosecution of employees is not always needed. But drug cases and sex crimes, for example, cannot be handled without the police. |

| Policy type | Coverage |
| --- | --- |
| Occurrence-based (the one to require) | Protects regardless of when the claim is filed. |
| Claims-made | Protects only while the policy lasts. |

- The client must also know the duration of the work, all costs, and how client and investigator will communicate.
- The corporate attorney must meet with potential providers. Their role is critical and they must participate actively during the investigation.

**Selecting and Placing an Investigator**

- Today corporate operatives are usually well educated, well trained, and highly motivated.
- Drugs:
  - The best agencies drug-test their operatives routinely.
  - Tests must be random, because the operative may be exposed to drugs at any time. They protect their credibility and discourage carelessness.
  - Unless their life is in danger, the operative never uses or tries illegal drugs. Doing so destroys their credibility and puts the investigation at risk.
- Fitting into the workforce: the client helps the agency choose someone who goes unnoticed.
  - Personal factors to consider: sex, age, work experience, education, and ethnicity.
  - They must also fit the socioeconomic level and lifestyle of the workers.
  - Example: among unskilled workers, without a high school diploma and with low wages, the operative must not seem highly educated or spend more than the typical employee.
- Communication: needs good written and oral skills. Writes daily reports and speaks with the agency supervisor and, sometimes, with the client.
- Position:
  - It must give them mobility to deal with many employees and get the information in the shortest time.
  - At the same time they must be able to perform their cover position.
  - They can pass as an on-site contractor or as an internal employee.
  - As an internal employee they are treated the same as the others, with no preferential treatment or special consideration.
  - Placement is crucial: the objectives must be achievable from that position.
  - Example: if finished products are being diverted at the shipping dock of a distribution center, the ideal position is on the dock or near it.
- The operative's most important activity is learning the job and becoming familiar with the environment.

**Best internal positions for the operative (5):**

1. Material handling.
2. Shipping and receiving.
3. Mailroom.
4. Customer service.
5. Uniformed security.

- If the position requires special skills, the operative must already have them.
- They can be given prior training (remedial training). But it is usually cheaper to choose another investigator who already has that level.
- If they use a vehicle, no one should be able to trace it to the investigator.

**Ways of entering the position:**

| Way | How it works | What the book says |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cold hire | The operative applies for the job and is treated like any candidate. | It is the preferred one, but perhaps the least practical. |
| Controlled hire | The hiring process is secretly manipulated to place them in the desired position and place. | It does not assess it. |

**Cover story:** a fictitious reason or motive given to hide the true one.

- It explains the operative's identity and how they got the job.
- It can be superficial or highly elaborate. Rule: the closer the relationship with the target group, the deeper the story must be.
- Social media and the Internet are both a challenge and an opportunity.
- The company and the agency must review them to see whether the operative's identity can be discovered before the investigation advances.

**Elements of the cover story (4):**

| # | Element | Example or rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Connection to the operative's real past | Ideally they have already held a similar job. |
| 2 | A solid "back story", with false personal data but that the target employee knows | Saying they went to a certain school, worked at a certain place, or lived in the same neighborhood as the target. |
| 3 | Backing for the actions foreseen in the plan | If they will ask the target to introduce them to their "fence", they must have that contact. If they say they have a customized sport utility vehicle (SUV), they must drive one. |
| 4 | Documents that support the story | The normal ones of any employee in their situation. Never carry documents with their real identity on the assignment. Alternative the book cites: hiding their driver's license when driving a company, assigned, rented, or personal vehicle. |

#### 2.6.2 Phase Two: Information Gathering
