- It begins when the client, the agency, and the operative agree on the details of the assignment.
- It involves two types of assets:

| Asset | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| People | The members of the project management team. |
| Processes | How information is communicated, how it is reported, and how it is kept in the case files. |

**The Project Management Team**

- It oversees the investigative work itself. It can be formed from the investigative team.
- It is five distinct but related people. Each has a different role, and they coordinate and communicate everything among themselves.

| Role | Functions | Requirements and cautions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Undercover investigator (operative) | Can make or break the investigation. The first thing is "relationship building": they learn the job, get to know the environment, and make contacts. | The book calls this stage "the most important phase". They must not press for information or seem curious: it arouses suspicion. They must preserve evidence. Example: if a computer needs forensic analysis, notify managers so nothing is destroyed. |
| Project manager | Usually from the agency. Handles the day-to-day administration of the case and the daily care and attention of the operative. Keeps regular contact with the employer and the rest of the team. Is the channel through which all case information passes and is distributed. | Good communicator, with excellent writing and a solid grounding in investigation and management. Police or military experience helps, but is not necessary. High ethics, knows the civil and criminal law applied to the investigation, and treats people well. Needs patience: cases of six months or more bore and frustrate. Must supervise, motivate, and guide the operative. Otherwise an inexperienced operative may invent evidence to keep the case alive. Some operatives have been accused of entrapment and of creating and committing the illegal conduct themselves. |
| Client representative | The employer's main point of contact. All information entering and leaving the organization passes through this person. This protects confidentiality and greatly lowers the risk of accidental discovery. | Be careful how reports are delivered to them. Example: information technology staff may open an email believing it is spam and discover the investigation. Must be high on the organization chart, with authority to decide policy and spend money. Must have a strong character and be trustworthy. |
| Corporate attorney | Main reason to include them: to protect the investigator's work product from disclosure of evidence at trial (discovery). | Many agencies and clients do not include them, and lawsuits show it. The security professional who relies only on their own knowledge and the agency's attorney risks their career. Must be a labor law specialist with experience in labor claims. Must understand criminal prosecution and its effect on employer liability. Contributes more if they join in the planning phase, because pre-litigation preparation in undercover work is sophisticated and unique. Must be able to resolve labor, contractual, and criminal issues. |
| Law enforcement representative, if needed | Has a critical role in most cases. In drug cases, no purchase without approval from the police with jurisdiction. In other cases it provides resources and sometimes helps supervise the operative. | — |

- On the attorney, the book adds a sentence: "unlike litigation", the prosecution must present all the evidence it will use, even if it harms it. If it does not, the rest of the evidence would be inadmissible and useless to the case.

Exam note: that sentence in the book is confusing and does not explain the contrast with "litigation". It is reproduced as it stands.

Exam note: the book uses "attorney–client product and privilege" (in 2.5 and in case files) and here "work product". Possible typo: attorney–client privilege and work product are distinct concepts.

Exam note: it calls relationship building the "most important phase", although it is not one of the five phases.

**Communication**

- The team, however it is formed, does not work without good communication.
- There are three channels: written reports, electronic communications, and telephone.

*Written reports (the most basic form):*

- The operative can begin writing them even before arriving at work.
- Most agencies require a daily report as soon as possible after each shift.
- Basic content: who, what, when, where, how, and why of what they observed and experienced.
- They must be easy to read and describe accurately.
- The operative writes them at the end of the day and sends them to the project manager, who reviews and distributes them.
- One must write as if the reader knew nothing about the case.
- Avoid relative terms: "short" for height, "small amount" for the drug, "late day", "early hour".
- Simple, direct language gives accuracy and clarity.

**Report types in the chapter:**

| Report | Appendix | Who writes it and when | Features |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Chronological (timeline) report | 2A | Operative, daily | Basic report. The facts follow a logical sequence and each incident is easy to locate. |
| Structured narrative report | 2B | Operative, daily | One of the most popular formats today. Chronological but with more detail. Simple, readable, and conversational in tone. In third person. Each entry is an independent paragraph. |
| Investigative summary | 2C | Project manager; normally after 10 consecutive days of the operative's work | Brings together the daily reports and orders them by date and by topic. In third person. Replaces the dailies for the investigative team. |
| Special report | 2D | For a special or out-of-the-ordinary event | No fixed format. Must be easy to read and prepare. 2D came easily out of 2C. If it may serve as criminal evidence, the operative reviews and signs it before it is delivered to the client. |

**Why the narrative report is in third person** ("the operative", not "I"):

| Reason | Explanation |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Practical (editing) | In first person, technically only its author can edit it. Example: "I observed Ben Richards leave his assigned machine" would have to be changed to "The operative observed…". Then the operative would have to authenticate it again. Writing everything in third person avoids a lot of rewriting and saves time and effort. |
| 2. Sworn testimony | If the results are challenged, the project manager may have to testify under oath. They would have to admit they altered the operative's report. They may not know how to point out the changes and make a mistake. That error would be used to suggest that the report delivered to the client was inaccurate. In third person, they can explain that their report is not a doctored version. That way they can vouch for its accuracy and explain the differences from the originals. |

- If 2A and 2B are compared with 2C, it is clear that going from 2A to 2C would require a great deal of literary license and a lot of "red ink".
- Some investigators begin each paragraph with an alphanumeric code. That way the project manager can pull the dailies together and sort them by topic or by date, quickly and in electronic format.
- The frequency of the summary depends on who uses it. The typical one is after 10 consecutive days of work. That gathers enough information to analyze calmly and with continuity.
- Since the daily is already in third person, it passes into the summary without being redone. Reordering it by topic makes it more readable and useful.

*Electronic communications:*

- Reports uploaded to the agency's network at the end of the day are downloaded, edited, and forwarded to the client within minutes.
- The agency can send video of a drug sale at work to almost anyone with Internet.

*Telephone:*

- In addition to the written report, the operative must talk with the project manager every day.
- Normally they do not need to take or keep notes of those calls.
- The project manager may record the calls to show the client that communication took place.
- In the end, what serves as the basis for action are the written reports.

**Case Files**

- When it ends, everything is kept: original notes, reports, summaries, and any evidence.
- **Custodian of record:** the person designated to keep and file the closed case file.
- The attorney can order that all records be delivered to them. That may give them more protection as "attorney–client product". They must decide it before the investigation and put it in a written instruction.
- The format is chosen by the project manager, but it must be simple and orderly.
- Minimum content (5): the operative's daily reports, investigation summaries, special reports, correspondence, and billing and expense information.
- It is confidential. Its name must not allude to the operative or the investigation. Even a code name or a case number could raise suspicion.
- Folders and files are downloaded and stored securely in electronic format.
- Duplicates can be deleted to save space. The originals are always kept.

#### 2.6.3 Phase Three: Verification and Analysis

- When the operative is withdrawn, the rest of the team takes further investigative steps.
- Evidence, surveillance video, and findings are carefully reviewed.
- The investigation can move from covert to overt, for example with employee interviews.
- Interviews are one of the most critical components of the whole undercover investigation (see Chapter 5).

Exam note: the text calls Chapter 5 "Interview and Interrogation", but in the table of contents it is titled "Interviewing".

**Order of interviews:**

| Type of investigation | Order |
| --- | --- |
| General investigation | Interviews move closer to the perpetrators over time. |
| After an undercover investigation | They usually start with the most involved and move toward the least involved. |

- A good interviewer can see that the statements of the least involved provide information about the most involved.
- There is no fixed formula: each investigation is different.
- Afterward, the team brings together all the information and separates it by person.
- **Four sources of information:**
  1. What the operative obtained.
  2. What the employee said in their own personal interview.
  3. What others said in their interviews.
  4. The physical evidence.
- The team guides the company's representatives through all the information on each person.

Exam note: 2.6.3 places the interviews after the operative is withdrawn. But 2.7 says they can be left in their position during the interviews, and that withdrawing them earlier exposes them as an informant.

#### 2.6.4 Phase Four: Disciplinary and Corrective Action

- When the interviews and the investigation are finished, the team hands the information to the client's decision makers. They decide the discipline.
- The security professional and the rest of the team only gather information. Disciplinary decisions are made by other people in the company.
- After discipline, all files, records, recordings, and evidence go to the custodian of record, for example the attorney.
- Corrective action may be sending the case to the police. Prosecutors and police review the file and decide whether there is criminal prosecution.
- The company may be the victim of the crime, but it is not the one who prosecutes employees. That is decided by the police and prosecutors.
- The file must stand on its own. If not, civil attorneys can file tort claims.
- The book cites false imprisonment and malicious prosecution, and leaves the list open.
- Evidence is kept for the statute of limitations period set by law.
- It may be necessary to communicate with the rest of the workforce. But only after all discipline and corrections have been applied. Meetings must be direct and clear.
- Documentation is crucial. Without it, many juries will think the testimony should be discarded.

#### 2.6.5 Phase Five: Prevention and Education

**Basic elements on which crime at work depends (6):**

| # | Element | The book's nuance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Quality of the people hired | Past behavior predicts future behavior. |
| 2 | Environment in which those people are placed | — |
| 3 | Quality and location of the security that deters misconduct | — |
| 4 | Clear and decisive action when an employee misbehaves | If one waits for the annual review for minor matters, the employee sees it as punishment and injustice. Fairness is key: every employee expects it and deserves it. |
| 5 | Quality of supervision of that environment and those people | A toxic environment can create the motive: the offended employee wants to "even the score". |
| 6 | Initial and periodic training in policies and procedures | Effective technique: personalize the policy. |

**Examples for personalizing the policy (2 exercises):**

1. "Write three situations in which you think it is OK to \_\_\_." Options the book gives: steal, use drugs at work, harass others at work, sexually harass. The book leaves the list open.
2. "Give me one good reason to excuse an employee who \_\_\_." Options: steals from you, says you gave them the illegal drugs, sexually harasses you at work. The book leaves the list open.

- Most offenders discovered in an undercover investigation should never have been hired. The logical thing is to stop the problem at the door with good preemployment screening.
- The gate that protects the company and the workforce is three things: background investigations, verification of employment references, and good employment interviews. Chapter 4 covers it in detail.
- Hiring badly takes minutes. Undoing the damage takes months or years.
- Undercover leaves lessons: it can reveal training failures and educational gaps.
- The team makes recommendations to management. Management chooses those with the highest ROI.
- Senior management may discover that some managers did not correct or ignored employee warnings before the problem grew.
- Often supervisors and managers lack the skills and tools to keep the problem from recurring. The answer is education and training.

**Training modules the book proposes (5):**

1. Detection and prevention of substance abuse at work.
2. Loss prevention and asset protection.
3. Prevention of employee theft.
4. Prevention of workplace violence.
5. Basic supervision and people management.

### 2.7 Removing an Operative

- In every undercover investigation there comes a point when useful information yields a diminishing return. Normally there is already enough information to remove the operative.
- When to remove them is one of the most debated topics among supervisors. The book's answer: keep them as long as possible.
- If they are removed, they must leave with a credible explanation.
- If interviews follow, they can be left in their position so as not to unmask them. They can even be interviewed as if they were a suspect.
- Good time to remove them: when they are named as an offender and when others are dismissed.
- Removing them earlier can arouse suspicion. If they leave before the interviews, everyone will know they were the informant. Remember that they may live in the same community.
- If at the end of the case they are not compromised, they can remain very valuable in their position.
- After a highly visible investigation, communication must be open with all levels of the organization.
- After discipline in a high-profile case, undetected offenders grow confident. Some boast of having escaped. They become easy targets for the operative and for new investigations.

### 2.8 Types of Undercover Investigations

- Undercover can reveal many loss prevention problems. Drug and theft investigations are especially well suited.

#### 2.8.1 Drug Investigations

- Contrary to what is believed, most are relatively easy and not always dangerous.
- Once the operative is accepted by coworkers, they can focus more.
- With the contacts from relationship building, they begin to identify those who use.
- With the guidance of the project manager and the police, they systematically seek information on each identified user.
- They can end up buying drugs from anyone who wants to sell. That way they separate users from dealers.
- The guidelines for buying drugs at work are not public policy or law. They are written by the investigative team to guide the operative.

**Guidelines for buying drugs (5):**

| Guideline | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Qualify the dealer | Entrapment = inducing a person to commit a crime. It is not a crime, but a criminal defense. To neutralize it, the operative "qualifies" each dealer with a simple two-point report. First: that the conduct already existed or that the dealer had sold before. Second: why the dealer wants to sell to them; the ideal motive is to make money. With a motive of their own, the dealer cannot claim to have been induced. The operative never invites another employee to commit a crime. |
| Coordinate with law enforcement | Every purchase is made under the control and supervision of the police. It is not always possible, but one must try to have the competent police know and approve. Especially in the "buy-bust" at the end. Without that immunity, the operative and the agency could be prosecuted for possession, transport, or purchase of a controlled substance. |
| Buy down, not up | The police usually buy ever larger quantities to move up the distribution chain to the source. At work the smallest quantity the dealer will accept is bought. The company only has to prove that a policy was violated; the quantity does not matter. That way purchases are made more often and from more people. Larger purchases are left for the end. |
| Make purchases on company time | In general, the company does not control what the employee does outside work. The focus is workplace misconduct, not enforcing public law. |
| Avoid actual drug use | Unless their life is at risk, the operative does not use illegal drugs. To maintain their credibility with the client, they are tested routinely. Periodic hair and urine tests show they do not use. |

- Purchases give credibility to the report of drug use at work. But making no purchase does not sink the case.
- Reasons to forgo a permitted purchase (examples from the book):
  - The dealer insists on buying outside the company, in places where they would be alone with the investigator.
  - The dealer insists that the operative use before selling to them.
- A well-designed investigation anticipates these situations in planning and finds solutions for them.
- Experienced investigators and arbitrators know it: purchases are not needed for a good drug case. "Good investigations make good drug cases."
- More than an operative is needed: use all the available tools. The book cites chemical tests and dogs, and leaves the list open.
- If criminal prosecution is among the objectives, one must confirm that it is needed. Its value is calculated within the total ROI.
- Criminal prosecution of employees rarely gives a real return to the company. Disciplinary action is preferable: criminal prosecution only reduces profits further.
- The company must decide on dismissals without depending on the criminal system. Plea bargains and evidentiary problems do not count in the disciplinary decision (King 2015).

#### 2.8.2 Theft Investigations

- They are like any other undercover investigation, but demand special care in planning.
- Strategy and tactics weigh heavily. The operative must be visible and accessible to those who steal.
- At the start it is not known who they are. That is why this part can be difficult, expensive, and slow.
- Alternative: draw the thieves toward the operative.
- Basic tactic: the operative appears to be already involved in some criminal activity. Someone who already breaks the law in one way easily joins in breaking it in others. This is called **cognitive consistency**.
- A well-known axiom says that where there is substance abuse there is also theft. Whoever investigates a theft must also look for use.
- If they appear to be involved in use, the operative gains direct access to those who steal or divert assets.
- Another investigator or a third party can play the shady partner with criminal contacts. They go in and out of the game without much preparation.
- The operative does not call them a fence, thief, or trafficker. They present them as a trusted friend, relative, or gang member.
- In theft cases, the operative can take part in a plan to steal. But each action must have an explicit purpose and the prior approval of the project management team.
- Lure: offering small electronic devices, cigarettes, or common hand tools at ridiculous prices, so that coworkers think they are stolen. Event tickets are another popular gift or item for sale.
- Caution: bringing in weapons of any kind is an unnecessary danger and almost surely illegal.
- Example: the operative who sells cheap merchandise may be asked for a gun. They must not be allowed anything that reduces their safety or that of the workplace, or anything illegal or inappropriate.

Exam note: do not confuse. In drug cases, the operative never invites another to commit a crime. In theft, they can take part in a plan to steal, with an explicit purpose and prior approval.

**Retail Theft**

- Theft occurs in any business, but it is especially common in retail.
- Retail shrink reached an all-time high in 2019. It was 1.62% of the retailer's "bottom line" and cost U.S. retailers about $62 billion (National Retail Federation 2020).

Exam note: possible typo. Shrink is usually measured as a percentage of sales, not of the bottom line. Memorize 1.62%, 2019, and $62 billion.

- **Organized retail crime (ORC):** it is a big problem and keeps changing because of new channels for selling what is stolen. It is increasingly organized, complex, and lucrative.
- **Definition by Matas (2020):**
  - Any organized, conspiratorial criminal attack against a retailer.
  - It involves two or more people who illegally or fraudulently obtain merchandise, tender, confidential data, and personally identifiable information of customers.
  - Its sole purpose is to convert that into criminal profit.
  - It is usually coordinated with well-planned procedures, rules, and technical knowledge.
  - It can operate at the local, regional, national, or international scale.
  - It causes worldwide losses of hundreds of billions of dollars a year.

Exam note: do not confuse total U.S. shrink ($62 billion, National Retail Federation) with worldwide ORC losses (hundreds of billions, Matas).

- Undercover is a tool against ORC because often an employee is the gang's inside accomplice.

**Areas where ORC has an impact (12):**

1. Shoplifting.
2. Sophisticated fraud schemes.
3. Deep supply chain theft.
4. Employee theft.
5. Identity theft.
6. Warehouse theft.
7. Complex return frauds.
8. Money laundering operations.
9. Theft in shipping and transport.
10. Creative con games.
11. Cyber frauds.
12. Counterfeiting.

- Public-private partnerships are often formed against ORC.
- **CVS case (WLS-TV 2019):**
  - 11-month undercover investigation in Chicago.
  - The CVS Organized Retail Crime Team, the Wilmette and Evanston police departments, and the Criminal Investigations Division of the Illinois Department of Revenue took part.
  - Result: 11 search warrants, $2 million in merchandise recovered, and 3 suspects arrested for reselling what was stolen.
  - It shows the value of public-private partnerships.
- According to Matas, the gangs hire hackers, mathematicians, and programmers. They get into the computer infrastructure to identify account algorithms.
- With that they launch attacks on the point of sale, attacks on web applications and e-commerce, insider threats, and coordinated attacks on databases and their own operating systems.
- **Effective alternative to undercover:** visible loss prevention agents. A study by the John Jay College of Criminal Justice found that they reduced shrink better than undercover agents (Seivold 2018).

**Buy-Busts and Sting Operations**

| Technique | What it is | When and features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Buy-bust | Arresting the perpetrator at the moment they buy or sell the item. The other party is usually a police officer or a designated agent. On an agreed signal, the person is confronted and arrested on the spot. | Prevents evidence from being lost or destroyed. Avoids having to pursue dangerous offenders later. Works best at the end, as in drugs. Since it usually closes the investigation, the identity of operatives and police may be revealed on purpose. But it does not always happen or always need to. |
| Sting operation | Similar, but the term indicates a longer and more complex effort. It usually includes elaborate setups and buy-busts. It can include setting up and running a front business where stolen goods are sold or exchanged. | Because of its complexity, it is usually expensive and slow. |

**Other tools in theft cases:**

- **Property markers:** invisible markers and sprays that have existed for years. They secretly mark goods; if stolen and recovered, the owner is identified. They are applied to vulnerable items.
- **Corroborating evidence:** in most theft cases, evidence separate from the operative's is worth a great deal.
  - The most common and cheapest form is video.
  - The scene is planned so that operative and thief are well framed on camera. That way the stolen item, the money, and the whole transaction are documented.
- **Multiple buys:** as in drugs, they make the case more solid.
  - The operative should always attempt more than one purchase from each participant.
  - They eliminate the argument that it was something spontaneous or isolated.
  - They prove the person is a real offender and not someone who made a mistake once.
  - At the start a minimum quantity is bought.
- **Recoveries:**
  - Knowing who steals and what they steal is important. Equally valuable is knowing why they steal and how they dispose of what is stolen.
  - If third parties receive the stolen goods, there can be civil recovery. The investigation can be designed to recover from them as well, in addition to the main perpetrators.
  - The item or its value (consideration) is recovered, and also the costs of the investigation.
  - Such an agreement greatly increases the ROI of any investigation, not just undercover.

#### 2.8.3 Other Investigations

| Area | Problem | How undercover helps |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Seniors and childcare facilities | Abuse and mistreatment of the elderly, who often cannot defend themselves or report. In childcare facilities, abuse and crimes against a vulnerable population. | Operatives posing as staff, nurses, or doctors infiltrate and monitor operations and employees. Not even hidden cameras know what is said behind closed doors or what the children are told to do or think. Well managed, they detect abuse and neglect. |
| Health care (hospitals) | Visitors and staff can no longer be allowed to move around freely. Baby theft, drug diversion, equipment sabotage, and assaults are concerns. Because they are open, they are an ideal target for thieves, vandals, and other criminals. More locks, cameras, and guards do not eliminate the problem. | Operatives inside the staff, or posing as patients and visitors. They are a cost-effective tool that complements the security that already exists. The great mobility of employees and visitors makes the hospital a perfect environment for undercover. |
| Technology companies | They must maintain their edge. If not, their own advances will be used to steal secrets, ideas, and people from them. | Some large U.S. technology companies use operatives discreetly to protect their most important secrets. It is cost-effective and appropriate. |
| Governments | — | Investigate discrimination complaints. Secretly monitor food processing plants and waste disposal sites. |
| Transportation and food and drug producers | Chains of interlocked companies and high turnover make them vulnerable to waste and fraud. | Could benefit from undercover. |

- **Fraud:** intentional deception to unlawfully take possession of another's property.

### 2.9 The Cost of Undercover Operations

- The size, scope, and nature of the problem decide how much the company should invest.

| Billing model | How it works | What the book says |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per week (or for a longer period) | Weekly rate based on a 40-hour week plus a minimum of "cover pay". Cover pay is the wage the operative collects for their cover position. The rate includes the time of the investigator and their supervisor, report writing, transcription, postal mail, and communication with the client contact. It covers everything except out-of-pocket expenses and other special supporting investigations. The operative keeps the cover pay and the agency pays them the difference up to their normal salary. | It is how most experienced firms charge today. |
| Per hour | Report preparation and calls are charged separately. Writing time, travel time, and overtime are also billed. | Done by less experienced agencies. The client feels "nickeled and dimed to death". Complicated invoices suggest everything is charged, minute by minute. Experienced clients know this system sets up hidden charges. |

- **Operative levels:** some agencies say they have several skill levels and charge a difference based on experience. Others pretend to have enormous "herds" of investigators by level.
- If an agency says so, the client should ask for:
  1. Written descriptions and requirements for each level.
  2. How many investigators it currently has working undercover at each level.
  3. An investigator and a supervisor from each level to interview.
- The best agencies do not have "second-level or tenth-level" employees. That system is too complicated and expensive to maintain.
- **Undercharging is much more common than overcharging**, and it does not benefit the client:
  - Good agencies that are struggling cut corners where they can and take unnecessary risks.
  - It unfairly reduces the profits of healthy agencies.
  - It reduces the ability of all to pay their investigators well and give them benefits.
  - It fills the market with inexperienced and unscrupulous competitors (inexperienced cutthroats), with dubious quality.
- No business keeps providing a service if it does not make money. Undercover is no different.
- Due diligence is essential when choosing an agency and operative. Police resources and contacts help identify agencies with a good ethical and success record.

### 2.10 The Future of Undercover Investigations

- The future is solid: more and more organizations see that undercover solves what other security methods do not.
- To compete in the global economy, companies cannot assume losses that can be discovered and corrected discreetly. Nor can they risk their reputation because of what their employees do.
- Types that are growing: cybercrime and social media abuses, such as bullying and child predators. Workplace harassment may also require undercover.

### Key terms

corporate undercover investigation · operative · surreptitious placement · surrogate vendor / customer · covert operation · sub-rosa · interactive investigation · Pinkerton · The Spy of the Rebellion · Molly Maguires · entrapment · conversion · conspiracy · reasonable expectation of privacy · protected union activities · overt vs. covert · cover up · return on investment (ROI) · attorney–client privilege · work product · discovery · chief security officer (CSO) · bar association rules · conflict of interest · need to know · exposure / compromise · pull-the-plug · investigative team · project management team · project manager · client representative · corporate attorney · law enforcement · self-reporting · remedial action · five phases (planning and preparation, information gathering, verification and analysis, disciplinary and corrective action, prevention and education) · unfair labor practice · embezzlement · additional insured · occurrence-based vs. claims-made · preemployment screening · cold hire · controlled hire · cover story · back story · fence · relationship building · daily report · chronological report · structured narrative report · third person · reauthentication · alphanumeric code · investigative summary · special report · custodian of record · case file · physical evidence · false imprisonment · malicious prosecution · statute of limitations · even the score · diminishing return · qualify the dealer · buy down, not up · buy-bust · sting operation · users vs. dealers · plea bargain · cognitive consistency · shrink · organized retail crime (ORC) · tender · personally identifiable information · public–private partnership · visible loss prevention agents · property markers · corroborating evidence · multiple buys · recoveries · civil recovery · drug diversion · fraud · cover pay · out-of-pocket expenses · nickeled and dimed · undercharging

## Capítulo 3 — Due Diligence Investigations (págs. 121–136)

**Central idea:** Due diligence is a proactive investigative process. It is used to make security risk management decisions. There is no single standard: it depends on the objective, the situation, the jurisdiction, privacy laws, and the time frame. The key is to understand what the client needs and to verify everything with primary sources, not only with what the other party provides.

### 3.1 Definitions

| Source | Definition (paraphrase) |
| --- | --- |
| ASIS International (2021, Security Glossary) | The care expected of a prudent person in examining and evaluating risks. |
| Black's Law Dictionary (Garner 2016) | The diligence reasonably expected, and ordinarily exercised, by someone who seeks to satisfy a legal requirement or an obligation. |
| Merriam-Webster (2021), sense 1 | The care of a reasonable person to avoid harming other people or their property. |
| Merriam-Webster (2021), sense 2 | The investigation and analysis of a company or organization before a business transaction, for example a merger or a purchase of securities. |
| Common business usage | The investigative process to evaluate a target company or its assets before a buyer acquires it. |
| Broad sense | "Doing your homework": investigating a matter, a person, or a deal to know whether it is legitimate. |

#### Origin

- It is born in the securities industry. Today it is a way of managing risk.
- The first common use of the term is in the Securities Act of 1933 (U.S. Securities Act of 1933).
- That law created the "Due Diligence Defense" for broker-dealers.
- It applied when investors accused them of not disclosing material information.
- If the broker-dealer had investigated and shared its findings, it was not liable for the negative that it had not discovered.
- That is why broker-dealers adopted it as standard practice. Today the term is used in almost any sector.

#### When it applies and what features it has

- It applies when a party decides to do (or not do) something based on current conditions or on what another party states.
- There is no absolute standard. It depends on five things: the objective of the investigation, the situation, the jurisdictions or geographic areas, privacy laws, and the time frame.
- Each case is unique. Appendix 3A has a suggested checklist for acquiring a company.
- It is partly subjective: one reviewer may conclude that due diligence was done and another that it was not.
- It is judged after the fact: when the deal has already closed and the investigation is compared with what really happened.
- In situations that repeat (mergers, choosing someone for a fiduciary position) models can be created. Not everything fits in a checklist, but a lot does.

#### Due diligence, negligence, and due care

| Concept | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Diligence | The positive side of due care. |
| Negligence | Failing to exercise due care. |
| Due care | Giving reasonable protection. |
| Due diligence | The effort and steps that show or prove that there was due care. |

- The idea of due diligence can be compared with the doctrine of negligence.
- Like negligence, failing to do due diligence can end in a judgment for damages if another party is harmed.
- Even if there is no harm to third parties, the one who fails also pays: they lose money, reputation, opportunities, and time through bad decisions.
- In short, it is a proactive investigative process that supports security risk management decisions.

### 3.2 Relevance to Assets Protection and Security Risk Management

- The main reason to do it is to protect the organization's assets.
- Not knowing whether a person, company, organization, or situation is legitimate and reliable greatly raises the risk of any deal.
- Due diligence is the point where investigations and security risk management intersect.
- It can be voluntary (a prudent practice) or required by law, regulation, or internal policy.
- It can be very informal, such as vetting a vendor, or very formal and lengthy, such as preparing a large joint venture. Also everything in between.
- The objective is always the same: to be as well informed as possible before entering a situation.
- It is especially critical in dealings with third parties.

#### Example: a manufacturer seeks a distributor

The manufacturer must check that the candidate:

- knows how to sell;
- has the financial and logistical capacity to maintain inventory;
- has a good reputation, so the association does not tarnish the manufacturer;
- has secure and adequate warehouses.

Without these guarantees, the manufacturer risks product, time, and reputation. Losses can be direct (the product), of opportunity, of market share, and of reputation. A warehouse without security is already a vulnerability that a threat can exploit.

Before signing, the manufacturer would want to know the following (13 categories):

| Category | What it wants to know |
| --- | --- |
| Business history | How many years it has been operating, how successful it is, and what products it distributes today. |
| Distribution method | How it distributes, whether it has its own trucks and how many. |
| Product tracking | What system it will use to track the product and protect it from the warehouse to the points of sale. What methods it has to identify the product throughout the chain. |
| Security and insurance | How secure the warehouse and the distribution network are. What insurance it has. |
| Relationships | Whether it is affiliated with other companies or with competitors. Whether its relationship history is good or problematic. Whether it can ally with others to distribute, sell, or market. |
| Litigation | Whether it has had lawsuits, with whom, and why. Whether they are the normal cost of business or reflect underlying problems. Whether it was plaintiff or defendant. Whether it tends to break contracts or sue its partners. |
| Reputation | The reputation of the company and of its managers, officers, and decision makers. Whether associating with it helps or harms. |
| Financial and credit issues | Its financial and credit situation and history. Whether it has resources to maintain inventory. Where its funds come from and how much there is. How much debt it has. Whether it has ever declared bankruptcy. |
| Sales and marketing | Whether it will help sell and promote. What channels it has, how large its sales team is, and in what media it advertises. |
| Officers and managers | Who decides and whether they have "skeletons in the closet". Their history, reputation, and financial situation. |
| Total sales volume | How much it sells today and how much more it could handle. |
| Facilities | What facilities it has, owned or leased. Their size, condition, and location. |
| Profitability | Its current and historical profitability, as a measure of operating efficiency. |

- Getting reliable answers to these questions is doing due diligence. The list is not exhaustive.
- A broader due diligence may also review nine areas: strategy, information technology (IT) infrastructure, data security, regulatory compliance, operations, outsourcing protocols, human capital, global reach, and culture.

#### Friendly and hostile deals

| Type of deal | Access to information |
| --- | --- |
| Friendly (acquisition or alliance) | The parties exchange information easily so the deal goes through. |
| Hostile | It is harder to obtain data from the target. |

- A common mistake is to rely only on the information the target provides.
- That information is usually biased (sometimes unintentionally), incomplete, or with things overlooked.
- If the target wants to close the deal, it will present the most favorable version.
- Verifying with primary sources is key to an impartial and complete due diligence.

### 3.3 Applications of Due Diligence Investigations

Transactions where it is used (7):

- Mergers and acquisitions.
- Divestiture actions.
- Joint ventures.
- Hiring and executive placement.
- Fiduciary level of trust determinations.
- Financial and real estate investments.
- Key outsourcing decisions, for example choosing a data center or cloud provider.

Internal use: to verify that policies, procedures, standards, governance processes, and documentation are aligned with the organization's goals and responsibilities. For example, that there are protocols throughout the company to protect information assets and privacy.

#### Market entry planning

It applies in six situations:

- Launching a new product or service line.
- Taking a product or service line to a new market, region, or country.
- Signing with a new partner or creating a joint venture.
- Opening an office or a research and development, manufacturing, or distribution center.
- Bringing in a new partner or a new strategy in the supply chain.
- Closing an office or facility.

It focuses on two things: (1) protecting intellectual property (IP) and information assets, and (2) complying with local laws, regulations, and policies.

The process can include seven steps:

- Identify and value IP and intangible assets.
- Thoroughly review the markets for infringements of intellectual property rights (IPR).
- Review what IPR registration each market, region, or country requires.
- Evaluate whether the IP laws of each jurisdiction allow continued use, ownership, and control of the IP, information, and intangibles during the deal and afterward.
- Inspect in person the new facilities or organizations involved.
- Review that contracts, agreements, security plans, licenses, registrations, insurance, and management practices are valid.
- Hire a trusted consultant or advisor who knows the culture, customs, language, laws, and regulation of each place.

#### What makes a good due diligence

- Since there is no single list, the approach and results vary greatly.
- Done well, it is the basis of a good decision. Done badly, it can completely mislead the parties.
- It does not stop at verifying: it uncovers new information, raises questions, and points to new areas to investigate.
- Example: failing to detect an internal investigation of the chief financial officer (CFO) for fraudulent accounting can sink the deal.
- It must be done, impartially and thoroughly, even with parties one believes one knows.
- It also helps detect risks that could keep the organization from meeting its objectives.

#### U.S. legal framework

- The USA Patriot Act of 2001 gave new obligations to financial institutions and multinationals.
- They must make the maximum effort to detect violations of the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act and the Patriot Act.
- The Department of Justice (DOJ) published the guidance Evaluation of Corporate Compliance Programs (ECCP) in three versions: 2017, 2019, and 2020.
- The ECCP requires due diligence in mergers, acquisitions, and third-party relationships. It looks for evidence of compliance failures, fraud, or other misconduct.

#### Missing information also counts

- Due diligence helps make good decisions even if it does not review every piece of data. It does not guarantee success, but it gives valuable insight.
- The gaps also inform:

| Gap | What it may indicate |
| --- | --- |
| It is almost impossible to get the financial statements | The company may have reasons to hide them, such as covering up a fraud. |
| Gaps in a person's work history | Technically it is a lack of data, but it says a lot about their history. |
| No reputation in their sector, no press, and no presence in trade magazines | They may not have the track record they advertise. |

- At a minimum, a good due diligence raises issues to investigate, confirms or contradicts what was received, and points to new sources.
- It does not guarantee success or prevent all errors of judgment. But with good practices, the report serves as a basis for further consultation and negotiation.

#### Madoff case

- Bernard Madoff attracted investors to his hedge fund by promising higher returns than any other fund or securities investment.
- At the end of 2008 it was discovered that he was not making the investments he claimed. It was a $50 billion Ponzi scheme.
- Because of his good reputation, investors skipped due diligence in exchange for speed, high returns, and getting into the fund.
- According to the Wall Street Journal, some analysts repeated his supposed trades and did not achieve the same returns. They doubted that Madoff made them.
- Barron's suggested that his returns probably came from front running on the clients of his own brokerage.
- Madoff discouraged questions and hinted that asking could close the door to investing.
- Lesson: use outside investigation experts, attorneys, and accounting firms for a complete and impartial due diligence.
- Exam note: the text attributes the analysts' point to the Wall Street Journal. The chapter's references only cite a New York Times article (Berenson & Henriques 2008).

### 3.4 Digital and Cyber Aspects of Due Diligence Investigations

They are two related areas, but the book says they must be treated separately:

| Area | What it consists of |
| --- | --- |
| Online and social media due diligence | Investigating public open-source information about the person or entity: their footprint on the internet and on social media. |
| Cyber and data security due diligence | Asking the target about its internal policies, protocols, security measures, incident response plans, and known data breaches. It seeks to reduce the risk of hidden cyber vulnerabilities. |

#### 3.4.1 Online and Social Media Due Diligence

- Almost every company is on social media. They use it to make the brand known, connect with customers, market to online audiences, and sell.
- Online sources help assess reputation, risks, and negative publicity.
- Example: an increase in complaints and negative comments around a date is seen.
- It is useful to confirm data from other sources, such as a new chief executive officer (CEO), a launch, or an alliance.
- Consumers demand social responsibility and hold companies and their executives to account on social media. This reveals the brand's reputation and its relationship with customers.
- It can bring to light key events, executives, and milestones that would not be published otherwise.
- It can include executives' public personal accounts. A personal comment by an executive can damage the company's reputation and profits.

#### 3.4.2 Cyber and Data Security Due Diligence

**Marriott–Starwood case**

- In 2015, Marriott announced the purchase of Starwood Hotels & Resorts for more than $12 billion. It thus became the largest hotel chain in the world.
- A few years later it faced fines of more than $100 million from U.S. and other countries' regulators, as well as class actions.
- The cause was a breach of Starwood guest data that, according to what was published, began in 2014.
- It exposed data of hundreds of millions of people, including millions of passport numbers.
- Lesson: Marriott did not detect the breach in its due diligence before the purchase and paid dearly for it.

**What to do**

- Cybersecurity and data security must be part of every due diligence.
- It is advisable to work with cyber forensic auditors, IT and cybersecurity experts, and legal counsel.
- The aim is to build cyber risk profiles and evaluate how an incident is prepared for, responded to, and mitigated.
- A breach, even if no one has detected it, affects the company's resources, valuation, and public image.
- Role of the security professional: security experts, especially cybersecurity ones, must come in early and take part often.
- It is not a risk only for technology companies: it affects all industries.
- One must review what sensitive information, proprietary or trade secret, is vulnerable: IP, designs, formulas, and financial information.
- Other possible components: threat intelligence, Dark Web research, leaked customer data, penetration testing, and network vulnerability assessment.

### 3.5 Techniques and Steps in Due Diligence Investigations

- There is no single, objective criterion.
- First step: examine the matter (the deal, the person, the investment) and understand which factors to look at.
- The strategy must answer at least these nine questions:

| No. | Question |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | What is the nature of the situation? What is at risk (for example assets, reputation, market share)? |
| 2 | What background does it have? How well do the parties know each other? |
| 3 | Who are the main players? |
| 4 | What factors matter to those making the deal? |
| 5 | Which problems would be the most costly? Which minor problems could have an influence? |
| 6 | What type of transaction do they expect? Of what size and how complex? |
| 7 | How much time and money will investigating cost? How hard will it be to obtain the information? |
| 8 | What risks do the players run? How much does it cost them if the deal fails? |
| 9 | Does the objective fit within the organization's risk appetite, risk tolerance, and risk capacity? |

- The key is to understand what the client needs and the specific transaction.
- If the investigator does not understand the object of the investigation, they will almost surely fail: they will not ask the right questions.

#### 3.5.1 Information Sources

| Primary sources | Secondary sources |
| --- | --- |
| Interviews with people | Government public records |
| Direct observation of people, places, or events | Commercial sources of records |
|  | Annual reports and other company documents |
|  | News and other media |
|  | Federal criminal and civil records |
|  | State criminal and civil records |
|  | Local and municipal records |
|  | Social media intelligence |

General rules:

- Many secondary sources are on the internet, but the data must be confirmed with the original source.
- The book gives U.S. examples. Other countries have agencies with equivalent records.
- In the U.S., some investigations fall under the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA, federal law on credit reports). This is because their result counts as a consumer report.
- Some sites require a current private investigator license. Others are open to the public.
- Access and records change daily, so sources must be checked regularly.
- Advantage of government records: they are usually impartial.

Sources the investigator can consult (19):

| Source | What it provides | Limits and notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Clerk records (county clerk records) | Liens, judgments, litigation, marriages, divorces, properties, mortgages, and other vital data. They show relationships, addresses, and patterns of behavior. | They usually give leads on where to keep looking. |
| SEC filings (filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission, the U.S. securities commission) | Data on publicly traded companies, licensed broker-dealers, and investment funds. The 10-K (annual report) covers organization, activities, finances, legal matters, and officers. The 10-Q (quarterly) updates the 10-K with important changes. | Private companies are not required to file them. They are consulted at www.sec.gov, in the SEC's public reading rooms, or by mail. Appendix 3B lists regulators of other countries. |
| Secretary of State or Division of Corporations (each state's corporate registry) | Documents of companies incorporated in the state: annual reports, name changes, mergers and acquisitions, date of incorporation, status, and names and addresses of officers. | Varies by state. Many give access online, by phone, by mail, or in person. |
| State regulatory agencies and licensing boards | License status, complaints, judgments, specialties, training, and other data. They regulate doctors, lawyers, therapists, private investigators, security guards, and gaming workers. | — |
| Uniform Commercial Code (UCC) filings | Sellers or creditors file them to record commercial debts. They show type and date of the transaction, amount, creditor and debtor (or seller and buyer), and how the debt is secured. | They are on the internet. |
| Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) documents | It allows requesting from agencies documents that are normally not public. The FBI, local police, the CIA, and the State Department have FOIA officers to give access to formerly classified documents. Some state and county offices too. | They take a long time to arrive. They often come with large redacted parts that cannot be published. |
| Criminal records | State and local ones, in most county clerk offices. Federal ones, in the federal court files at PACER.gov. | There is no legal way for the public to search the whole country at once: each state and county has its own rules. It is advisable to use reputable screening or investigation firms. There are online international searches for more than 200 countries, but they are hard to verify, slow, and expensive. |
| Property records | The county assessor, recorder, or auditor's office gives owner, value, sales, deed transfers, and previous owners. Google Maps and other free satellite services give aerial and street-level views. | — |
| Patent records | The U.S. Patent and Trademark Office gives free online access. Google Patents also allows searching for free. | — |
| Credit reports | TransUnion, Equifax, and Experian give financial, organizational, and personnel data on companies and individuals. | Signed authorization from the subject is required. By themselves they are not a good source of public information. What they say must be confirmed with primary sources. |
| Corporate reports | Dun & Bradstreet produces reports, especially on publicly traded companies. Lexis-Nexis and Dialog gather news on public and private companies. The company's own annual reports are also useful. | — |
| Watchdog data | CorpWatch has a step-by-step guide with links for researching companies. Others: Corporate Watch (the British version of CorpWatch), CSRWire, OpenCorporates, and Violation Tracker. | They can be biased by each group's point of view. |
| Interviews | They give information and context, and lead to other sources. | The interviewee tells things their own way. A skilled investigator still obtains important data. |
| Direct observations | Visual inspection of facilities, materials, or activities. | — |
| News media | Labor problems, lawsuits, public criticism, investments, and details that do not appear in other public records. They also give leads. | — |
| General Internet searches | Websites, blogs, and social media with a great deal of corporate and personal information. | Everything must be verified separately: nothing guarantees it is accurate. |
| Federal records | Screening firms access PACER, the federal repository of criminal, civil, bankruptcy, and appellate cases. | One must ensure the record is the subject's. Expertise is needed to obtain enough personal identifiers (the book abbreviates them "PII"). |
| State and local courts | Screening firms usually have direct access to state criminal and civil records. If they do not, they send trained investigators or court runners (people who go to the courthouse). | One must validate that the record corresponds to the subject, as with federal ones. |
| Social media | Of a company: reputation, relationship with customers, events, milestones, and news. Of a person: lifestyle and possible conflicts of interest. It can reveal critical details that are not in other sources. | It is a difficult source: identifiers are often missing to ensure the right person. An attorney's guidance is advisable: some jurisdictions restrict these searches. |

- Prudent policy: limit the social media search to four categories:
  1. Potentially violent behavior.
  2. Potentially illegal activity.
  3. Sexually explicit material.
  4. Ties to criminal, terrorist, or hostile groups.

#### 3.5.2 Outsourcing Due Diligence Investigations

**Who offers the service (5):** private investigation firms, market research firms, preemployment screening companies, law firms, and accounting firms.

**How to choose:**

- Each firm understands due diligence in its own way and not all investigate with the same depth.
- Interview them to understand their sources and methods before hiring.
- It is essential to ask for references and a sample report.
- Some providers are excellent and specialize by sector, region, or type of transaction.
- It works if the provider is properly verified and the objectives and tactics are clearly agreed.

**Why outsource only a part:**

- Lack of time.
- Lack of in-house experience.
- Topics that the internal investigative unit does not master.
- Investigations so sensitive that an outside expert is advisable.
- The risk of a leak to staff exists with outside attorneys or experts as much as with internal resources.

**Client–investigator relationship:**

- The client must give detailed background and work closely with the firm.
- Communication is critical to success.
- They must agree on the topics that matter most to the client, such as its goals and objectives.
- During the investigation, the investigator reports findings. The client decides whether a lead is followed or whether the information is already enough.
- Those decisions depend in part on the organization's risk appetite.

**International demand:**

- Big, high-profile frauds, such as Madoff's, make due diligence more critical and more routine in any project.
- International demand grows because of the global business environment, the economy, and threats such as terrorism and international organized crime.
- Examples: finding out whether a future joint venture partner had poor labor or environmental practices, or whether it was targeted by animal rights extremists.
- International free trade zones also increase the importance and demand for this due diligence.

#### 3.5.3 Reporting Due Diligence Findings

- How it is done and reported depends on the purpose and the audience.
- There may be formats set by sector or region, legal limits, or the organization's own requirements.
- The report must be coherent, easy to read, and complete.
- The investigator delivers it in writing and must be ready to defend it if needed.
- Content of the report (7):
  1. Identity of the client and the provider.
  2. Purpose and background.
  3. Scope of work.
  4. General methodology.
  5. Findings.
  6. Conclusion.
  7. Legal disclaimer, with any limits the investigation had.
- Confidentiality and dissemination controls are critical to the integrity of the report. Client and provider must agree on them before starting.

### Key terms

Due diligence · Due care · Negligence · Due Diligence Defense · Securities Act of 1933 · Broker-dealer · Target company · Vetting · Friendly deal · Hostile deal · Primary sources · Secondary sources · Mergers and acquisitions · Divestiture · Joint venture · Fiduciary level of trust · Market entry planning · Intellectual property rights (IPR) · Intangible assets · USA Patriot Act · Foreign Corrupt Practices Act · Evaluation of Corporate Compliance Programs (ECCP) · Ponzi scheme · Front running · Online and social media due diligence · Cyber and data security due diligence · Open-source information · Cyber risk profile · Threat intelligence · Dark Web · Penetration testing · Risk appetite · Risk tolerance · Risk capacity · Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA) · Consumer report · SEC filings · 10-K · 10-Q · Secretary of State / Division of Corporations · Uniform Commercial Code (UCC) filings · Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) · PACER · Court runners · Watchdog data · Social media intelligence · Legal disclaimer · Dissemination controls

## Capítulo 4 — Background Investigations and Preemployment Screening (págs. 143–165)

**Central idea:** A single bad hire can cost a great deal in theft, violence, lawsuits, and reputation. A background investigation program that is lawful, fair, and consistently applied prevents losses and protects against negligent hiring and discrimination claims. It is designed according to the position, the risk, the regulation, and the information available, with security, human resources (HR), and legal counsel working together.

### 4.1 The Need for Background Investigations

- A single bad hire (a thief, a drug user, or a violent criminal) can damage productivity, profits, reputation, and morale.
- The ideal program is comprehensive, lawful, fairly applied, and both confirmatory and investigative. It finds the right person and reduces losses.
- Losses are enormous on three fronts: employee theft and fraud, workplace violence, and use of illegal drugs and alcohol.
- The two main loss prevention strategies are candidate screening and employee socialization.
- The best way to reduce internal theft and violence is to hire people of integrity.
- Employees are often involved in workplace crimes, as perpetrators or as victims.
- In HR terms: hiring someone takes days or weeks, but getting rid of the wrong candidate often takes years.
- A good investigation reduces the risk of lawsuits for negligent hiring, for discrimination, and for costly mistakes.
- It is essential for the brand, staff morale, security, and asset protection.

#### Commonly confused terms

They are used as synonyms, but they are related and distinct disciplines.

| Term | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Background check | It is confirmatory: it verifies what the candidate provides, such as education and work history. |
| Background investigation or screening | It actively searches: it obtains new references and looks for criminal records. Its goal is to discover what the candidate might hide, such as crimes or dismissals. |
| Background screening, according to the ASIS Glossary | Inquiring into the history and conduct of a person being considered for employment, credit, access to sensitive assets (for example national defense information), or other purposes. |
| Preemployment screening | Determines whether the candidate is reliable or able to do the job. It can include all of the above plus tests. |
| Personal risk assessment (PRA) | The book mentions it among the terms that get confused, but does not define it. |
| Reference check | Contacting former bosses, supervisors, coworkers, and teachers. It verifies employment, education, character, attendance, knowledge, skills, and abilities. |

- The investigation can range from just checking jobs and degrees to reviewing civil, criminal, and medical histories.
- Preemployment screening can also include six tests:
  1. Drug or alcohol screening.
  2. Psychological tests (personality and integrity).
  3. General intelligence or aptitude tests.
  4. Medical exam and physical ability tests.
  5. Polygraph.
  6. Position-specific tests.
- The legality of each test and the moment when it can be applied are heavily regulated in many places. An attorney must be consulted.
- The background investigation and references are the main ways to obtain information from someone other than the candidate.
- The investigation looks for whether the candidate fails to qualify for four reasons:
  1. A relevant criminal conviction.
  2. Motor vehicle violations.
  3. Poor credit history, when it matters for the position.
  4. Lies about education or work history.

#### Legal liability

- In the U.S. and other countries, screening reduces the risk of being held liable for an improper hire.
- It is well established that the employer is liable for the negligence of the employee on duty or work-related.
- There are many precedents that make it liable also for intentional conduct by the employee. This occurs if it knew, or should have known, of a background that anticipated them.
- Example 1: landlords found liable for hiring ex-convicts who attacked residents of the building.
- Example 2: employers found liable for hiring people with records who injured coworkers in ways similar to their previous crimes.
- Some jurisdictions give statutory immunity "for obtaining references". Giving false, misleading, or malicious information about the former employee is prohibited.
- Exam note: the text says immunity for "obtaining" references, but the next sentence deals with "giving" information. Possible typo.
- When giving a reference, the employer must be truthful and first have the employee's signed authorization and consent.

#### Paul Calden case (Hull 2005)

- Fireman's Fund asked Allstate Insurance for references on Calden. He brought a reference letter signed by an Allstate executive. Allstate only confirmed the dates of employment.
- Once hired, he said he came from Mars and needed daily transfusions to clean his "Martian blood". He threatened a coworker with death for parking in "his" spot.
- They fired him. He kept calling to meet with the executives, threatened them with death, and was a suspect in the burglary at the HR manager's home.
- Eight months after the dismissal he came back and shot the five executives who had fired him. He killed three and injured two for life. Then he killed himself.
- At Allstate the same thing had happened: the Mars story, the transfusions, and a meeting he arrived at armed. They paid him a settlement to leave on his own.
- Allstate did not tell Fireman's Fund. This gave rise to the first lawsuit for negligent referral.
- Exam note: Hull's reference is listed with the date 2005, but its link points to a 1994 file.

#### Context and evolution

- For centuries it was easy to learn about someone: people lived in small communities, moved little, and knew each other at least by reputation.
- Today companies are global and the requirements of each position are more defined.
- The preemployment screening industry was born in the U.S. in the 1980s.
- Worldwide interest grew because of terrorism, insider threats, IP theft, and workplace violence.
- Today large companies implement and periodically review screening policies for candidates, employees, and trusted third parties.
- Many countries restrict preemployment evaluations by law. The main concerns are privacy and data protection.
- Many jurisdictions give exceptions for screening candidates.
- What information exists and how it can be used varies greatly from place to place. Many countries are still deciding how to regulate it. In others there is no data because it is poorly recorded.
- 2019 report of the Professional Association for Background Screeners (formerly the National Association of Background Screeners, NABS): 96% of employers use one or more types of screening (HR.com 2019).
- Most do it at hiring. Others do it throughout the employment life cycle.

### 4.2 Issues That Require Investigation

#### 4.2.1 Résumé Fraud

- It is not easy to hire someone honest, nonviolent, and drug-free: the honest and the dishonest look alike.
- Many résumés are misleading.
- HireRight 2020 study: the most common lies are in previous employment, criminal records, and degrees. They appear at all levels, including senior management (C-suite).
- Example: in January 2020, a senior executive of the UK's National Health Service received a two-year suspended prison sentence. He had lied with a degree in classics.

#### 4.2.2 Employee Theft

- It is one of the biggest problems for companies today.
- Global Fraud Survey 2018 (Ernst & Young 2018): survey of senior executives of large companies in 55 countries. Four findings:
  1. Although some countries improved, fraud and corruption did not decline worldwide since the 2012 survey.
  2. Younger respondents more readily justify fraud or corruption to meet financial goals or survive a crisis.
  3. Government fines reached levels never seen: more than $11 billion since 2012, imposed by U.S. and UK authorities.
  4. 93% said their leaders show commitment to compliance and 95% that they set a good ethical example. However, unethical conduct and business failures did not decline.

#### 4.2.3 Workplace Violence

- It is a serious concern for security professionals.
- In the U.S., homicide, the most extreme form, is the fourth leading cause of workplace death.
- Bureau of Labor Statistics, Census of Fatal Occupational Injuries: in 2018 there were 453 workplace homicides, out of a total of 5,250 workplace deaths.
- Society for Human Resource Management (SHRM 2019): almost half of HR professionals said their organization had a violence incident in 2019.
- Most said they have a process to detect employees with a violent history. Even so, 1 in 7 employees feels unsafe at work.
- Conclusion: a preemployment screening that detects candidates with problem behaviors is advisable.
- Exam note: the text cites "Bureau of Labor Statistics 2019", but the reference is dated 2020. It also says "outlined about": a typo for "above".

#### 4.2.4 Drug and Alcohol Use Among Workers

- Use at work harms everyone, owners and employees.
- Obvious effects: absenteeism, accidents, and errors. Less obvious but also costly effects: low morale and more illness.
- 2019 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (U.S.):

| Group | Illegal drug use in the past year |
| --- | --- |
| Adults over 26 | About 17% |
| Employees over 26 | About 20% |
| Employees aged 18 to 25 | About 42%, roughly double |

- Source cited: Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration 2019. Exam note: the reference is dated 2020.
- Some states legalized marijuana for medical use, recreational use, or both.
- When the book was published, marijuana, and specifically its component THC (tetrahydrocannabinol), was still illegal at the federal level.
- Many companies have drug-free policies: a positive test for THC can lead to sanction.
- According to the text, there has to be an incident or a suspicion that justifies the test. Example: an accident with a company vehicle.
- For legal liability, and even if there are state laws, the company can create and enforce drug-free policies to protect its employees (HF&C/Assurex Global 2014).
- Exam note: the incident point clashes with 4.7.1. There the drug test is a normal part of the final phase, and federal law requires it for some positions.

### 4.3 Benefits of Preemployment Screening

- The main purpose of investigating backgrounds is to prevent losses and reduce harm.
- Legally, it protects against lawsuits for negligent hiring and for discrimination.
- If a bad employee steals, the value of what was stolen is lost. The investigation and the recruiting, hiring, and training of the replacement are also paid for.
- That is why the asset protection program must gather and analyze all the relevant information the law permits.
- The policy must be cost-efficient: the most expensive components are applied last.
- Screening must be done and evaluated before the position is offered.

| Benefit | Why |
| --- | --- |
| Deters anyone with something to hide and encourages honesty | The candidate knows that what they say will be checked. |
| Shows that the company did due diligence | It can serve as a defense at trial. It attracts good candidates, who prefer a safe environment. |
| Confirms that the candidate has the background, education, experience, and skills they claim | The decision is based on facts, not on hunches or promises. |

### 4.4 Developing a Screening Program

Its function is to screen candidates in order to hire the best. It requires the participation of many people and areas.

| Who | Role |
| --- | --- |
| Senior management | First approves and backs the policy. |
| Security and HR team | Ideally designs and implements the policy. |
| Procurement (purchasing) | Can analyze the bid packages if the investigation is outsourced. |
| Legal counsel | At the end reviews and endorses the program, because of the many judicial and personal data compliance issues. |

The program changes according to four factors:

1. Access to information.
2. Nature of the position.
3. Legal liability if the work is done badly.
4. Industry regulation.

#### 4.4.1 Access to Information

- In a global environment, obtaining and verifying candidate data is a challenge of its own.
- International companies find it hard to maintain uniform hiring policies because the available information varies.
- Candidates from several countries bring their own complications. Customs and rules vary enormously.
- The most important thing is to know the privacy laws of each jurisdiction:

| Jurisdiction | Rule or regulation |
| --- | --- |
| Canada | Personal Information Protection and Electronic Documents Act (PIPEDA). |
| European Union | General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR). Other countries have similar measures. |
| U.S., Australia, and Canada | They prohibit certain questions about gender, ethnicity, age, disability, and other personal data. Elsewhere they may be legal. |
| U.S. | The FCRA regulates access to credit, insurance, and criminal records. Criminal convictions are usually public. |
| U.S. (FOIA) | "Public interest" records must be available. Credit history is not covered by this law and is not considered public. |

Cultural differences (Infocubic.com 2020):

| Country | What is needed |
| --- | --- |
| China | The name in Chinese characters and the Chinese identification number. Many institutions do not have the name translated. |
| Mexico and other Latin American countries | The mother's maiden name, to search records thoroughly. |

- That is why a local provider or a company specialized in global investigations can be hired.
- Whether done in-house or outside, the criminal and privacy laws of the country where the data are located must be well understood.

#### 4.4.2 Nature of the Position

- Not all positions require the same level of screening.
- More scrutiny is required for positions that:
  - handle highly sensitive information or large sums of money or other financial instruments;
  - have close contact with the public, work in customers' homes, or with children;
  - handle hazardous materials or protect other people.
- The organization must review its job descriptions and choose the appropriate level.

#### 4.4.3 Liability Issues

- Some positions hardly allow for error. It is taken for granted that ship captains, pilots, doctors, and child caregivers have the right training, skills, and education.
- It is up to the employer to verify it. Rule: the more responsibility, the deeper the screening.
- Questions the book raises: what if a pilot does not have the training or the alcohol and drug history they declared? What if a child caregiver has a violent background? What if the chief financial officer has a history of embezzlement?
- Accidents and injuries from the employee's error or incompetence usually end up being the employer's responsibility.
- Lawsuits and settlements can cost so much that they close the company.

#### 4.4.4 Industry Regulations

- In the U.S., federal and state laws set screening requirements in many sectors.
- They are usually sectors that require a professional license, that deal with vulnerable populations (children, the elderly), or that are high-risk.
- Examples: banking, transportation, child care, education, and security.

Although it varies according to those four factors, every program has the same basis (3):

1. Data collection: a standard application specific to the company, plus supplementary documents.
2. Investigation and screening according to written and standardized policies and processes.
3. Data analysis and hiring decision.

#### 4.4.5 A Comprehensive Preemployment Screening Policy

| Step | What to do | Details |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Write clear job descriptions for all positions | They describe the tasks and the personal qualities needed. Only clearly necessary qualities are requested. A requirement that cannot be justified can end in a complaint to a regulator or a civil lawsuit. In the U.S., employers with at least 15 employees must comply with the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) when setting requirements. The description is the yardstick of the first filter, which is why it is crucial. |
| 2 | Assess the risk of each job classification | The reasonable or foreseeable potential for problems is measured with seven questions (see below). |
| 3 | Define the scope of screening for each classification | Standard and consistent measures for temporary, permanent, full-time, part-time, contractors, and volunteers. Apply them rigorously across the whole classification, to avoid accusations of unfair or illegal hiring. Different levels by position are valid if they are appropriate and applied equally to all. Example: checking credit for all accountant applicants and for no cafeteria worker. Set periods, for example checking felonies from seven years back for a whole classification. Sometimes the law imposes it. |
| 4 | Assemble a hiring packet with HR and legal | It includes a complete application and the authorization and release forms. Legal reviews that it complies with the law. If the investigation finds inconsistencies, HR is notified that more investigation may be needed. |
| 5 | Set criteria for evaluating the information | Define what information disqualifies and who decides. For example, which prior crimes prevent holding certain positions. Many companies use a team of HR, security, and legal to review negative findings. The standard of proof for not hiring is "good faith". |
| 6 | Communicate the policy and its purpose to staff | On bulletin boards, intranet, employee handbook, or wherever it fits the culture. Highlight more security, less turnover, and greater operational effectiveness. |
| 7 | Periodically review compliance | Ensures it is applied equally throughout the company. |

Questions for step 2 (7):

1. Will the person have access to financial instruments or sensitive information?
2. Will the person have regular or unsupervised contact with the public? Examples: meter reader, appliance technician, carpet installer.
3. Will the person receive keys, alarm codes, or after-hours access?
4. Does the position require credentials, licenses, or a specific professional standing?
5. Will the person have to carry a firearm?
6. Will the person have access to hazardous materials, such as explosives or certain chemicals?
7. Will the person have to drive a vehicle?

### 4.5 Data Collection: The Basic Information Gathering Tools

- The investigation begins with what the candidate provides: the job application, the personal history questionnaire, the résumé or CV, and other biographical documents.
- Even if the candidate brings a résumé, they must be required to fill out the standard application designed by the company.
- A single tool is never used to evaluate a candidate. That way all the relevant and legal information possible is obtained.

#### 4.5.1 Résumé

- It is the first sign of whether the candidate qualifies. It serves for the first filter.
- If it does not show the necessary experience or education, the candidate leaves the process.
