- It is a promotional tool: it is hard to hold it strictly to account for what it says.
- That is why it is not accepted in place of the job application or a complete work history.
- It is always reviewed, because it provides additional information.
- The main source is the company's own application, which is reviewed before investigating.

#### 4.5.2 Application

- It is the starting point of screening.
- Its body is questions to know whether the candidate meets the minimums for the position.
- Role of the security manager: to help ensure the application allows tracing the candidate's history and has only lawful questions.
- In the U.S., the application counts as a "test", like any other selection tool.
- That is why it must be shown not to have adverse impact on candidates protected by federal anti-discrimination laws. An attorney helps verify it.
- Example application: Appendix 4A.

What it must include:

| Element | What to ask or state |
| --- | --- |
| Material omissions and misstatements | State that omitting or falsifying something material (important or significant) is grounds for ending the process when discovered. |
| Correct and complete name | Also any other name used in schools, addresses, or jobs. |
| Current and previous address | The current one, and the permanent or legal one if different. Also, all addresses during jobs, studies, or any period that will be verified. |
| Education | For each level: (1) name and address of the school; (2) course or program; (3) dates of attendance; (4) final status, for example graduated, degree earned, or withdrawn; (5) degree or certificate earned; (6) grade average or rank; (7) reason for withdrawal, if any. Many schools only confirm attendance or graduation; the candidate's authorization helps obtain more. |
| Licenses and certifications | Verify them with the issuer: that they were granted and have not expired. |
| Employment | The whole history, including self-employment, for the period to be reviewed. If 5, 7, or 10 years are reviewed, the candidate presents them in chronological order, with employment, self-employment, and unemployment. A form with only two or three boxes suggests nothing more is expected. The instructions must ask for the complete history and an extra sheet if needed. There must be a space to indicate whether the current employer can be contacted. |
| Data for each previous job | (1) Name and address of the employer; (2) starting and final position; (3) period; (4) reason for leaving; (5) name and phone number of the last direct boss. Appendix 4B has a reference form. |
| References | At least three people who have known the candidate for a year or more and are not relatives. Ask for names, work and home addresses, email, and phone. Also ask how long and in what capacity they know the candidate (neighbor, friend, coworker). After several interviews a trend should be visible about their performance and work style. The references are asked for another list of people who know the candidate; that second list can give a more accurate and complete picture. A former boss who still knows them is an excellent reference. |
| Military service (U.S.) | (1) Period; (2) branch; (3) type of discharge or separation; (4) rank at discharge. Outside the U.S., consult an attorney first. The Privacy Act limits what the military can reveal without a subpoena about unfavorable service, but the candidate can reveal it. That is why they are asked whether they had an honorable or dishonorable discharge and may be asked for the discharge document. |
| Criminal record | It is the most common screening method. Laws vary, so it is advisable to consult an attorney before drafting the questions. Ideally ask broadly: convictions, guilty pleas, and nolo contendere (no contest) pleas over a period, for example 7 or 10 years. State that a conviction does not automatically disqualify. |
| Release or authorization | The candidate agrees to the preemployment screening. The authorization also serves for future screenings for retention, promotion, or change of position. Many sources are regulated by law or have become reluctant to give data. In the U.S., if an outside service is used, the FCRA requires: (1) notifying the candidate that a consumer report may be requested, and (2) their written authorization. Both must be separate from the application. Consult an attorney about the laws of each state and municipality. |

Ban-the-Box and the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC):

- In the U.S. there is a strong trend to protect the rights of convicted persons, called "Ban-the-Box".
- Federal contractors, many states, and municipal governments prohibit asking about criminal records on the application until after a conditional offer.
- The U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC) has prosecuted employers with a blanket ban on hiring people convicted of felonies.
- That blanket ban violates its requirement of an "individualized assessment" of each conviction.
- An attorney must take part in the policies on criminal records.

### 4.6 Early Application Review and Initial Interview

- The first review of the application is an important tool.
- The candidate's falsehoods are of two types:

| Type | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Willful omissions of material facts | Deliberately omitting important facts. |
| Misrepresentations of material facts | Falsifying important facts. |

- The candidate may omit what hurts them. They may have been falsifying for years and believe no one will check. Far too often they are right.

#### 4.6.1 Red Flags in Early Application Review

The most common omissions and falsehoods are eight:

1. Unsigned forms.
2. Incomplete addresses or P.O. boxes. At this point the text adds that some give schools without dates or degrees, and that an unknown school is always verified.
3. Dates that do not add up among employment, education, or military service.
4. Employment gaps. Any gap of more than a month in employment or in life is investigated.
5. Changes of occupation. Abnormal work patterns are reviewed in detail.
6. Incomplete employment data or a former employer that has already closed.
7. Social Security number that does not add up (U.S. only). The number may not match the area where they say it was issued. On its own it does not weigh much, but it is a clue if the investigation needs to be expanded or the candidate questioned further.
8. Not giving the reasons for leaving. The candidate avoids them if they do not know what the former employer will say. If asked directly, they almost always answer something.

- Exam note: in point 2, the text about schools without dates does not fit its title ("incomplete addresses"). Possible editing typo.

#### 4.6.2 Initial Applicant Interview

- Best in person and after the candidate hands in the application.
- It serves to clarify omissions: missing signatures and dates, unanswered questions, or incomplete histories.
- No negative questions or questions that make the source judge are asked, because they give misleading answers.
- If it is clear that the candidate does not qualify, they are told on the spot so as not to give false hope.
- Videoconferencing has become more accepted, and the COVID-19 pandemic accelerated its use because of social distancing.
- According to recruiters, the key points of the in-person interview apply equally remotely: punctuality, attention, and preparation (Stuart 2020).

### 4.7 Final Selection Phase

- The best selection comes from a cooperative effort within the organization.
- If the candidate passes the first phases, they enter the final phase, where the deepest screening is.
- Most wait until the candidate is chosen to verify the résumé, references, criminal records, licenses, education, and other credentials.
- In this phase the candidate may take six tests:
  1. Drug or alcohol screening.
  2. Psychological tests (personality and integrity).
  3. General intelligence or aptitude tests.
  4. Medical exam and physical ability tests.
  5. Polygraph.
  6. Job tests.
- The legality and timing of each test are heavily regulated. An attorney must be consulted.

#### FCRA process for an adverse decision (U.S.)

| Step | What it requires |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Pre-adverse notice (letter) | If the candidate is expected to be rejected based in part on a consumer report (for example a background report from a third party), they are notified before deciding. The letter goes with several documents, the FCRA Summary of Rights, and a copy of the report. |
| 2. Reasonable period | The candidate must have time to review the report, see whether it is accurate and complete, and dispute whatever is inaccurate. |
| 3. Adverse decision letter | If they do not dispute and it is decided not to hire them in part because of the report, this second letter is sent. |

- An attorney must always be consulted to set up this program: errors often end in class actions.
- iReviewNow is a proprietary system that reduces these risks. The candidate reviews their report, disputes errors, and comments at the same time the employer sees it.

#### 4.7.1 Drug and Alcohol Screening

- U.S. federal law requires mandatory testing in some positions.
- Where it is not mandatory, the risk assessment from step 2 helps set the level.
- Every organization that tests must have a written policy with three points: when tests are done, how they are done, and what consequences the result has.
- The policy is communicated to all candidates and employees.
- After the legalization of marijuana in some countries and U.S. states (first medical and then recreational), many employers stopped testing for it.
- In safety-sensitive positions, such as truck drivers or pilots, candidates and employees continue to be tested. They may be subject to U.S. or other countries' federal laws that require it (Gates 2019).
- Where it is legal, the employer must understand the legal, contractual, and cultural issues.
- In some jurisdictions one cannot discriminate against someone who tests positive for marijuana, unless not doing so would lose benefits under a higher law.
- One must be transparent with staff about what is expected, in preemployment and in later tests (Chapa 2015).

#### 4.7.2 Testing

The goal of the tools used before hiring is to decide with information.

| Test | What it measures or what it is like | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Personality tests | Basic traits: attitudes, emotional stability, motivations, interpersonal skills, and interests. | They serve to see whether the person fits the position. Example: someone shy or introverted may not fit in sales. |
| Integrity or honesty tests | The tendency toward undesirable behaviors: lying, stealing, and abusing drugs or alcohol. | There are two types. Overt or direct: explicit questions about past dishonest acts. Personality-oriented or indirect: they measure traits such as reliability and respect for authority. |
| Biographical questionnaires | Open-ended questions that lead the candidate to reveal data and allow follow-up questions to be prepared. | Example from the book: "How many times have you hit a coworker out of anger in the last 24 months?". The book says they are objective. |
| Intelligence tests | Logic problems, verbal problems, pattern identification, and other questions. | They are used because intelligence predicts performance well. |
| Aptitude tests | Cognitive ability in an area: memory, vocabulary, or numerical perception. | — |
| Medical screening | Only if it is directly related to a requirement of the position. | If it reveals an illness or disability that does not affect the position, employment cannot be denied. |
| Physical ability tests | Strength, endurance, or range of movement. | They say whether the person will be able to perform the essential functions without risk to themselves or others. |
| Polygraph | Before the Employee Polygraph Protection Act of 1988 it was used to verify backgrounds in the U.S. | That law prohibits the preemployment polygraph. Some government agencies and local, state, and federal police still use it. Its validity is questioned, especially as evidence at trial. |
| Job-specific tests | The skills and knowledge they will use on the job. | Examples: a writing test for someone who will write reports, or a knowledge assessment for a subject matter expert. |

Rules for tests:

- Psychological ones are done in writing or with specialized software. It is essential to administer, score, and interpret them properly.
- Their results are just one more factor. Misused, they can harm the organization. It is advisable to consult an attorney before starting.
- Intelligence and aptitude ones are useful if properly administered and scored, but they are not infallible. They are one more factor and are discussed beforehand with an attorney.
- The medical exam is delicate because of how personal the tests are and the legal risk.
- Medical and physical ones, and the program that directs them, must be reviewed and endorsed by attorneys before being implemented. The reason is the risk of lawsuits for discrimination or disparate impact.
- That is why review of medical history is not part of a typical background investigation.
- In the U.S. these tests may count as a medical exam. In some states they are only applied after the job is offered.

Exam note:

- The polygraph appears in the lists of tests (4.1 and 4.7), but the text itself says the 1988 law prohibits it in preemployment in the U.S.
- The biographical questionnaire is described with "open-ended questions", but the example is a closed counting question and the text calls it "objective".
- In 4.1 the investigation can include "medical history". In 4.7.2 it is said that medical review is not part of a typical investigation.

### 4.8 Investigation Procedures

Once the policy is approved, it must be decided who investigates. The three most common resources are:

1. The security or asset protection department.
2. The HR department.
3. External consumer reporting and investigative agencies.

- They are not mutually exclusive: many organizations split the parts of the investigation among them.
- What weighs most in choosing is the size and structure of the organization.
- Available staff, experience, efficiency, and cost also count, to decide what is done in-house and what outside.

#### 4.8.1 Using the Security or Assets Protection Department

It needs three resources:

1. Experienced and well-trained investigators.
2. Sufficient reference material.
3. Adequate communications.

- Administrative staff can do database searches and gather employment, education, and reference data.
- But the investigation and the analysis of results must be done by an experienced investigator.

#### 4.8.2 Using the Human Resources Department

- If it has enough staff, HR can have an important role.
- It knows labor law and interview techniques.
- It is ideal for the first filter, for example the first calls to references to see whether the candidate has the necessary experience.
- That first filter usually decides whether the candidate continues. If they continue, the rest is done by an experienced investigator.

#### 4.8.3 Using a Contract Consumer Reporting and Investigative Agency

The number of screening firms has grown enormously. To choose, the book proposes 16 questions:

| No. | Question |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Does it give information to the client and to those investigated in compliance with the law (for example the FCRA in the U.S. and PIPEDA in Canada)? |
| 2 | Can it guide the client on regulation? |
| 3 | Is the information the most current and accurate available? |
| 4 | Does it offer all the services the client needs, or will more than one provider be needed? |
| 5 | Does it have convenient ways to place orders? |
| 6 | How long does it take to deliver the information? |
| 7 | How much does it charge and how does that compare with the competition? |
| 8 | What does it do to confirm the candidate's true identity? |
| 9 | What quality controls does it use to ensure accuracy? |
| 10 | Does it have errors and omissions insurance or other applicable coverage? |
| 11 | Does it have adequate procedures to protect the security and confidentiality of the information? |
| 12 | Has it provided similar services to organizations in the client's sector? |
| 13 | Will it give references of clients who can be contacted? |
| 14 | What satisfaction guarantees does it offer? |
| 15 | Does it provide material or updates on legal and practical screening issues? |
| 16 | Will it provide a sample report? |

#### 4.8.4 Data Analysis

- Analysis begins when all the results arrive.
- In the best case, everything about the candidate is confirmed and there is nothing negative. If something negative turns up, the decision gets complicated.
- The security manager consults the written policy on which prior crimes disqualify.
- The local privacy law can have an influence.
- In the U.S., the employer must communicate negative findings to the candidate and let them correct false or misattributed data.
- The application must say that material omissions and misstatements are grounds for ending the process when discovered.
- That way the process can be ended for the candidate's dishonesty and not only for the negative data.

### 4.9 Future of Background Investigations and Preemployment Screening

- Privacy laws will greatly shape the future of these investigations.
- Many countries debate how to collect, store, and transfer data securely, balancing privacy and human rights.
- Technology speeds up verification and makes investigating cheaper worldwide.
- Many online databases respond almost instantly. But they can have major limitations and unconfirmed data, so they are only used as a complement.
- Technology also improves how information is gathered and shared within the company. It simplifies a slow and tedious process, and makes it more efficient and secure.
- Preemployment screening will remain essential in every good security program.
- Role of the security professional: screening can prevent employee theft, workplace violence, and other incidents from the start.

### Key terms

## Capítulo 5 — Interviewing (págs. 175–207)

**Central idea:** The interview is the main tool the private and corporate investigator uses to obtain information. The chapter covers preparation, documentation, interviewee factors, how to read verbal and nonverbal behavior, the confrontational interview (Reid), and legal limits. Every interview must withstand judicial review.

### Introduction (unnumbered) — Context

- Purpose of an investigation: to reach, objectively, a reasonable conclusion based on the facts.
- **The "three I's"** of the investigator: **information, interrogation, instrumentation**.
- The chapter deals with information obtained by interview and interrogation. It focuses on the interview, because interrogation is associated more with the police than with private investigation.
- The people involved are the most available and valuable sources of information.
- **Interview:** a question-and-answer session designed to obtain information.
- Interviews of victims, witnesses, informants, and confidential informants are the most critical aspect, because they are a primary source.
- The value of each interview depends on three things:
  1. The investigator's knowledge.
  2. Their skill in eliciting information.
  3. Their ability to evaluate the truthfulness of what is obtained.
- The investigator's most valuable talent is obtaining information by asking. That way the case is closed successfully, at low cost, and on time.
- Interviews are always conducted in accordance with statutes, regulations, and standards of fairness.
- Fields:

| Field | What it investigates |
| --- | --- |
| Private investigator | Domestic cases, parental abductions, traffic accidents |
| Corporate | Corporate fraud and similar misconduct. Limited to matters that can harm staff, financial stability, or the company's reputation. The information serves to improve loss prevention and gather intelligence |
| Public sector | Violations of the law |

- Possible locations: a private residence, a street corner, or a formal interview room.
- Possible purposes:
  - To learn a candidate's background.
  - To obtain information about someone indirectly involved.
  - To obtain a witness's written statement.
- The quality of the interview is crucial for closing the investigation and for any later litigation.

### 5.1 Definitions

| Source | Nonaccusatory term | Accusatory term |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ASIS Investigations standard (ASIS International 2015) | **Interview:** conversational exchange to gather information that reveals the facts and the truth about what is being investigated | — |
| Common usage among investigators | Interview: process of gathering information | **Interrogation:** questioning an accused or suspected person |
| Deming 1997, *Interviewing Techniques* | **Informational interview:** nonaccusatory | **Confrontational interview:** may be accusatory; in general, highly structured |
| Ferraro 2015, *Investigative Interviewing: Psychology, Method and Practice* | **Administrative interview:** simple and normally unstructured. Applied to witnesses, friends, supervisors, and anyone who is not the subject or suspect | **Investigative interview:** structured. Used when there are reasons to believe the subject committed the offense, alone or with others |
| Inbau 2013, *Essentials of the Reid Technique* | **Interview:** free, nonaccusatory meeting or chat to gather information | **Interrogation:** accusatory interaction with a suspect, in a controlled environment, to persuade them to tell the truth |
| This chapter | **General interview** | **Confrontational interview** |

- Deming's terms are recommended for the corporate and private setting. This distinguishes the corporate security professional's approach from the police approach.
- The key difference between interview and interrogation is **the person interviewed**.
- The three nonaccusatory variants (interview, informational, administrative) are equivalent: free conversation to gather information about what is being investigated.
- The three accusatory variants (interrogation, confrontational, investigative) are also equivalent: controlled environment and persuasion for the suspect to tell the truth.

**Definitions used in the chapter:**

- **General interview:** nonaccusatory questions to gather information about what is being investigated. **All** people are interviewed: victims, witnesses, and subjects.
- **Confrontational interview:** confrontational interrogation of someone suspected of committing a crime or a serious violation of corporate policy. It is reserved for people whose involvement is **reasonably certain**.
- **Subject:** person believed to have committed the offense. Based on accumulated evidence, proximity to the incident, motive, access to restricted areas, and witness statements. The book leaves this list open. They are often called *suspect*, but investigators prefer *subject*.
- **Witness:** any person, other than the subject, with information about the incident.
  - May not have seen the incident directly, but know pertinent facts.
  - May be a one-time informant who wants to help for moral reasons.
- **Caution:** **anonymous informants** are the most likely to give false or biased information.
- The informant's safety is a priority.

**Purpose and objectives:**

- At its most basic, the interview helps discover the truth.
- Questions must be objective, factual, and focused on obtaining evidence.
- John E. Reid and Associates, Inc., a firm that teaches interviewing and interrogation, points out **at least four objectives**:
  1. Obtain valuable facts.
  2. Eliminate the innocent.
  3. Identify the guilty.
  4. Obtain a confession.

**Figure 5.1 — Differences between general and confrontational interview** (the differences change how the interviewer prepares):

| General interview | Confrontational interview |
| --- | --- |
| Nonaccusatory | Accusatory |
| Dialogue | Monologue |
| Variable setting | Controlled setting |
| Private or semiprivate | Absolute privacy |
| Guilt unknown | Guilt indicated |
| No rights warning\* | Rights warning\* |
| Not long | Long |
| Cooperative | Hostile |
| Unstructured | Structured |
| Some planning | Extensive planning |
| Note taking is acceptable | Note taking inhibits |

\*Varies by country.

Note: in the extracted text "Hostile", "Cooperative", and the heading of the confrontational column came out with corrupted characters. They were reconstructed from the pattern of the source. Pending: confirm in the original.

Exam note: Figure 5.1 says the general interview is "not long". But in 5.3.2 the book says informational interviews of possible perpetrators can last several hours.

### 5.2 Psychosocial Aspects

- The interview is a human interaction. Each party brings emotions, experiences, and desires.
- The goal is to obtain truthful information. To do so, the interviewer must recognize the interviewee's emotions and handle them with dignity and sensitivity.

#### 5.2.1 Behavioral Science

**Human needs according to Maslow (Maslow 1987):**

| Need | Book's examples |
| --- | --- |
| Physiological/survival | Food, drink, health |
| Safety: physical and emotional needs | Clothing, shelter, protection from attack |
| Affection | Belonging to a family or other small group |
| Esteem | Self-respect and achievements recognized and appreciated by others |
| Self-fulfillment | Using one's potential to the fullest, working with and for others |

- When a primary need is satisfied, it stops driving behavior. The next higher-order one replaces it.
- These needs are also present in the interview.
- If the interviewer understands the interviewee's needs and connects with them, communication is better.
- A successful interviewer satisfies as many of the interviewee's needs as they can.

#### 5.2.2 Motivation

- All behavior is motivated and seeks a goal. The force that drives it is satisfying a need.
- Behavior always goes **toward a perceived reward** or **away from a perceived punishment**.

**Theft triangle:** historical explanation of why people steal.

| Side | What it means |
| --- | --- |
| Need or want (desire) | The person needs or wants the item |
| Rationalization (motive) | They can justify to themselves taking the item |
| Opportunity | They have access to the item |

- **Example:** a bookkeeper who works excessive hours and almost never takes vacations. They may be doing something dishonest and not want others to see the records.
- Needing and wanting are not synonyms. Example: someone needs treatment for a serious illness, but wants a big television. They can justify that the pride of having the television outweighs the fear of dying.
- Other factors in rationalization: believing there is little chance of being discovered, or that the punishment will be minimal.
- **Key to confession:** the interviewer helps the subject build a socially acceptable rationalization of what they did.

Exam note: the book labels rationalization as "(motive)". It also treats "needing vs. wanting" as part of the rationalization side. But in its own definition, need and desire form the "desire" side.

#### 5.2.3 Interviewer

- They must look professional, show confidence, and relate well to others.
- If possible, the interview is held where the subject feels comfortable and safe.
- The interviewer's attitude sets the tone. A negative attitude gives negative results.
- Making positive comments about the investigation and the interview conveys professionalism and an expectation of success.
- They must know their own skills and emotions and recognize the interviewee's personal traits.
- They must evaluate the truthfulness of what they hear while formulating new lines of questions.
- **Floating-point strategy:** continually reevaluate the hypothesis as information arrives, and adjust the questions.
- Inexperienced investigators tend to neglect listening when they interview suspects.
- **Active listening** = attentiveness, concentration, acceptance, detachment, and patience.
- They must contrast the facts already established with what the interviewee says.
- **The key to success is gaining the interviewee's cooperation.**
- **The most important factor is establishing good rapport** (a relationship of trust) with the interviewee.
- The good interviewer recognizes personality traits useful for gaining cooperation. They try to understand the interviewee's behavior and satisfy the needs behind it.
- Persevering and listening attentively is critical. They must resist the temptation to think of the next question while the other answers.
- The inexperienced interviewer is usually impatient. Active listening is acquired with patience and a lot of practice.
- They do not take on an adversary role. They are an objective and neutral fact-finder.
- Interviewees who lie usually suffer a lot of stress. They show it with verbal and nonverbal behaviors that the interviewer must know how to evaluate.

### 5.3 Preparation

- The **most critical** preliminary action is to know the case information thoroughly.
- Normally there is time to prepare. Sometimes urgency prevents completing all the steps.

#### 5.3.1 Gathering Information

- Before interviewing, all available historical information must be known. Sometimes there are only a few minutes to mentally review the facts. The available time must be used.
- Ideally the interviewer knows in detail:
  1. Who is going to be interviewed?
  2. What is the interview about?
  3. Under what conditions will it be held?

**Matter Under Investigation:**

- In the initial phase, all the basic information that can be lawfully obtained is gathered.
- Every investigation ends up developing a **theory or theme**. The theory ties together the scattered evidence and explains how the crime was committed.
- The interviewer must understand the current theory of the case and review all the known facts.
- Special attention to **undisclosed facts**, not revealed to employees or the public. Whoever knows them is a witness or a participant in the crime.

**Background Information:**

- Knowing their personal traits makes rapport easier and helps evaluate their truthfulness.
- In a fraud, **motive, ability, and opportunity** are relevant.
- Useful data: age, address, criminal record, work history, education, and hobbies.
- Criminal investigators make a **personality profile**: mental, emotional, and psychological traits.
- Ethnic influence on nonverbal responses is covered later (5.6.4).

**Records Review:**

- Review the records of the organization and of other entities.
- In large companies, investigation and personnel files are usually in databases.
- Example: a product shortage in a warehouse. The records must be searchable by product name, product number, warehouse, and people involved.
- Other investigations, open or closed, about that product, facility, or staff may suggest a line of investigation or an interview method.

| Record | What it provides |
| --- | --- |
| Personnel files (high priority) | Performance evaluations; reports of habitual tardiness, fights, or other unacceptable conduct |
| Payroll files | Financial stability. Garnishments or wage withholdings and child support orders may indicate a need for money |
| Criminal justice files | Previous problems with the police. Access must be ethical and in accordance with laws and policies |

**Discussion with Legal Counsel:**

- The policy of the investigating company or the client usually dictates what is consulted with the attorney.
- Often the client does not know the legal nuances and depends on the investigator.
- Planning is done with the end in mind: possible civil or criminal actions must always be considered.
- **U.S.:** searches must comply with the **Fourth Amendment** of the Constitution. It includes inspection of lockers if there is an expectation of privacy, for example if employees use their own locks.
- In some jurisdictions, investigation reports and statements are subject to **discovery** in civil or criminal proceedings.
- What is done on the attorney's advice may be protected as **attorney work product**.
- **U.S.:** an admission obtained by a private citizen who interrogates in violation of due process, but who otherwise follows constitutional conduct, **may be admissible**.
- When in doubt, consult the attorney.

#### 5.3.2 Planning the Interview

If there is time, these key factors are considered.

**Location:**

- The setting must look fair to an objective outside observer.
- A judge, jury, or arbitrator may weigh the circumstances when deciding whether to admit a statement.
- The interviewee's privacy is a main consideration.
  - Acceptable: a quiet talk in an area away from the scene.
  - Unacceptable: an intense talk with the interviewee in front of a door open to a busy hallway during a shift change.
- A competent opposing attorney will point out any condition that seems unfair.
- The choice of location depends on four factors:
  1. The nature and phase of the investigation.
  2. The interviewee's apparent involvement.
  3. The interviewee's confidentiality.
  4. The level of formality desired.

| Situation | Recommended location | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Witness with no apparent involvement | Office or home | Less tension and more trust; but normal activity may interrupt |
| Some cases | At or near the scene of the incident | — |
| Subject who is not expected to cooperate | Security office or interview room | Controlled setting, with suitable equipment and furniture |
| Need for confidentiality | Discreet place: private residence, public restaurant, or other site unrelated to the incident or to security | — |
| Age or illness of the interviewee | Hospital or nursing home | The physical environment may be outside the interviewer's control |

- Flexibility is required. In every location privacy and confidentiality are maintained.

**Room layout:**

- No unnecessary objects that distract. The subject's office is not ideal: it usually has photos and mementos.
- If there is no dedicated room, a small meeting room is enough.
- Straight chair, with padded seat and back, **without wheels**.
- Avoid plush or reclining armchairs: they invite leaning back. An overly relaxed suspect does not give full attention.
- The subject faces the interviewer. Any witness sits to one side.
- The subject must not see distractions in front, such as a window.
- **Do not interview across a desk or table:** it makes rapport harder and prevents observing all the nonverbal behavior.
- The technique used dictates the space between chairs. In general, **about 5 feet (≈1.5 m)**.
- **Avoid continuous eye contact:** it can be seen as an attempt to dominate.
- The subject must **always** have a clear path to the exit. Otherwise, accusations of **duress or false imprisonment** can be alleged and succeed.
- Interview **one person at a time**, with no one able to overhear. That way one witness does not influence another.
- It is useful to understand current slang, because the interviewee may use it. The interviewer must not use profanity or informal language.

**Time Factors:**

1. Time elapsed since the incident.
2. Time already scheduled for interviews of other investigations.
3. Interviewee's availability.
4. Interviewer's availability.
5. Expected length of the interview.

- Incident: interview **as soon as possible**, because details and nuances are forgotten quickly.
- Routine investigation: do not summon at an inconvenient time for the interviewee. In the company, interview during normal working hours, unless circumstances call for otherwise.
- The general interview is usually short: the information is gathered and the witness statement is quickly prepared.
- Informational interviews of possible perpetrators can last several hours. Even so, they must end in a reasonable time to avoid accusations of **duress**.

**Questioning Order**, from first to last:

1. Those who probably will not be available later. Example: an employee who will be transferred far away.
2. Those believed to have the most pertinent information.
3. Those who are likely to be hostile witnesses.
4. **The perpetrator or perpetrator group goes last.** The theory of the case helps identify them.

- What the nonsuspects say can confirm or refute the subject's guilt before questioning them.

**Questions to Ask:**

- The interviewer must be clear on the key points of the case and the people involved.
- They must also know the **elements of proof** of the alleged offense.
- With those two bases a general format of questions is assembled.
- Using prepared notes or memorizing the questions is a matter of technique.
  - Advantage of notes: they show careful preparation.
  - Disadvantage: they distract if overused.
  - They are acceptable if not consulted constantly.
- The essential thing is to prepare. Asking at random does not give results and shows a lack of professionalism, and the interviewee notices.
- Do not try to write everything down: **only the pertinent facts**.

**Persons Present:**

- Normally there are one or two interviewers and the interviewee. No one confesses before a crowd.
- A second person present (normally another investigator) has two functions:
  - Protect the interviewer from accusations by the interviewee.
  - Corroborate statements against interest and confessions.
- That witness sits to one side of both, at a reasonable distance. The arrangement keeps the interviewee's attention on the interviewer.
- The interviewee must understand everything. If the interviewer does not master their language, a **qualified interpreter** is needed.
- **Different sexes:** a witness of the same gender as the interviewee is advisable. If there is none, the door is left **open**.
- **Confidentiality:** the interviewee sits with their back to the door.

Exam note: here the book asks to write down only the pertinent facts, not everything. In 5.4.2 it asks to briefly note **all** the subject's answers.

### 5.4 Documentation of the Interview

#### 5.4.1 Audio and Video Recording

**Advantages:**

- Confirms what everyone present said.
- Confirms that the interviewee participated voluntarily and prevents accusations of duress.
- With modern cameras, the recording can go unnoticed.
- It is the **most reliable** means of documenting evidence verbatim.
- Video acts as a **neutral third party** in the interview.

**Technical requirements:**

- Test the equipment before each interview.
- It may end up at trial. The audio must be reasonably clear and the video must distinguish the participants.
- In both cases, the voices must be understandable.

**Legal requirements:**

- Record in accordance with the law and in circumstances a court considers fair. Otherwise, the recording can harm the case.
- It is best that the interviewee consents and that it is recorded openly. It helps to explain to them that the recording protects everyone.
- To record without their knowledge, it is **critical to consult the attorney first**. Recording and assuming no one will find out is bad practice.
- If they do not consent and it is not possible to record discreetly, **notes** are taken.

**What the recording must capture, at a minimum:**

1. Interviewee's consent at the start.
2. Date, time, and place.
3. Full identification of the interviewee.
4. Identification of everyone present.
5. Possible uses of the information provided.
6. Who leaves the room and at what time.
7. Who enters the room and at what time.
8. Start time, end time, and reason for each break.
9. Reaffirmation of consent to the interview and to the recording.

#### 5.4.2 Notes

- Notes document the subject's answers.
- To distract as little as possible, all answers are noted briefly, in a kind of shorthand.
- Example: one must stay calm and write down a phrase such as "suppose I am guilty".
- Look at the subject while they answer and then take brief notes.
- **Avoid noting opinions or general impressions** about the subject.
- A skilled interviewer waits a few minutes before writing something down. That way the subject does not notice what caught their attention.
- **The subject must never see what is written.**
- In the question-and-answer phase, verbal and nonverbal answers are noted.

#### 5.4.3 Interview Log

- A log is kept with the **same data** required for the audio recording (the 9 points of 5.4.1).

### 5.5 Subject Factors

#### 5.5.1 Intoxication and Impairment

- Information from someone under the influence of alcohol or another substance is unreliable to some degree. Their statement can be thrown out at trial.
- They must be asked about alcohol consumed in the **last 24 hours** and about any medication.
- The amount of alcohol and the name and dose of the medication are noted.
- Each case is assessed separately.
  - It may be impossible to interview a homeless alcoholic sober, but they may be the best witness available.
  - Someone very active, with dilated pupils, who has gone days without eating or sleeping may be on **amphetamines**. They will be, at most, unreliable.
- The usefulness of the information is weighed according to the interviewee's state.

#### 5.5.2 Physical and Psychological Condition

- It greatly affects the reliability and admissibility of the information.
- **Stockholm syndrome:** hostages form an emotional bond with their captors. That makes their interview doubtful.
- After a physically exhausting situation: interview **briefly on the crucial points** and then let them rest.
- Psychological effects may not be evident, but they weaken just the same. Stress colors the answers, which may be inaccurate.
- They may persist a long time, even subliminally. Asking about something distant may provoke an unexpected emotional reaction.
- Do not subject someone who is weakened or stressed to long interviews. Their attorney can later use the duration and their state to throw out the statement.
- Ask the interviewee to confirm that no physical or mental condition prevents the interview.

#### 5.5.3 Prejudice

**Of the interviewee:**

- Their prejudice toward a person or group contaminates the interview.
- They may answer with generalities about the traits they attribute to the group, for or against.
- Those answers serve to evaluate them, but rarely provide concrete facts of the case.
- The interviewer must make sure the answers refer to the case and the specific people.

**Of the interviewer:**

- Their prejudice damages the whole process. It can leave the interview without results and harm future interviews and the whole investigation.
- They must suppress it and remain neutral.
- They commit the **tort of slander** if, knowingly, they say something false and harmful about the subject.
- If they especially dislike the person or group, it is better for another investigator to interview.

#### 5.5.4 Perception

- During the interview, the interviewee's physical and mental perception is established.
- **Physical:** whether they could see the events or documents. Crucial factors:
  - Corrected visual acuity.
  - Available light.
  - Distance to the event or document.
- **Mental:** equally important.
  - Example: someone in finance immediately recognizes a financial document, but struggles with a detailed map of the facility.
  - A maintenance worker understands the map, but does not see the importance of the financial document.

#### 5.5.5 Age

- Age can greatly affect the interview.
- **Children:**
  - In the corporate setting, children are rarely interviewed. In some areas of private investigation it is common.
  - They tend to be shy and distrust strangers; they are taught to be cautious.
  - Sitting them on a parent's lap gives them physical security and eases their cooperation.
- **Older people:**
  - They must be interviewed in all areas of investigation.
  - They may ramble or enjoy the interviewer's company.
  - They may pay extraordinary attention to detail.
  - A few extra minutes of friendly chat can yield unexpected cooperation and detailed information.

### 5.6 Conducting the Interview

#### 5.6.1 Opening Procedures

| Step | Content |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Identify the interviewee | Name, address, date and place of birth and, if applicable, position and workplace |
| 2. Identify the interviewer | Name, position and workplace, and the name and role of anyone else present |
| 3. Clarify the subject | It is best to ask the interviewee what they understand to be the reason for the interview. Their words or nuances may reveal something about the case. Their misunderstandings are corrected. Details are not needed yet; they are explained later |
| 4. Establish rapport | See below |

**Rapport:**

- Merriam-Webster (2021) defines it as a relationship of agreement, mutual understanding or empathy that makes communication possible or easy.
- The interviewer's role is to obtain information. For that, clear communication and good rapport are needed.
- What damages rapport and provokes a defensive response:
  - A haughty or superior attitude.
  - Displaying authority.
  - Standing in front of a seated interviewee.
  - Pointing at them with an accusing finger.
- If the interviewee attacks verbally, the interviewer stays controlled, understanding and nonjudgmental.
- Technique: when introducing the others present, invite them to spend a few minutes with the interviewee. This tends to provoke an affirmative response and makes cooperation easier.
- The interviewee should show **signs of congruence** that indicate a positive attitude.
- **Hidden persuaders:** starting a general conversation before the direct questions.
  - The interviewee is usually tense; their image of an interview comes from detective shows.
  - Talking about pets, gardening or sports predisposes them favorably.
  - That conversation gives clues about their personality that are useful later.
  - Objective: to get to know them and present oneself as someone helpful and understanding.
- Maintaining eye contact favors rapport.

Exam note: in 5.3.2 the book says to avoid continuous eye contact because it appears dominant. Here it says that maintaining it favors rapport.

#### 5.6.2 Investigative Questions

- Interviewing a witness, a record custodian or another person not directly involved is usually straightforward. Both are identified, the investigation is briefly explained and the information is gathered.
- Start with **broad, general questions** and then move on to more **specific** ones.
- Even with a case theory and a question format, a broad answer may suggest other questions. Any answer may correct the theory or the format.
- **First question:** normally the interviewee is asked to relate the facts.
  - Do not interrupt or guide during this phase.
  - If they stray too far from the subject, they are redirected.
  - The interviewer takes mental notes of points to develop.
  - When the account ends, the interviewer asks about the relevant points and moves on to narrower questions.

**Types of questions:**

| Type | What it is | When |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Closed | Specific, with few possible answers: "yes", "no" or other short answers | At the start, to put the interviewee at ease |
| Open-ended | Seek a narrative answer. Built with who, what, when, where, why and how. Example: asking them to explain how an account is verified at the end of the day | As the interview progresses |
| Leading — **avoid** | Suggest the answer or contain the information the examiner wants confirmed | Never |

- Questions as simple as possible, depending on the case and the interviewee's involvement.
- The interviewee must understand each question precisely. If in doubt, rephrase it.
- Each answer must be complete before the next question.
- Several open-ended questions at once produce mixed answers that then have to be clarified.

Exam note: the book says to start with broad questions and ask for an account of the facts (open-ended). Then it says the start should have closed questions.

#### 5.6.3 Verbal and Nonverbal Behavior

- Throughout the process, verbal and nonverbal responses are observed.
- Especially when the subject answers about the details of the case, their alibi and their relationship with the victim.
- The book says this matters especially "in the primary phase". Pending: the book does not clarify which phase that is.
- Two things are analyzed and compared: the evidence or factual information, and the behavior of the person giving it.
- Every investigator who asks about procedures, sequences or motives evaluates behavior and draws conclusions about truth or deception.

#### 5.6.4 Types of Responses

| Type | Includes | Mechanism |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Verbal** | Spoken words. Also gestures that replace words: nodding (yes) or shaking the head (no). Also vocal traits: tone, speed, pitch and clarity. The good listener also considers timing and emphasis | A socialized person does not like to lie. Deception creates a conflict that generates anxiety and stress. An evasive response or objection to a direct question seeks to avoid the anxiety of openly denying |
| **Nonverbal** | Body movements, changes of position, gestures, facial expressions and eye contact | It is internally motivated to reduce anxiety |

- Nonverbal ways of relieving anxiety after a deceptive answer:
  - **Distraction:** changes of posture, bringing a hand to the face, crossing the arms.
  - **Displacement behavior:** picking lint off clothing, pacing, rapid repetitive movements.

**Preliminary Cautions:**

1. No isolated word or behavior automatically means that someone is lying or telling the truth. Each behavior is assessed in its context, according to the intensity of the environment and compared with the subject's normal behavior.
2. The evaluation is based on the **overall pattern** of behavior, not on a single observation.
3. **Timing** and **consistency** are evaluated. To be reliable, the changes must occur right after the question or at the same time as the answer.
4. The subject's intelligence, sense of social responsibility and maturity are considered.
   - The most reliable behaviors are shown by socially responsible people, those with more at stake (family, employment, reputation), or more mature ones.
   - Caution is needed with emotionally or psychologically unstable people.
5. A truthful person who feels fear, anxiety, anger or distrust may show behaviors that suggest deception.

**Verbal responses — truthful vs. deceptive:**

| Aspect | Truthful person | Deceptive person |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Form | Direct, frank, spontaneous and sincere, especially in response to simple and clear questions | Takes time to answer or repeats the question to gain time and make something up ("Let me see now"). Some answer too quickly, before the question is finished |
| Reflection | Does not need to think about the answer. The good interviewer uses **silence** | Pretends to think seriously to remember what they said before or to camouflage their reactions |
| Consistency | A single answer, substantially the same even if the question is repeated | Evasions. To "did you steal the money from the safe?" answers "I was at home all day" or "I don't even know the combination" |
| Structure | Rational | Irrational; fragmented or incomplete sentences ("It's important that", "If you think", "I hope that you") |
| Memory | Reasonable | Fails under incisive questions, with half-lies ("I don't remember", "as far as I know"). Or else an excessively good or selective memory; the result is usually not very credible |
| Reinforcers | — | Phrases such as "to be totally honest" or "to be frank" |
| Sophisticated liar | — | Plans a "protective verbal layer": "at this moment", "if I remember correctly", "as I understand it", "if my memory serves me", "I may be wrong, but". Looks for an escape hatch without lying openly |
| Explanations | — | Complicated, detailed, with data and broader than the question asks. The pathological liar invents stories even if the truth is simpler |
| Vocabulary | Hard, realistic words: steal, rape, kill, rob, stab | Avoids them to ease their guilt |
| Religion and oaths | — | Religious facade or oaths: "I swear to God", "on a stack of Bibles", "with God as my witness", "on my mother's grave". Using religion as a defense ("I couldn't, I'm \[religion\]") usually indicates a lie |
| Voice | Speaks clearly ("No, I didn't steal the money") | Mumbles or speaks so low it cannot be heard; or speaks fast; erratic changes of tone or pitch. Nervous laughter or a joke to camouflage |
| Details | — | Argues over minimal details ("they say it was at 1:30 and I got back at 1:45"). Offers excuses or justifications |
| Denials | **General**: "I didn't steal that $12,000 or whatever; not a penny" | **Very specific**: "I didn't take that $12,437.18" |

**Summary from the book:**

| The truthful person | The deceptive person |
| --- | --- |
| Denies in a general and broad way | Denies in a very specific way |
| Gives unqualified, direct and spontaneous answers | Avoids realistic words |
| Shows a reasonable memory | Makes seemingly irrelevant comments |
| Answers rationally and with a clear voice | Gives delayed, evasive or vague answers |
| — | Shows an unusually poor, selective or remarkable memory |
| — | Qualifies their answers or uses religion or oaths to support them |
| — | Speaks irrationally, in a fragmented, mumbled or muted way |

**Nonverbal Responses:**

- Much of the meaning of a message is nonverbal.
- Words are thought out and consciously controlled. Movements and gestures almost never are.
- The true meaning is seen by combining verbal and nonverbal content.
- **Example:** the subject is asked about embezzlement.
  - Sincere message: answers "absolutely not, I had nothing to do with it" in a firm voice, leaning forward, with an open posture and sustained eye contact.
  - Insincere message, with the same words: tension-relieving gestures, weak voice, shifts position in the chair, looks at the floor, crosses arms and legs and leans back.
  - Most people would notice the difference even if they could not explain it.

**Activities Suggesting Deception:**

| Type | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Significant change of posture | Rapid, abrupt movements of the trunk or legs; even leaving the room during the questions |
| Grooming gestures | Rubbing or wringing the hands; stroking the back of the neck; touching nose, earlobes or lips; biting or tearing at the nails; dragging, tapping, swinging or arching the feet; readjusting clothing or jewelry; brushing, removing lint or pulling threads from clothing; adjusting or cleaning glasses; smoothing or stroking the hair |
| Supportive gestures | Covering the mouth or eyes while speaking; crossing arms or legs; hiding hands or feet; holding the forehead with the hand; placing the hands under or between the legs |

- If the suspect **repeatedly** combines these reactions with their verbal responses, it is a strong indication that they are not telling the truth.

**Comparative Postures:**

| Truthful person | Deceptive person |
| --- | --- |
| Sits upright, but not rigid | Slouches or leans back in the chair |
| Faces the interrogator | Sits stiffly and unnaturally |
| Leans toward the interrogator when emphasizing something | Sits sideways, not facing the interrogator |
| Appears relaxed and natural | Keeps elbows against the body, arms crossed and locked in front, legs crossed |
| — | Changes posture quickly and erratically |

**Eye Contact:**

- **Deceptive:** does not look directly; looks at the floor, to one side or at the ceiling. This relieves their anxiety and makes it easier to lie.
  - Avoids the gaze with compensatory movements, **or** overreacts by staring defiantly.
- **Truthful:** is not defensive and holds the gaze effortlessly.
  - Speaks fluently and gets to the point.
  - Does not move excessively or fidget while answering.
  - Is relaxed and confident even if apprehensive.
  - Is not worried about whether they are believed. Is attentive and their naturalness is genuine.
- **Evidence is the cornerstone of every investigative decision.** Knowing how to obtain complete information and evaluate its truthfulness guides the investigation. Someone who knows and analyzes these behaviors is better able to tell fact from fiction.

**Influence of Culture:**

- The culture of the interviewer and that of the interviewee must be considered. What is normal in one culture may offend in another.
- Respecting the interviewee's culture facilitates rapport. Properly understood, these differences can be used to the interviewer's advantage.

| Behavior | Reading according to culture |
| --- | --- |
| Eye contact | In the West it indicates sincerity. In Asian cultures, looking a superior in the eye is discourteous. Avoiding it may seem insincere but be courtesy |
| Hand gestures | In some cultures they are commonly used for emphasis. A demonstrative person uses them a lot; a less demonstrative one avoids them. This does **not** equal truth or lies (prevarication) |

#### 5.6.5 Behavior Analysis Interview

- The **Behavior Analysis Interview (BAI)** was developed by **John E. Reid**, founder of John E. Reid & Associates, Inc.
- It is a systematic evaluation of the subject's verbal and nonverbal behavior. It is done in a **structured** interview with two types of questions:
  - **Investigative:** who, what, when, where, why and how (already covered in 5.6.2).
  - **Behavior-provoking:** seek responses or behaviors that distinguish the truthful from the liar.
- Theoretical models of the expected differences were developed, then tested and statistically validated.
- There are **more than 30** behavior-provoking questions (Horvath 2005). The book presents 5.
- Context of the example: theft of credit cards in a bank's mailroom.

| Question | Idea of the question | Truthful response | Deceptive response |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Purpose** | What do you understand to be the purpose of this interview? | Describes the facts accurately. Uses direct words such as "steal". May mention how many cards or the victims' names | Vague, imprecise description. Non-descriptive language ("an incident", "something happened") or qualifiers ("apparently", "it seems", "maybe"). Gives no number of cards or names |
| **"You"** | Cards have gone missing; if you had anything to do with it, tell me now. A novice does not dare to ask it, but it is usually the shortest path to the perpetrator | Brief, direct and unequivocal denial ("No, I didn't steal any cards"). Broad and total language ("Absolutely not! I haven't stolen anything from here") | Longer, unemotional denial. Evasive ("I don't know anything about this"), "I didn't even know cards were missing", or objection: "why would I risk my job?" |
| **Knowledge** | Do you know for certain who stole any of the cards? | Usually offers suspicions ("not for certain, but I have ideas"). May show concern or anger ("I wish I knew") | Brief, unemotional denial ("No, I don't know"). Offers no ideas or spontaneous feelings |
| **Suspicion** | Whom do you suspect? | Thinks about it carefully. If they suspect someone, they can justify why | "I have no idea", without thinking. May name unlikely suspects, without opportunity or access |
| **Vouch**, also called **"Trust"** | Is there anyone you work with who is above suspicion? | Thinks about it and usually rules out possible suspects | Does not vouch for anyone, so as not to shrink the list of suspects and not to increase their own risk of being discovered |

- Combining these questions with the investigative ones gives more clarity about the subject's truthfulness.
- It allows suspects to be ruled out with more confidence and the process to move on to the confrontational interview of the probable culprit.

Exam note: the text cites "Horvath 2005", but the references list Horvath and Buckley, **2004**.

#### 5.6.6 Confrontational Interview

- It is conducted when all the general interviews have been done and the evidence points to a specific person as the probable perpetrator.

**Reid Nine Steps of Interrogation:**

- Often considered the most effective process for persuading a deceptive subject to tell the truth.
- It is applied only when the investigator is **reasonably certain** of the subject's involvement.

| Step | Name | What the interrogator does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **Positive Confrontation** | Enters with a file summarizing the investigation. After greeting, accuses the suspect. The direct accusation is made only if guilt seems very clear; otherwise it is less direct. Pauses to observe the reaction and repeats the statement. Sets the file aside, sits directly in front of the suspect and moves to an understanding tone |
| 2 | **Theme Development** | Presents a moral justification for the conduct. Blames another person or external circumstances: the perpetrator tends to minimize their responsibility. Examples: the employer paid them little; someone left the card as a temptation; unusual family expenses; desperate circumstances; the influence of a friend; revenge for an argument; drug or alcohol dependence. Minimizing makes it easier to admit. Understanding tone; showing interest in solving the problem breaks the ice. Speaks in a **monologue**: the suspect does not speak until ready to admit |
| 3 | **Handling Denials** | The more times they deny, the harder it is to admit later. Interrupts each "I didn't do it" with a blocking statement. Maintaining the theme weakens the guilty person's denials. Many guilty people go from defense to attack and raise objections. The innocent person does not ask permission to speak: they maintain their innocence immediately, without pleasantries. **The innocent person never gets past this stage** and stays firm |
| 4 | **Overcoming Objections** | Two groups: **trait objections**, such as "I wasn't raised that way" or "you'd have to be stupid to do that"; and **factual objections**, such as "I don't even have the combination", "I didn't even know him", "I don't need the money". They are weak explanations even if partly true. Do not argue or show surprise or irritation. A detached reaction discourages the subject, who feels that the objection failed. Once the objections are overcome, the subject usually withdraws and thinks about the punishment |
| 5 | **Keeping a Subject's Attention** | The guilty person becomes pensive, apathetic and quiet. The most important thing is that they listen to the theme (the psychological justification) and do not think about the punishment, which would reinforce their refusal. The interrogator **moves physically closer**: this recovers their attention. Begins to steer the theme toward the likely alternatives |
| 6 | **Handling a Subject's Passive Mood** | Some cry, which usually expresses remorse. Others adopt a defeated posture: head and shoulders drooping, legs relaxed, vacant gaze. The interrogator intensifies the theme and focuses on the psychological justification. Gestures of sympathy, such as a hand on the shoulder, help them tell the truth |
| 7 | **Presenting an Alternative Question** | Two **incriminating** options about aspects of the act, derived from the theme. Example: "did you plan it or did it happen suddenly?". Either option is an admission. It is based on assuming guilt. Do **not** ask "did you do it or not?": it invites denial. Accepting one option is the **first admission**; it is then turned into an acceptable confession |
| 8 | **Having the Subject Relate Details** | After the answer, a **reinforcement statement** that acknowledges the admission. Objective: a brief oral review of the sequence, with enough detail to corroborate guilt. Help them rationalize and not be embarrassed. No ambiguous questions. Short, clear questions, with short answers. It is premature to say "tell me everything". Open-ended questions without emotionally charged words. Then, detailed questions that the investigation can corroborate. Afterward one can return to the chosen alternative to establish the real purpose and intent |
| 9 | **Converting an Oral Confession** to a written one | After "warning" the subject (Pending: the book does not say about what), the interviewer leaves, supposedly to check something. Returns with a witness, introduced as someone who takes part in the investigation. Repeats the essential details of the confession; the witness asks a few confirming questions. The oral confession is turned into a written one |

Exam note: in step 8 the book asks for short questions with limited answers. On the same page it asks for open-ended questions.

**The four written statement formats (step 9):**

1. Statement written by the subject personally.
2. Statement drafted by the interviewer, read and signed by the subject.
3. Statement taken by the witness and typed, for the subject to read and sign.
4. Statement recorded (audio or video) by the subject.

**Rules for the statement of guilt:**

- It must be legible and understandable to someone who does not know what the subject did. For that reason it includes an account of the facts.
- It uses the vocabulary, grammatical errors, profanity and idioms that the subject would use when speaking.
- Each error, change or crossing-out carries the subject's initials and an "OK" in the margin.
- It must state that they were treated well, that there were no threats or promises and that they made the statement freely.
- When they finish reading, they are asked to "write your name here" while pointing to the line. "Sign here" is avoided, because "sign" sounds like too much legal significance.
- The subject signs each page in front of the interviewer and the witness. Then both sign.

**Requirements for a written statement or confession to be credible and admissible in a US court:**

- It must be given **voluntarily**, be **trustworthy** and include:
  1. Date, time and place where it was prepared.
  2. Full identification of the declarant: full name, date and place of birth and address.
  3. All relevant points from the interview, with emphasis on the **elements of proof** of the crime.
  4. A statement that they are declaring freely, after fair treatment, without payment, threat, coercion or promises.
  5. Consecutive numbering of all pages, each signed by the declarant.
  6. The declarant's initials on each addition, deletion or change.
  7. Signature on the last page by the declarant, the interviewer and a witness.

**After the confession:**

- The written confession **is not the finish line**. It must be verified and supporting evidence obtained for trial.
- Before trial, either attorney may request a formal sworn statement (**deposition**). It seeks to learn how the investigation was conducted, what evidence was obtained and what witnesses and subjects said.

#### 5.6.7 Other Suspect Interviewing Techniques

- The Reid method is common in the US, but there are other methods for obtaining a confession (Stevenson-Cooper 2020).

| Technique | Origin | Characteristics |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **PEACE Model** | Created for UK police; adopted by police forces elsewhere in the world | Acronym for **P**reparation and Planning, **E**ngage and Explain, **A**ccount, **C**losure, **E**valuate. Seeks to **reduce false confessions**. Focuses on fairness, openness, accountability and fact-finding, not just on achieving a confession |
| **WZ Non-Confrontational Method** | Wicklander-Zulawski & Associates | Structured, conversational alternative. Produces fewer denials and objections and eliminates conflict. Begins by gaining credibility with an introductory statement; builds rapport by rationalizing the reasons for the act; and encourages telling the truth |
| **Kinesic Interview** | — | Evaluates truthfulness by the subject's reaction to stress. Often combined with other techniques. Uses hypothetical questions, such as asking their opinion on the punishment the crime would deserve. Verbal and nonverbal responses are analyzed |

#### 5.6.8 False Confessions

- Some subjects confess to crimes they did not commit. These "false confessors" consume time and resources.
- They may have read or seen news reports and press releases. They may even believe they committed the crime.
- **Technique to detect them:** present them with an alternative scenario with false details and misleading photos. Observe whether they corroborate the invented crime.

**Defending against the later accusation of a "false confession":**

1. Let the suspect provide details without guiding them.
2. The true perpetrator may give details that only they know.
3. Do not suggest answers or give details at the start, which a gullible interviewee could repeat.
4. After obtaining the confession, present an invented crime and see whether they confess to it as well.
   - Key: add facts that make the fictitious crime **a little more serious** than the real one.

### 5.7 Closing Procedures

- The closing may not be the last meeting. Another interview may be needed, or the interviewee may be called as a witness.
- Show appreciation for the cooperation.
- Even if they did not cooperate, **do not end on a hostile note**. There are cases solved because someone called later with new information.
- Best closing strategy, in addition to the general conversation:
  - Ask whether they want to add anything.
  - If necessary, ask whether they can be contacted again.

### 5.8 General Legal Aspects

- The interviewer must be well versed in the legal requirements of their jurisdiction.
- **US — Weingarten case** (*NLRB v. Weingarten, Inc.*, 420 U.S. 251, 1975). NLRB is the National Labor Relations Board. It recognizes the interviewee's right to have a **union representative** present at an investigative interview.

#### 5.8.1 Statements and Confessions

- A confession or sworn statement must be **competent, material, relevant and voluntarily obtained**, under legally acceptable circumstances.
- Only then will it be admissible in a later proceeding or hearing.

#### 5.8.2 Promises and Guarantees

- Sometimes something has to be promised. The interviewer must be sure that what is promised can be legally fulfilled.
- Avoid any promise or guarantee, explicit or implied, that:
  1. Is illegal.
  2. Cannot be confirmed by the client or senior management.
  3. Cannot be fulfilled.
- If the interviewee asks for an opinion about the outcome, the investigator cannot and must not guarantee it. Their role is to gather facts.
- Saying that cooperating will lead to a more favorable outcome will **probably invalidate** any confession.

#### 5.8.3 Threats, Intimidation, and Duress

- Any coercion, duress or psychological restraint is unacceptable and can make the confession inadmissible.
- Verbal abuse, threats and intimidation are unprofessional, unethical and unacceptable.
- Either type of abuse invalidates confessions and statements. It may also give rise to civil or criminal action against the interviewer.
- The interviewee must understand that they **can leave** whenever they wish.
- Indicating, expressly or by implication, that they cannot leave opens the door to accusations of **false imprisonment**.

#### 5.8.4 Consent

- The interviewee must consent to the interview and to any statement.
- Consent must be entirely voluntary and free of coercion. Otherwise the statement will be inadmissible.
- They must be **legally competent** to consent. The following cannot consent:
  - A minor.
  - A person under the influence of alcohol or other substances.
- Consent **can be withdrawn at any time**, and the interviewee must know it.

#### 5.8.5 Access to Restrooms and Meals

- Access to the restroom at reasonable intervals and, above all, whenever requested.
- In a long interview, meals are scheduled at appropriate times.
- Breaks for coffee or another drink serve to **change the mood** of the interview.
- In a public area, the environment is not conducive to discussing the subject. Even so, meals and breaks can improve communication.
- To avoid interruptions, before starting the interviewer asks:
  1. Are you feeling unwell in any way?
  2. Are you thirsty?
  3. Do you need to use the restroom?

### 5.9 Conclusion

- One key to the process is knowing how to interview suspects.
- Television series suggest that a clue at the scene leads to the perpetrator. In reality, **most cases are solved with information from interviews** and the later work to corroborate or refute it.
- The good interviewer is **patient, persistent and prepared**.
- Each interview is conducted professionally, with respect for the subject and in compliance with all legal requirements.
- Every interview and interrogation must **withstand the scrutiny of judicial review**.

### Key Terms

three I's (information, interrogation, instrumentation) · interview · interrogation · general interview · confrontational interview · informational interview (Deming) · administrative interview (Ferraro) · investigative interview (Ferraro) · subject · suspect · witness · anonymous informant · confidential informant · Maslow's hierarchy of needs · theft triangle (desire, rationalization, opportunity) · socially acceptable rationalization · floating-point strategy · active listening · rapport · hidden persuaders · congruence · theory/theme of the case · elements of proof · attorney work product · discovery · Fourth Amendment · false imprisonment · duress · statements against interest · interview log · Stockholm syndrome · tort of slander · corrected visual acuity · closed questions · open-ended questions · leading questions · verbal responses · nonverbal responses · displacement behavior · escape hatch · grooming gestures · supportive gestures · Behavior Analysis Interview (BAI) · behavior-provoking questions · purpose / "you" / knowledge / suspicion / vouch ("trust") questions · Reid Nine Steps of Interrogation · positive confrontation · theme development · blocking statement · trait objections · factual objections · alternative question · statement of reinforcement · deposition · PEACE model · WZ Non-Confrontational Method · kinesic interview · false confession · Weingarten rights · consent

## Capítulo 6 — Evidence (págs. 209–252)

**Central idea:** Evidence is what proves or indicates an assertion, and it can be oral, documentary or physical. The security professional must collect, protect, examine and transport it to the standard of criminal and civil trials, even if the case does not go to trial. Two things are non-negotiable: the chain of custody and the safety of personnel at the scene.

### Introduction — General Concepts of Evidence

- **Evidence:** that which provides proof or indication of an assertion.
- Courts constantly accept new types of evidence. They arise from advances in medicine, technology, surveillance techniques, information systems and diagnostic tools.
- **Why it matters in private security:** its interests are broad (criminal, civil, contractual and corporate). In addition, it must find the root cause of vulnerabilities.
- For this reason the security professional needs advanced evidence-collection skills. It is a key capability of every corporate or organizational investigations unit.
- Many units outsource collection and analysis. They use specialized investigation firms, crime labs or consultants, especially for digital evidence.
- **Golden rule:** handle evidence to the criminal and civil standard even if it will not go to litigation. That keeps the option open and lends credibility to any administrative or disciplinary action.
- **There is no universal standard** for evidence handling. Every nation, state, jurisdiction and agency has its own procedures and rules.
- All recognized standards follow a single basic model, which is the one the chapter presents.
- The investigator follows best practices. They also comply with the rules of the jurisdiction, agency or laboratory (public or private) in each case.
- A single mistake can be costly. The evidence may not reach trial if the chain of custody is not preserved or the standards of that jurisdiction are not followed.

#### Basic Forms and Categories

Forms of evidence: oral, documentary and physical.

| Category | Includes | Definition | Example from the book |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Direct evidence | Real and material | First-hand knowledge | A witness sees a driver run a red light and crash into another car |
| Indirect evidence | Circumstantial and hearsay | Well-founded inference. As circumstantial evidence, a fact is deduced from testimony about a series of events | The witness was around the corner. Hears the screech of brakes and the crash, runs over and sees the light change from red to green. Deduces that the driver ran the red light |

#### Use of Evidence After an Incident

- The security professional collects the evidence, or supervises its collection, with care. They do so whether or not the police become involved.
- Evidence serves four purposes: establishing a timeline, resolving the matter, analyzing how the incident occurred and, if necessary, changing security measures to prevent others.
- At trial, the judge decides the competency and materiality of the evidence. Normally this relies on case law.
- In non-criminal cases, the presentation of evidence allows a more open interpretation and more concessions.
- In both criminal and non-criminal cases, the defense is entitled to that information during discovery.
- The investigator must be able to recognize a scene that has been staged to mislead the investigation.

#### Requirements for Presenting Physical Evidence at Trial

1. A clearly demonstrated chain of custody. It is defined as the uninterrupted control of the evidence.
2. Correctly identified evidence.
3. Evidence relevant to the case.

Chain of custody best practices:

- Mark the evidence with the investigator's initials at the time it is collected.
- Limit to a minimum the number of people who handle it.
- Document each transfer. That way the prosecution can show that it is the same evidence that was seized.
- Always apply these practices, even if the case will not go to a court or a hearing.

### 6.1 Oral Evidence

- **Oral evidence:** spoken statement about what a person knows. It can be direct or indirect and come from any source.
- **Hearsay evidence:** it is indirect evidence. It is normally not admitted at trial.
- **Materiality:** the rule most used to measure how much a piece of evidence matters and whether it serves to prove something. It asks about its quality, its substance and its connection with the incident or the case.
- **Res gestae** (Latin, "things done"): exception to the rule against hearsay. What is said naturally, spontaneously and without thinking during an event leaves little room for misunderstanding. For that reason courts give it great credibility.

#### Exceptions to the Hearsay Rule Mentioned in the Book

| Exception | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Dying declaration | Statement by a dying person to a credible witness |
| Statements against self-interest | A person who repeats what another said makes statements that go against their own interest |
| Res gestae | Spontaneous statements made during the event (three categories) |

The three categories of res gestae:

1. Words or phrases that form part of a physical act or explain it.
2. Exclamations so spontaneous that it is impossible for them to be made up (belie concoction).
3. Statements that show someone's state of mind.

Source: the section adapts for security the manual of the National Institute of Justice (NIJ), *Eyewitness Evidence: Trainer's Manual for Law Enforcement* (2003). It follows the Principle / Policy / Procedure structure.

#### 6.1.1 Initial Report and First Response

**Answering the Call**
