- Principle: the call taker obtains complete and accurate information without suggesting answers (non-suggestive).
- The information may include the name or description of the perpetrator.
- They must be alert to signs that cast doubt on the credibility of the caller or the plausibility of the accusation.

**Preliminary Scene Investigation**

- Principle: the scene must be preserved and documented, with the information from witnesses and the physical evidence. The preliminary investigator's methods influence the quantity and accuracy of the information in the entire investigation.
- Policy: obtain, preserve and use the maximum amount of accurate information from the scene.
- Procedure: first secure the scene, calm the victim and attend to the injured. Then, six steps:

| Step | Preliminary investigator's action |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Identify the perpetrators and detain them if they are still at the scene |
| 2 | Determine what type of incident occurred |
| 3 | Broadcast an updated description of the incident, the perpetrators and the vehicles |
| 4 | Verify the identity of the witnesses, because they will have to be contacted again |
| 5 | Separate the witnesses and ask them not to discuss details among themselves. They must not hear the account of others. It is advisable to find out whether they already spoke to each other before separating them |
| 6 | Canvass the area for more witnesses. Some are reluctant to talk or have already left. Nearby employees, contractors, guests or visitors may have seen or heard something |

**Exception in a union environment:** there the employer's investigator must not demand confidentiality from the employees being interviewed. There are four exceptions:

1. When witnesses need protection.
2. When the evidence is in danger of being destroyed.
3. When the testimony is in danger of being fabricated.
4. When a cover up must be prevented.

**Obtaining Information from Witnesses**

- Principle: the way the information is obtained determines its quantity and accuracy.
- Policy: obtain the information from the witnesses, document it accurately and preserve it.
- Procedure (8 steps):

| # | Step | What it is for and detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Establish rapport | The witness feels comfortable and gives more information. It allows learning their communication style: how they tell an everyday event versus how they tell the incident |
| 2 | Ask how they are doing ("How are you doing?") | Reinforces rapport. Alerts to physical or mental states that affect their recall or their account: intoxication, medication, shock |
| 3 | Open-ended questions, supplemented with closed questions; never leading questions | Open-ended: "What can you tell me about the car?". Closed: "What color was the car?". Leading, to be avoided: "Was the car red?". The leading question hints at the answer and can distort perception or memory. Only what the witness knows matters, without influence from what the investigator expects or knows from other sources |
| 4 | Clarify the information with the witness | Ensures it was understood and recorded correctly |
| 5 | Document the information, including the witness's identity | It will be needed for the follow-up interview |
| 6 | Encourage them to make contact again if they remember anything else | Witnesses often remember useful details later. Nothing is trivial. Example: if they remember that the perpetrator drank from a soda can, there may be fingerprints or saliva on it |
| 7 | Ask them to avoid the media and its news about the incident | The media can contaminate their memory. An interview request or an offer of payment may push them to make things up |
| 8 | Ask them not to discuss details with other potential witnesses | Independence among witnesses makes it possible to check whether their account fits the other statements and evidence |

- Summary: the witness's information can corroborate other evidence. That is why it must be documented accurately.

#### 6.1.2 Interviews by the Follow-Up Investigator

**Pre-Interview Preparations and Decisions**

- Principle: preparing the interview increases the witness's cooperation and the interviewer's efficiency.
- Policy: review all the information from the witness and the case, and organize an efficient and effective interview.
- Procedure (6 steps):
  1. Review the available information, such as police reports and scene information. That way the interview is tailored to get the most out of it.
  2. Conduct the interview as soon as the witness is physically and emotionally fit.
  3. Choose a place that is comfortable for the witness and has minimal distractions.
  4. Have key materials ready (notebook, recorder, camera) so as not to interrupt.
  5. Separate the witnesses. Their independent statements serve to corroborate.
  6. Find out what prior contacts the witness has had with the police, with security or with the company. That context helps assess their credibility and reliability, and later to build rapport.

**Conducting the Interview** — principles for cooperating witnesses:

1. Social dynamics: the social dynamics between interviewer and witness.
2. Facilitation of the witness's memory and thinking: helping the witness's memory and reasoning.
3. Communication between the interviewer and witness: the communication between the two.

Exam note: the book's list ends in "and" after the third point, as if a fourth principle were missing. The text does not include it and develops only three. Pending: confirm in the printed book whether a fourth exists.

**Social Dynamics**

- Maintain or regain rapport.
- Encourage the witness to tell the story actively and voluntarily, not to answer passively.
- Rapport helps especially when personal or intimate information is requested.
- Show understanding and interest: ask about their health, put yourself in their place, do not judge and look for common ground.
- Personalize the interview and treat them as an individual:
  - Avoid questions that sound formulaic or artificial. Example to avoid: "Is there anything you can tell me that would further assist this investigation?".
  - Call them by their name.
  - Ask questions that follow from their previous answers.
  - Repeat the concerns they express.
  - Lean forward and look them in the eye.
- Five techniques to get them to provide information without being asked:

| Technique | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| State expectations | The witness may not know what is expected of them, or may expect something else. Tell them clearly that they must provide information on their own |
| Ask open-ended questions | The witness does most of the talking |
| Avoid interruptions | Interrupting takes away their active role and cuts off their memory. Note down the questions and ask them afterward |
| Allow pauses | When they fall silent, the witness organizes their ideas and continues the account |
| In-person interviews | Whenever possible. If not, by teleconference with video and not only audio, to see their reactions in real time |

**Facilitation of the Witness's Memory and Thinking**

- Reduce physical distractions, such as noise or the presence of other people. They can be invited to close their eyes and concentrate on the memory.
- Ask them to mentally reconstruct the incident, for example what they were thinking or feeling at that moment.
- Adjust the questions to what they are describing. If they are thinking about the perpetrator's face, ask about the face and not the license plate. First, an open-ended question about that subject; then, non-suggestive closed questions.

**Communication Between the Interviewer and Witness**

- The interviewer pursues several aims: resolving the incident, enforcing company rules and preparing for possible civil litigation.
- The witness knows the details. For the interview to be productive, both must communicate what they need and what they know.

**Weight and Risks of Oral Evidence**

- Jurors and judges give great weight to oral evidence. The security employee who investigates must master how to obtain it for later proceedings.
- Do not promise anything or offer rewards in exchange for a statement. If discovered, the statement is voided. In addition, the investigator may be accused of witness tampering.

### 6.2 Documentary Evidence

- **Documentary evidence:** information, normally letters, figures or other marks, that is on or in an object. Examples: paper products, rubber stamps, plastic bags and seals.
- If police labs do not examine it, the security professional can turn to private laboratories and experts (see Appendix 6A).
- The questioned document examination is adapted from the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) *Handbook of Forensic Services*, 2019 edition.

#### 6.2.1 Handwriting, Hand Printing, and Signatures

- Analyzing the characteristics of writing sometimes makes it possible to determine the origin or authenticity of a questioned writing.
- Sometimes altered or crossed-out writing can be deciphered, and forged signatures detected.
- **Application to security:** keep images of the anonymous letters and threatening documents that have caused concern. That way the letter in one case can be linked to those in others.

**Exemplars of persons of interest**

- Reproduce the original conditions as closely as possible: text, paper size, writing space, writing instrument and style (cursive or print).
- Give instructions, oral or written, about the text to be written.
- Do not give instructions about spelling, punctuation or layout of the writing.
- In addition, eight rules:
  1. Each sample goes on a separate sheet.
  2. The writer and a witness put their initials and the date on each page.
  3. The writer does not see their earlier samples or the questioned writing.
  4. Ask for many repetitions, until their normal writing emerges.
  5. Ask for samples with the right hand and with the left.
  6. Ask for hand printing in upper and lower case.
  7. Gather enough samples to cover the natural variation in their writing.
  8. Obtain undictated writing: business records, personal letters and canceled checks.

**Types of false signatures**

| Type | How it is done |
| --- | --- |
| Traced | An authentic signature is used as a template or pattern |
| Simulated | An authentic signature is copied or drawn |
| Freehand | The forger writes in their normal handwriting, without trying to imitate the other person's style |

#### 6.2.2 Typewriting

- Today it is uncommon. It may matter if a typewriter or an impact printer was used.
- Also in old or "cold" cases, dormant for years, that are reopened because of new information.
- Sometimes a questioned typewritten text can be linked to the machine that typed it.

#### 6.2.3 Photocopies

- Sometimes they are linked to the machine that made them. Condition: that the samples and the questioned copies date from close to one another.
- Make 10 copies with each of these four methods:
  1. With no document on the glass and the lid down.
  2. With no document on the glass and the lid up.
  3. With the document on the glass and the lid down.
  4. With the document run through the automatic document feeder.
- Note on each page the date, the name of the person collecting it and the conditions.
- Note about the machine: make, model and serial number; toner and components; whether the paper is in sheets or on a roll; and options for color, reduction, enlargement, zoom, mask and trim.

#### 6.2.4 Other Materials and Images

**Graphic Arts and Photographs**

- A printed item can sometimes be linked to a common source or to known printing material: originals, negatives or plates.
- The examination of films, negatives, digital images, paper copies and video recordings makes it possible to:
  - Compare the people in questioned images with those in known images (photos, videos).
  - Compare firearms, vehicles, clothing and other objects that appear.
  - Determine the place, time and date of capture.
  - Determine whether the image is a composite, an alteration or a copy.
- Measurements can be calculated with mathematical formulas or by comparison on site, through photogrammetry. Examples: a person's height and the length of a weapon or tool.
- When collecting or using photos as evidence, it is crucial to check whether the image was altered and to document it. Common programs such as Adobe Photoshop make it possible to falsify them without it being noticeable.

**Paper and Documents**

- Torn edges can sometimes be matched beyond any doubt.
- If there is a watermark, the manufacturer can sometimes be determined.
- The impressions left by writing pressure (indentations) can be examined. Do not rub them with a pencil or write over the evidence.
- Soaked documents can be dried and preserved. Frozen ones can be thawed and preserved.
- The information in burned or charred documents can sometimes be deciphered. Touch them as little as possible and transport them in one of three ways:
  1. In the container in which they were burned.
  2. In a polyester film sleeve.
  3. Between layers of cotton inside a rigid container.
- The earliest date on which a document could have been made can sometimes be derived from watermarks, embossed writing, printing or typewriting.

**Ink**

- The analysis is usually limited to comparing the components of the organic dye.
- Even if two inks have the same formula, it cannot be asserted that they come from the same source and not from another.
- It is not possible to know how long the ink has been on the document.
- Package ink evidence separately from any document or surface with ink marks.

**Other Media**

| Medium | What it may reveal |
| --- | --- |
| Used carbon paper or carbon film ribbon | Sometimes the content of the text |
| Impression from a checkwriter | Sometimes the machine that made it |
| Embossed or stamp impression | Sometimes the instrument that made it |
| Rubber stamp impression | Sometimes the stamp that made it |
| Plastic bags (sandwich or trash bags) | Sometimes the roll or box they came from |

#### 6.2.5 Shipping Documentary Evidence

- Keep it as it was found.
- Do not fold, tear, mark, soil or seal it, or write on it. Do not touch it unnecessarily.
- Prevent it from accidentally receiving writing impressions or other marks.
- It can be discreetly marked in pencil with initials, date and other data.
- Whenever possible, send the original to the laboratory. Photocopies lose detail and complicate the examination.
- Copies are enough for searching reference files. Do not store photocopies in plastic sleeves.
- To send it to the laboratory, package it well and send it by registered mail or by a carrier that tracks the shipment. That preserves the chain of custody.

### 6.3 Physical Evidence

- **Physical evidence:** tangible evidence. Examples: airborne particles, a puddle of liquid, soil or dust, clothing, tools, electronic devices, weapons, vehicles and body fluids. It includes everything that can be touched or detected physically or technologically.

| Category | Definition | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Corpus delicti | Proof that a crime was committed | The corpse in a homicide |
| Associative evidence (also called circumstantial evidence) | Links a suspect to the scene | Fingerprints, DNA |
| Identifying evidence | Associative evidence that establishes who the suspect is | Fingerprints, bite marks, blood at the scene |
| Tracing evidence (not to be confused with trace evidence) | Helps identify and locate the suspect | Credit card receipt, clothing label |
| Trace evidence | Examined by the forensic section of the laboratory | Fibers, paints, dyes, glass and similar materials |
| Impression evidence | Impressions and marks | Firearms, tool marks, bites, footprints |

Exam note: the book calls indirect evidence "circumstantial" (in the introduction) and also associative evidence (in 6.3). Fingerprints and bites appear in more than one category. Distinguish tracing (used to locate the suspect) from trace (microscopic remnants).

#### 6.3.1 Evidence Searches

- Physical evidence is more productive if the search is planned, coordinated and carried out by trained security employees.
- Four ideas they must keep in mind:
  1. The best search options are usually the most difficult and slow.
  2. Physical evidence can never be overdocumented.
  3. The search can be cautious in visible areas, to avoid losing or contaminating evidence, or intensive in hidden areas.
  4. It must be done in time, while the evidence still exists. Example: a bank forgery from 6 years ago may leave no physical evidence, because the law only requires the bank to keep records for 5 years.

**Team Members**

- If possible, the team discusses the search with the personnel involved before arriving.
- It checks that everyone knows the common types of evidence and how to handle them.
- If possible, it assigns roles before arriving. One person may have two or more responsibilities.

| Role | Functions |
| --- | --- |
| Person in charge | Secures the scene; keeps the administrative log; does the initial walkthrough; writes the narrative description; solves problems; makes the final decisions |
| Photographer | Photographs and records the evidence and the scene |
| Sketch preparer | Draws and records the scene |
| Evidence recorder | Acts as custodian of the evidence and records it |
| Scene supervisor | The nine functions below |

Functions of the scene supervisor:

1. Communicates with the appropriate company managers, including legal counsel if needed, to resolve problems that arise in the search.
2. Coordinates agreements between departments when several take part in the search.
3. Obtains the materials and equipment to collect and package evidence.
4. Prepares the paperwork that documents the search.
5. Provides personnel with protective clothing, communications, lighting, shelter, transportation, equipment, food, water, medical assistance and security.
6. In long searches, organizes shifts of two or more teams. The paperwork and responsibility pass from one team to another according to a prior plan.
7. Stays alert to the evidence.
8. Takes extensive notes.
9. Looks after the safety of all personnel.

**Procedures: team tasks at the site, in order**

| # | Task |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Secure and Protect the Scene |
| 2 | Conduct a Preliminary Survey |
| 3 | Evaluate Evidence Possibilities |
| 4 | Produce a Narrative |
| 5 | Photography |
| 6 | Sketches |
| 7 | Physical Evidence Collection |
| 8 | Final Survey |
| 9 | Release |

**1. Secure and Protect the Scene**

- Take control of the scene immediately and assess how well it was protected.
- Consult those who know how it was originally.
- Do not let anyone in without authorization and record who enters and who leaves.
- Appoint one person for the final decisions and problem resolution.
- One or more people take extensive notes.

**2. Conduct a Preliminary Survey** — serves to plan the search:

- Walk through the scene carefully and take preliminary photos.
- Delimit the search area and be ready to expand the initial perimeter.
- Detect the especially problematic areas.
- Organize methods and procedures.
- Choose the narrative technique: written, audio or video.
- Formulate and develop a general theory of the incident.
- Take extensive notes on the scene, the physical and environmental conditions and the movements of personnel.
- Identify and protect transient evidence, that which may soon be lost.
- Estimate what personnel and equipment are needed and assign specific tasks.
- Maintain administrative and emotional control at all times.

**3. Evaluate Evidence Possibilities**

- Attend first to evidence that may be lost. Leave the least transient for last.
- Search first in accessible areas and then in those not in view. Look for hidden objects.
- Check that there are enough collection and packaging materials and equipment.
- Everyone must think about all types of evidence, not only that of their specialty.
- Assess whether the evidence appears to have been moved and whether the scene appears contrived.

**4. Produce a Narrative**

- It is a continuous description of the scene, made methodically.
- Everything that merits attention merits being recorded.
- Photos and sketches complement the narrative; they do not replace it.
- In general, no evidence is collected during the narrative.
- Minimum content: (1) case identifier; (2) date, time and place; (3) weather and lighting; (4) identity and tasks of personnel; (5) condition and position of the evidence.

**5. Photography**

- Photograph the scene as soon as possible.
- Before entering, obtain if possible prior photos, blueprints or maps of the site.
- With a digital camera, take some test photos first to rule out distortion. Distortion comes from an incorrect point of view, inadequate perspective or misleading tones such as shadows.
- Shoot at eye level to show the normal view.
- Follow a progression: overall, medium and close-up views.
- Start with the most fragile areas. Photograph all phases of the investigation, including the findings.
- Photograph the evidence before collecting it.
- Every photo for examination includes a scale. First it is taken without a scale and then with a scale.
- Important evidence is photographed twice:
  1. At medium distance, showing its position relative to other evidence.
  2. Up close, with a scale and filling the frame.
- Photos carry the photographer's name and the date.
- Interior: series of overlapping photos, with a normal lens if possible. Overall views can be made with a wide angle.
- Exterior: locate the scene with a series of overall views that include a landmark.
- Photograph entrances and exits from inside and outside.
- Cover the full 360 degrees. Consider aerial photography.
- Keep a photographic log with all the photos and the description and location of the evidence.

**6. Sketches**

- They serve to record the location of objects, conditions and relative distances and sizes.
- Number them so that they match the numbers in the evidence log.
- Each sketch includes:
  1. Case identifier.
  2. Date, time and place.
  3. Weather and lighting.
  4. Identity and tasks of personnel.
  5. Measurements of rooms, furniture, doors and windows.
  6. Distances between objects, people, bodies, entrances and exits.
  7. Measurements that locate the evidence relative to two fixed elements, such as doors or walls.
  8. Key, legend, compass orientation, scale or scale disclaimer, or a combination of them.

| Comparison | Narrative | Sketch |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Common elements | Case; date, time and place; weather and lighting; personnel and tasks | The same four |
| Own elements | Condition and position of the evidence | Room and furniture measurements; distances; location relative to two fixed points; key, legend, compass and scale |

**7. Physical Evidence Collection**

- It is the most critical moment and the one with the most mistakes. Extreme care must be taken when searching, photographing, recovering, marking, identifying and packaging.
- It is done by an evidence technician or the investigator.
- It is essential to isolate and protect the scene to preserve the evidence.
- **Application to security:** that is why every basic police or security officer course must teach how to secure a scene.
- Choose a search pattern: grid, strip or lane, or spiral.
- Check entrances and exits. Go from the general to the particular. Be alert to any evidence.
- Fingerprints are the most common physical evidence. Collect and preserve them first.
- Use latex or cotton gloves so as not to leave new prints.
- Once recovered, do not touch the evidence more than necessary.
- Seal all packages at the same scene.
- Time and temperature deteriorate organic materials. Measures must be taken to prevent it.
- Obtain known standards, such as fibers from a known carpet.
- Two people must see the evidence in place, during recovery and while it is being marked.
- Properly marking the evidence when seizing and transferring it is what establishes the chain of custody.
- Mark directly on the evidence only when necessary. Before that, try to mark the container.
- The minimum to certify a piece of evidence is the investigator's initials.
- Photograph everything before collecting it and note it in the photographic log.
- Evaluate the scene completely.
- If possible, a single person acts as the custodian of the evidence.
- Indicate on the sketch where each piece of evidence was.
- Constantly review the paperwork, packaging and data for errors.
- Document the chain of custody throughout the process, with all the people who touched the evidence.

**8. Final Survey** — review all aspects of the search:

- Discuss the search with all personnel.
- Check that the documentation is correct and complete.
- Photograph the final condition of the scene.
- Confirm that all the evidence is secured and that all equipment was collected.
- Confirm that no hiding place or hard-to-reach area was overlooked.

**9. Release**

- After the final survey, assess whether specialists such as human resources, labor relations or safety should see the scene.
- Confirm that the evidence was collected according to legal requirements, documented and marked.
- The release document states the time and date, to whom the scene was released and who released it.
- It is released only when all personnel are sure that the search was correct and complete.
- Only the person in charge can release the scene.

#### 6.3.2 Requesting Evidence Examinations

- Every request is in writing, on organization letterhead and addressed to the appropriate laboratory.
- Requirements vary by laboratory. It is usual to include:
  1. Name, organization, address, email and phone number of the sender.
  2. Previous case numbers, previous evidence submissions and communications about the case.
  3. Nature and basic facts of the case related to the examinations.
  4. Names and description of those involved (subject, person of interest or victim).
  5. Case number of the submitting organization.
  6. List of the evidence enclosed or sent separately, with the means of shipment.
  7. Types of examination requested.
  8. Address to which the evidence is to be returned.
  9. Address to which the laboratory report is to be sent.
  10. Notice of whether another expert has already examined the evidence, whether there is local controversy or whether other organizations are interested in the case.
  11. Reasons for requesting an urgent examination, if any.
  12. Separate communications for different cases.

#### 6.3.3 Shipping Evidence

Steps for sending evidence to a laboratory:

1. Before packing and shipping, call the laboratory to ask for specific instructions.
2. Wrap and seal each piece of evidence separately so they are not contaminated.
3. Place it in a clean, dry, unused inner container.
4. Seal the inner container with tamper-evident tape or filament tape.
5. Put the "BIOHAZARD" label on the inner container if applicable. If latent prints are to be searched for, also put the "LATENT" label.
6. Place the sealed inner container in a clean, dry, unused outer container, with clean padding material. Do not use loose foam.
7. Include in each package a copy of the examination request, in an envelope separate from all the evidence.
8. Completely seal the outer container so that any tampering is noticeable.

- In the US, every shipment of hazardous materials complies with the standards of the U.S. Department of Transportation and the International Air Transport Association.
- A trained and qualified evidence technician must help label, package and ship any hazardous material.

**Shipping live ammunition**

1. Package and ship the ammunition separately from the weapons.
2. Label the outside of the container: "ORM-D, Cartridges, Small Arms". ORM-D is a shipping category for regulated material; the book does not explain the acronym.
3. Record on the Declaration of Dangerous Goods the number of packages and the gross weight in grams.

- It is advisable to use courier services with detailed tracking.
- Security personnel note the shipping method and tracking numbers on the chain of custody form.

**Disposal of hazardous materials**

- The Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) is administered by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency.
- It is known as the "cradle-to-grave" rule. It follows chemicals from the time they are generated (cradle) until they are disposed of (grave).
- It imposes obligations on generators and transporters, and on transportation, storage and disposal facilities.
- When a material is declared hazardous, full responsibility lies with the generator.
- Deciding whether a material is hazardous waste falls to qualified personnel.
- Even a new material in its original container can be waste if it no longer has a use.
- Hazardous waste contractors and transporters can help remove materials from the scene.
- Hazardous materials removed from a scene are evidence. The RCRA waste rules do not apply to them.
- But when the case is adjudicated or the material is no longer needed, a qualified contractor who knows the local rules must be sought promptly.
- Examples of scenes where waste may need to be removed: clandestine drug labs and environmental incidents.

#### 6.3.4 Handling Physical Evidence

- In the private sector, almost all investigators call the police to take charge of the evidence in serious cases, such as homicides and terrorist attacks.
- The guidelines below serve for other cases or until the police arrive.
- They are adapted mainly from the FBI *Handbook of Forensic Services* (2019), which covers more types of evidence.
- The book lists 26 types, in this order: weapons and ammunition; audio and video recordings; biological samples and DNA; construction materials; unknown chemicals; computers; controlled substances and drugs; electronic devices; glass; latent prints; lubricants; metals; paint; pepper spray; polymers; product tampering; rope; safe insulation; serial numbers; footwear and tire impressions; soil; tape; tool marks; toxicology; video; and wood.

#### Ammunition and Firearms

**Guns and Gun Parts**

- Examining parts makes it possible to determine the caliber and model of the weapon they came from.
- Before the examination, make sure all weapons are unloaded.
- Touch weapons as little as possible so as not to lose or destroy evidence.
- No object should enter the barrel, chamber or other operating surfaces, or touch them. Never put anything down the barrel.
- When handling a recovered fired bullet, protect the rifling marks.
- Pick up cartridges by the open end with tweezers or another tool. That way partial prints and other evidence on the outside are not destroyed.
- Do not mark the item.
- Identify the parts with a tag indicating caliber, make, model and serial number.
- On the container: date, time, owners' names, place, name of the person collecting it, case number and evidence number.

Exam note: the book says "spare cartridges" and asks that they be picked up by the "open end". Possible typo for "spent" (already fired casings). Exam note: here it says "do not mark the item". By contrast, the general rule asks to mark with initials and Appendix 6B asks to mark each item on the item itself.

**Bullets, Jackets, and Casings**

- Fired bullets reveal general rifling characteristics (caliber and shape of the marks) and the bullet manufacturer.
- Cartridge casings, shotshell casings and wadding reveal caliber or gauge, manufacturer and whether there are marks useful for comparison.
- Images of questioned casings can be scanned into the National Integrated Ballistics Information Network. That way they are compared with evidence from other shootings.
- The microscopic characteristics of bullets and casings indicate whether they were fired from a specific weapon.
- Unfired cartridges reveal caliber or gauge and whether there are useful marks. Also whether they were loaded into a specific weapon and extracted from it.
- Fired and unfired cartridges can be linked by manufacturing marks.
- Package each component (bullets, casings, shotshell casings) separately. On the container: date, time, place, who collects it, case number and evidence number.

**Gunshot Residue on Clothing**

- The residue left on clothing changes according to the distance between the muzzle and the target.
- The pattern is reproduced by firing the same weapon and ammunition combination at test materials at known distances. That way the distance between the muzzle and the garment is estimated.
- Handle the clothing with care, air-dry it and wrap each garment separately in paper.
- If the clothing has blood: air-dry and put "BIOHAZARD" on the inner and outer containers.
- On the container: date, time, place, who collects it, case number and evidence number.

#### Audio and Video Recordings

- Write-protect the original recordings.
- Never use pause when viewing an original video recording.
- Send the original (film or video) whenever possible, because it has the maximum detail. If it does not exist, send first-generation audio, video and photos.
- When requesting forensic examinations of video images:
  - Leave the original cued near the moment of interest.
  - State in writing the date and time of that moment, according to the date and time stamp or the counter.
  - Send as a reference the prints of the relevant frames, if there are any.
  - Develop all the film, including surveillance film, before sending it.
- Labels on the outer container for shipping: "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT" or "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE AUDIO/VIDEO MEDIA", and also "KEEP AWAY FROM MAGNETS OR MAGNETIC FIELDS".

#### Biological Samples and DNA

- DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) is analyzed in body fluids, stains and other biological samples.
- The DNA of questioned samples is compared with that of known samples. That way victims or persons of interest can be linked to each other or to the scene.
- If it is not properly documented, collected, packaged and preserved, the DNA does not meet the legal and scientific requirements for a court to admit it.
- Risks: that its origin is challenged, that it loses biological activity, that it is contaminated, or that it decomposes and deteriorates until it is useless.
- The method of collection, packaging and preservation depends on whether the evidence is liquid or solid and on its condition.
- The more intact it reaches the laboratory, the more useful examinations can be performed.
- In general, these samples should be collected by the police.

**Blood Samples (blood samples from a person)**

- Only qualified medical personnel draw blood from a person.
- For DNA: at least two 5-milliliter purple-top tubes, with EDTA as the anticoagulant. The book does not explain the acronym EDTA.
- For drug or alcohol analysis: gray-top tubes with sodium fluoride.
- Each tube carries the date, time, name of the person, place, who drew it, case number and evidence number.
- Refrigerate without freezing. During shipping, use cold packs, not dry ice.
- Package each tube separately, in foam or in cylindrical tubes, surrounded by absorbent material. Send to the laboratory immediately.
- Outer container labels: "KEEP IN A COOL DRY PLACE", "REFRIGERATE ON ARRIVAL" and "BIOHAZARD".

**Blood on a Person or Surface**

- Liquid blood or clots: absorb them with a clean cotton cloth or a clean swab.
- Dried blood: absorb it with a cotton cloth or swab moistened with distilled water.
- Leave a part of the cloth or swab unstained as a control.
- Air-dry and package in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed. Do not use plastic.
- Blood in snow or water: collect it right away so it does not dilute further. Put it in a clean, airtight container and freeze it.

**Blood Stains**

- Air-dry stained clothing, taking care to preserve the stain pattern.
- Wrap the dry clothing in clean paper.
- Put any debris or residue that falls from the clothing in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed.
- Objects that cannot be moved: cut a large sample with a clean, sharp instrument. Also collect an unstained sample as a control.
- Package in such a way that rubbing during shipping does not erase the stain. Use clean paper. Do not use plastic.

**Saliva, Urine, and Semen**

- They are collected the same way as blood and blood stains.
- Cigarette butts: pick them up with gloves or clean tweezers. Do not send the ash. Air-dry them.
- Gather the butts from the same place (for example, an ashtray) in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed.
- Do not send the ashtray, unless a search for latent prints is requested. In that case, package it separately.
- Chewing gum, envelopes and postage stamps: pick them up with gloves or clean tweezers and put them in a clean envelope. Do not use plastic.

**Oral (Buccal) Swabs**

- Rub the inside of the cheeks with clean cotton swabs.
- Air-dry them and place them in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed.
- They do not need refrigeration. Do not use plastic.

**Hair**

- Pick it up carefully with clean tweezers so as not to damage the root tissue.
- Air-dry it if it is mixed with body fluids.
- Package each group of hairs separately in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed. Do not use plastic.
- Refrigerate and send as soon as possible.

**Tissues, Bones, and Teeth**

- Pick them up with gloves or clean tweezers.
- Tissues: in a clean, airtight plastic container, without formalin or formaldehyde.
- Teeth and bones: in clean paper or in an envelope with the corners sealed.
- Freeze and package with dry ice for overnight shipping.

Exam note: tissues are the only biological sample that goes in plastic. Blood tubes are refrigerated but not frozen, and are sent without dry ice. By contrast, blood in snow or water is frozen, and tissues, bones and teeth are frozen and go with dry ice.

#### Building Materials

- When a building material is drilled or damaged, its debris sticks to people, clothing, tools, bags and vehicles.
- If possible, send the entire piece of evidence so that the laboratory examiners remove the debris.
- Package each piece of evidence in its own paper bag.
- Do not process the tools for latent prints.
- Collect known samples from the drilled or damaged areas for comparison.
- Send the known and the questioned debris separately so they are not contaminated.
- Send the debris in airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles. Do not use paper or glass.
- Package so that the chunks stay whole.

Exam note: objects go in paper bags, but loose debris never goes in paper, only in airtight containers.

#### Chemicals (General Unknowns)

- These are powders, liquids and stains of unknown origin or difficult to classify.
- It may not be possible to identify them fully, but they can almost always be classified in general terms.
- If there is a known sample, an opinion can be given as to whether the unknown substance is consistent with it.
- Send powders and liquids in airtight containers.
- Do not send large stained objects. If possible, send a small sample in a heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag.
- Collect an unstained sample as a control, package it separately and send it along with the stained evidence.
- If the sample cannot be cut, rub the stain with a clean cotton swab, dry or dampened with alcohol.
- Air-dry the swab and package it in a heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag. Send an unused swab as a control.

#### Computers

- Today the examination of hardware, software, data media and peripherals is key in almost every investigation.
- What examiners can do:
  1. Determine what types of files are on the computer.
  2. Compare files with known documents and files.
  3. Determine when and in what order the files were created.
  4. Recover deleted files by means of a bit stream backup.
  5. Convert files to other formats.
  6. Search for words or phrases in the files.
  7. Recover passwords.
  8. Decrypt encrypted files.
  9. Analyze the source code.
- The Computer Analysis Response Team (the FBI's computer analysis team) combines investigation and laboratory support in its forensic examinations, including those involving malware.
- Investigators seize printouts and other copies of data, and mark and control them. That way the forensic team performs a clean examination that does not alter the original media.
- Critical points:
  1. Know the type of computers and operating systems.
  2. If there is a network, know the network software, where the servers are and how many computers there are.
  3. Know whether there is encryption or passwords.
  4. Decide whether the computers and media are seized or examined on site.
  5. In general, send only the central processing units and the internal and external storage media.
- Packaging for shipping:
  - Use a sturdy cardboard box. If possible, the original box with its custom-fitted padding.
  - Protect with large bubble wrap or foam rubber pads.
  - Do not use loose foam pieces: they get into the equipment and create static electricity. That can erase data or damage the boards.
  - Close the box with strong packing tape.
  - Pack and ship the central processing units upright. Label the outside: "THIS END UP".
  - Pack disks, cartridges, tapes and hard drives so they do not move.
  - Label the outside: "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT" and "KEEP AWAY FROM MAGNETS OR MAGNETIC FIELDS".
- Appendix 6B contains a model policy for seizing computer equipment.

Exam note: here it asks that only the central processing unit and the media be sent to the laboratory. Appendix 6B, by contrast, asks that monitors, keyboards, mice, printers and other peripherals also be seized. What is seized is one thing and what is sent is another.

#### Controlled Substances and Pharmaceuticals

- The examination of controlled substances can detect traces of a drug, identify it and quantify it.
- Send each piece of evidence in its own heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag.
- Fold clothing to preserve traces.
- Do not send used field test kits with the evidence.
- The examination of pharmaceuticals can identify their components, active ingredients, quantity and weight.
- When sending them to the laboratory, state the name of the drugs and the information on their use.
- If possible, send them in their original container.

#### Electronic Devices

- Data can be extracted from mobile phones and from "interception of communication" devices (transmitters and receivers for eavesdropping on conversations).
- Their operating characteristics and modifications can also be identified.
- Sometimes they have to be disassembled during the examination.
- Label the outside: "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT" and "KEEP AWAY FROM MAGNETS OR MAGNETIC FIELDS".

#### Glass

- The comparative examination tells whether glass particles came from a specific broken piece.
- The fracture examination tells the direction and type of the force that broke it, and the order of the shots.

**Comparison**

1. Send samples from each window or broken piece in airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles. Do not use paper or glass.
2. Laminated glass (for example, a windshield): samples from each face, labeled "INSIDE" and "OUTSIDE". Package them separately in airtight containers. Do not use paper or glass.
3. Send the relevant clothing air-dried, each garment in its own paper bag.
4. Look for particles in hair, skin and wounds. Send them in airtight containers. Do not use paper or glass.
5. Look for particles in vehicles by vacuuming each area separately. Do not use adhesive tape to collect glass. Send what was vacuumed in airtight containers. Do not use paper or glass.
6. Send the known and the questioned debris separately so they are not contaminated.
7. Do not process the evidence for latent prints.

**Fracture**

1. Label the faces of the glass in the frame ("INSIDE" and "OUTSIDE"). Label which part of the frame it was taken from: "TOP", "BOTTOM", "LEFT" and "RIGHT".
2. Send all the pieces. That way they can be fitted together to see the radial cracks next to and at the impact points, and it is easier to match the edges. Package each piece separately and well secured so that it does not move or break.
3. Laminated glass: send the entire piece if possible, secured between sheets of plywood or sturdy cardboard. Do not place anything over the impact area.
4. Do not process the evidence for latent prints.

#### Latent Fingerprints

- The best place to develop latent prints is the laboratory. Sometimes they must be developed at the scene.
- Care must be taken not to destroy them. Measures to protect them at the scene:
  1. Photograph the prints before any treatment.
  2. Examine all the evidence with the naked eye and with a laser or an alternate light source before using another method.
  3. When using a development method, follow the manufacturer's instructions and safety warnings.
  4. Use personal protective equipment: safety glasses, mask, gloves and lab coat.
  5. Consult the FBI's *Processing Guide for Developing Latent Prints* (2001). It is a complete list of methods and protocols for developing prints.
- Following that guide gives more options for developing prints and preserves the evidence in case other forensic examinations are needed.

#### Lubricants

- They include petroleum derivatives, natural fatty ester oils and polyalkylene glycol oils.
- Automobile fluids (engine oil, brake fluid), some cosmetics (bath oils, lotions) and some polishing products contain lubricants.
- They are examined in cases of sexual assault, vehicular homicide or sabotage of heavy machinery.
- Guidelines:
  1. Send the entire item (for example, the clothing) if possible. Air-dry it and package each in its own paper bag.
  2. Absorb the lubricant with a clean cotton cloth or swab, and leave a part unstained as a control. Air-dry and package in a heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag.
  3. Package the lubricants separately in airtight containers.

#### Metals

| Subtype | What the examination can determine |
| --- | --- |
| Comparison | Whether two metals or metal objects come from the same source or one from the other. Analyzes surface characteristics and microstructure: fractured areas, accidental damage and manufacturing marks. Reveals whether they share an origin, the manufacturing method, the mechanical properties (how the metal responds to a force or load) and the chemical composition (alloy and trace elements) |
| Broken or Mechanically Damaged Metal | The cause of the breakage or damage. The magnitude of the force or load, how it reached the metal and in what direction |
| Specification Frauds and Noncompliant Materials | Whether in a procurement fraud parts were replaced with lower-quality ones. Whether composition and properties meet the contract or the standards. Sometimes, the country of origin. Also the precious metal content |
| Burned, Heated, or Melted Metal | The approximate temperature it reached, the type of heat source and whether the metal was in a short circuit |
| Cut or Severed Metal | The cutting method: sawing, shearing, milling, turning or thermal cutting. Sometimes, the heat source, such as a burner bar or an electric arc welder |
| Metal Fragments | How they were formed. Whether they were formed by an abrupt load (very brief and with very rapid deformation), whether an explosive was detonated and its detonation velocity. Often, also what object produced them |
| Watches, Clocks, and Timers | What caused them to stop or fail. Whether the timer of a device damaged by fire or explosion was on or off |
| Lamp Bulbs | Whether the bulb was lit when its glass broke or when it received a blow, as in a vehicle collision |
| Internal Components | X-ray radiography shows the internal construction without destroying it, and whether there are defects, voids or foreign materials. Also the position of switches and other mechanical parts |

Pending: the book gives no packaging guidelines for metals.

#### Paint

- The layer structure of a questioned paint can be compared with known sources.
- For each layer, the order, relative thickness, color, texture, number of layers and chemical composition are compared.
- From an original factory paint chip, the color, manufacturer, model and year of the automobile can be determined.
- Paint from safes, vaults, window sills and door frames transfers to tools, and vice versa. The paint on an object can be compared with that on a tool.
- In an accident or incident, paint transfers from one car to another, from a car to an object or from an object to a car.
- Collect control chips:
  - Take them near the damaged area, but not from it.
  - If there is no visible damage, take them from several areas of the surface.
  - Each layer is a point of comparison. The control must have all the layers, down to the base surface.
- Three ways to obtain the control:
  1. Cut the paint with a clean, sharp instrument.
  2. Lift or pry up loose chips.
  3. Dislodge the paint by gently tapping the opposite side of the painted surface.
- Package in airtight containers such as vials or pillboxes.
- Do not stick the particles to adhesive tape.
- Do not use plastic bags, cotton or envelopes.

#### Pepper Spray or Foam

- Oleoresin capsicum is a resin from several peppers. It is used in self-defense sprays and foams.
- They may also contain ultraviolet dye or tear gas. They can be analyzed for oleoresin, dye or tear gas.
- Guidelines:
  1. Send the entire item (for example, the clothing) if possible. Air-dry it and package each in its own paper bag.
  2. Moisten a clean cotton cloth or swab with isopropanol (rubbing alcohol) and wipe it over the spray or foam.
  3. Prepare a second moistened cloth or swab as a control.
  4. Air-dry and package each in its own heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag.
  5. Send the spray cans when possible.

#### Polymers

- They are usually pieces of plastic or other artificial materials.
- In general, composition analysis does not reveal their origin, use or manufacturer.
- In hit-and-run cases, the vehicle's moldings can be compared with the plastic left on what was struck.
- If the molding has the manufacturer's part number, the manufacturer, make, model and year of the vehicle are known.
- The plastic of cable insulation and other plastics, such as buttons, can be compared with known sources.
- After an accident, pieces may be left at the scene, such as those from a headlight. If their edges are intact, they can be fitted together with the pieces remaining on the car.
- Packaging:
  - Send the fragments in airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles. Do not use cotton or paper.
  - Package each damaged part of the vehicle separately, in zip-closure plastic bags or in boxes.
  - Package objects (for example, clothing) that may have polymer debris or stains separately, in paper bags and not plastic.

#### Product Tampering

- It is deliberately altering a commercial product to harm someone or to extort.
- Examples: tampering with medications in a medical setting or adulterating food in a supermarket.
- Guidelines:
  1. Send control samples of the unadulterated product.
  2. Package and send each sample separately so they are not contaminated.
  3. Use airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles. Do not use paper or glass.
  4. Take care not to destroy latent prints.

#### Ropes and Cords

- A piece of rope can be compared with a questioned rope.
- Its composition, construction, color and diameter can be determined. If it has a tracer thread, the manufacturer is known.
- Guidelines:
  1. Send the entire rope.
  2. If it has to be cut, indicate which end was cut when collecting it.
  3. Label the known and the questioned samples.
  4. Handle the pieces carefully so as not to contaminate them or lose traces.
  5. Send in plastic or paper bags, heat-sealed or zip-closure.

#### Safe Insulation

- The insulation of a safe can be compared with a known source. Sometimes the manufacturer is determined.
- Collect samples from the damaged areas.
- Insulation sticks to people, clothing, tools, bags and loot, and transfers to vehicles. That is why the evidence must be sent to the laboratory and the examiners remove the debris.
- Package each piece of evidence in its own paper bag.
- Do not process the tools for latent prints.

#### Serial Numbers

- Obliterated serial or identification numbers on metal, wood, plastic or fiberglass can often be recovered.
- If possible, remove the part bearing the number and note where it came from.
- If it cannot be removed, make a cast and send it to the laboratory. Call the laboratory to find out which casting kit to use and how.

#### Shoeprints and Tire Treads

- They often appear at scenes, in two- or three-dimensional form.
- Almost all of them, even partial ones, are useful for a forensic comparison.

**Photographing**

- General photos of the scene, close up and from afar, to place the impressions in it.
- Examination-quality photos, for maximum detail. They are taken directly above the impression, with a tripod and lighting.
- The goal is a detailed negative that can be enlarged to actual size.

| Setting | Value the book asks for |
| --- | --- |
| Camera | Digital single-lens reflex (SLR) camera of 12 megapixels or more, or a 35 mm film body. With tripod and cable or electronic release |
| File format | Uncompressed RAW, RAW with lossless compression or TIFF with lossless compression. JPEG is not accepted |
| Sensitivity | ISO 400, with the right white balance to capture the true color |
| Mode and aperture | Aperture priority or manual mode, to control depth of field. Aperture f/16 or f/22 |
| Lens | Fixed focal length, twice the camera's normal focal length |
| Scale | A linear ruler next to the impression and in the same plane. Plus a label that ties it to the scene notes |
| Tires | A long tape measure as scale. Photos in overlapping sections until a full revolution is covered: 7 to 9 feet (2 to 3 meters) |
| Focus | On the bottom of the impression |
| Flash | Electronic flash at a very low angle, 10 to 15 degrees above the ground, so that raking light enhances the detail |
| Number of photos | Shoeprints: at least 3, moving the light around the entire impression. Tires: 4, lighting from the four sides |
| Files | Copy them to a hard drive, CD or DVD. At least 2 copies marked as original or master, plus copies for viewing, processing and printing |

**Impressions in Snow**

- They are hard to photograph because they have little contrast. It is best to photograph them as if they were in soil.
- To provide contrast, spray them lightly with snow print wax or with colored spray paint.
- Hold the spray at least 2 or 3 feet from the impression, so that the force of the spray does not damage it.
- Apply little paint, at an angle of about 30 to 45 degrees, so that it touches only the high points.
- Painted impressions absorb heat from the sun. They must be shaded until they are photographed and cast, so they do not melt.

**Casting**

- If the evidence itself cannot be sent, it is cast with dental stone and the cast is sent.
- Plaster of Paris, modeling plasters and ordinary dental plasters are not hard enough.

| Step | Guideline |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Keep the pre-measured plaster in zip-closure plastic bags. Mix it with just enough water inside the bag, until it is like pancake batter or thick cream |
| 2 | Do not pour it directly onto the impression, because its weight and volume destroy the detail. Pour it beside it and let it flow in on its own. Fill the impression until it overflows |
| 3 | If it is too thick, vibrate a finger or a stick on the surface so that it flows. Do not sink them more than 1/4 inch, because they damage the impression |
| 4 | Before it fully hardens, write on it the date, the initials of the person collecting it and other data |
| 5 | Leave it undisturbed for at least 20 to 30 minutes in warm weather, and longer in cold. In sand or loose soil it comes out easily; in mud or clay it may require careful digging |
| 6 | Let it air-dry for at least 48 hours. Package it in paper, not in plastic |
| 7 | Only a laboratory examiner cleans the cast |

**Lifting Two-Dimensional Impressions**

- Lifting an impression transfers the residue or dust to a lifting film. That way it can be sent to the laboratory to be photographed and examined.

| Method | Surfaces | When it works and guidelines |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Electrostatic lifts | Porous and nonporous, without damaging the impression | Dry dust impressions or residue on clean surfaces. It does not work if the impression is wet or gets wet. Follow the device's instructions |
| Black gelatin lifters | Porous and nonporous | Light-colored impressions, dry or damp |
| White gelatin lifters | Porous and nonporous | Impressions developed with fingerprint powder, or dark enough to contrast with the white background |
| White adhesive lifters | Only smooth and nonporous | Impressions developed with fingerprint powders |
| Transparent adhesive lifters | Only smooth and nonporous | Impressions developed with black powders |
| Transparent tape (for example, 2-inch fingerprint tape) | Only smooth and nonporous | Impressions with powder, if they are then transferred to a white card |

How to store the electrostatic film:

- It is easily damaged if stored improperly. Its residual charge attracts dust and dirt, and makes it stick to other surfaces.
- Tape one edge inside a clean, smooth paper folder. Or tape the edges inside a flat photographic paper box.
- Do not use low-quality cardboard boxes, such as pizza boxes. The charge attracts their dust and contaminates the impression.
- Objects with a dry residue impression are not wrapped or stored in plastic, because part of the impression would transfer to the plastic.

#### Soil

- The examination compares color, texture and composition to determine whether two soils have the same origin.
- Collect the samples as soon as possible, because the soil at the scene can change a great deal.
- Where to collect:
  - In the immediate area of the scene and along the logical routes of entry or escape.
  - Where the color, texture or composition changes to the naked eye.
  - At the same depth from which the questioned soil could have come.
  - If possible, at the alibi locations, such as a garden or a workplace.
- Mark on a map where each sample was taken.
- Do not remove the soil stuck to shoes, clothing and tools.
- Do not process the tools for latent prints.
- Air-dry the soil and the clothing, and package them separately in paper bags.
- Carefully remove the soil stuck to vehicles. Air-dry it and package it separately in paper bags.
- Send the known and the questioned soil separately so they are not contaminated.
- Send them in airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles. Do not use paper envelopes or glass.
- Package so that the chunks stay whole.

Exam note: the book asks that dry soil be packaged in paper bags and, at the same time, sent in airtight containers and "not in paper envelopes". It is inconsistent. Pending: clarify which packaging applies to each case.

#### Tape

- Its composition, construction and color can be compared with known sources.
- A torn piece can be compared with a roll of tape.
- Laboratories examine duct tape, vinyl electrical tape and packing tape. They may not examine cellulose acetate tape (for example, Scotch tape).
- Guidelines:
  1. Whenever possible, send the tape stuck to its surface. That way fewer traces, latent prints and contact impressions are lost.
  2. If that is not possible, peel it off by hand and place it adhesive side down on a clean, colorless plastic sheet (for example, transparency film or tubular plastic on a roll).
  3. Do not place it on cardboard, paper or vinyl document sleeves.
  4. Do not deform or tear the tape when peeling it off.
  5. If it has to be cut, document each cut and put your initials. Cut in a way that the pattern is unique, for example with pinking shears.

#### Toolmarks

- Tools have unique microscopic characteristics, due to their manufacture and use. Those characteristics transfer to the surfaces they touch.
- The marks on the evidence can be compared with the recovered tools.
- With no questioned tool, the examination says what type of tool made the mark and whether the mark is suitable for comparison.
- It also includes the examination of locks and keys.
- Guidelines:
  1. Take samples of the material left on the tools. Send them in airtight containers, such as film canisters or plastic pill bottles.
  2. To avoid contamination, do not bring the tool near the marked evidence.
  3. Send the tool, not test cuts or marks.
  4. Mark the ends of the evidence and indicate which end was cut when collecting it.
  5. Photos help locate the marks, but they are not useful for identifying the tool.
  6. If the marked evidence cannot be sent, make a cast and send it. Call the laboratory to find out which kit to use and how.

#### Toxicology

- The examination detects alcohol, drugs and poisons in biological samples and in food.
- It helps understand what happened in incidents and accidents involving drugs or poisons.
- Because there are a great many toxic substances, they are sometimes sought by families of poisons:

| Family | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Volatile compounds | Ethanol, methanol, isopropanol |
| Heavy metals | Arsenic |
| Nonvolatile organic compounds | Drugs of abuse, pharmaceuticals |
| Miscellaneous substances | Strychnine, cyanide |

- In drug-facilitated assaults, the biological evidence must include a urine sample. It is collected as soon as possible, and never more than 96 hours after the alleged drugging.
- The amount of sample depends on four factors: whether the toxic substance is known, the route of administration, the time between exposure and collection, and whether the people are alive or dead.
- Call the laboratory before sending the samples, to send the right amount.
- Shipping guidelines:
  1. Each sample goes separately, labeled and sealed, in a glass tube, a plastic cup or a heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag.
  2. Put "BIOHAZARD" on the inner and outer containers.
  3. Refrigerate or freeze the samples during storage and shipping, so they do not deteriorate.
  4. Package without breakage, leaks or contamination.
  5. Send a copy of the incident report.
  6. Describe the person's symptoms at the time of the incident.
  7. State the drugs, known or suspected, that the person took or had been prescribed.
  8. Describe any environmental exposure to toxic substances, known or suspected.

#### Video

- **Authenticity examinations:** tell whether a recording is original, continuous, unaltered and consistent with the device that recorded it.
- **Enhancement examinations:** seek the maximum clarity of the video signal, with video processors and time-base correctors.
- Enhanced prints can be made from the video images.
- Audio and video signals can be combined into a single recording.
- Special effects, such as a mosaic, can be added to hide a person's identity.
- Write-protect the original recording and never use pause when viewing it.
- When sending originals to a laboratory, indicate where the images are on the recording and describe them.
- Label the outside: "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT" or "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE AUDIO/VIDEO MEDIA", and also "KEEP AWAY FROM MAGNETS OR MAGNETIC FIELDS".

Exam note: this section repeats almost all the guidelines in "Audio and Video Recordings".

#### Wood

- The examination can match faces, ends and fractures, and determine the species of the wood.
- It also compares wood particles found on clothing, vehicles or other objects with the wood at the scene.
- Send the wood in plastic or paper bags.

#### Review Table: Packaging According to the Book

| Packaging | Types of evidence |
| --- | --- |
| Clean paper or envelope with sealed corners; never plastic | Cloths or swabs with blood; bloodstained clothing and its debris; saliva, urine and semen; cigarette butts; chewing gum, envelopes and postage stamps (clean envelope); buccal swabs; hair; teeth and bones |
| Wrapped in paper or in a paper bag | Clothing with gunshot residue (each garment separately); objects with construction debris; clothing with glass; whole objects with lubricant or pepper spray; clothing with polymer debris; objects with safe insulation; soil and clothing with soil; shoeprint casts |
| Airtight container (film canister, plastic pill bottle); never paper or glass | Construction debris; glass samples, particles and vacuumings; soil (nor paper envelopes); polymer fragments (nor cotton); samples of tampered products; material left on tools |
| Airtight container (no further detail) | Chemical powders and liquids; lubricants |
| Vials or pillboxes; never plastic bags, cotton, envelopes or adhesive tape | Paint |
| Heat-sealed or zip-closure plastic bag | Small samples and swabs of chemicals; lubricant swabs; pepper spray swabs; controlled substances; toxicology samples (also glass tube or plastic cup) |
| Airtight plastic container without formalin | Tissues |
| Zip-closure plastic bags or boxes | Damaged vehicle parts |
| Plastic or paper (either) | Ropes and cords; wood |
| Clean, airtight container, frozen | Blood in snow or water |
| Never plastic, for other reasons | Objects with a dry residue impression (the impression transfers to the plastic); storage media (static, according to Appendix 6B) |

#### Review Table: Temperature

| Evidence | Guideline |
| --- | --- |
| Blood tubes from a person | Refrigerate, do not freeze. Cold packs, not dry ice. Send immediately |
| Blood in snow or water | Freeze in an airtight container |
| Buccal swabs | Do not need refrigeration |
| Hair | Refrigerate and send as soon as possible |
| Tissues, bones and teeth | Freeze and send with dry ice, overnight |
| Toxicology samples | Refrigerate or freeze during storage and shipping |
| Organic materials in general | Time and temperature deteriorate them: take measures |
| Computer equipment in transit (Appendix 6B) | Avoid extreme heat and cold |

#### Review Table: Shipping Labels

| Label | When it is used |
| --- | --- |
| "BIOHAZARD" | Inner container with biological hazard; bloody clothing and toxicology samples (inner and outer); blood tubes (outer); containers for contaminated sharps |
| "LATENT" | Inner container when latent prints are to be searched for |
| "KEEP IN A COOL DRY PLACE" and "REFRIGERATE ON ARRIVAL" | Outside of blood tubes |
| "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT" | Computers, electronic devices, audio and video recordings |
| "FRAGILE, SENSITIVE AUDIO/VIDEO MEDIA" | Audio and video recordings (in place of the previous one) |
| "KEEP AWAY FROM MAGNETS OR MAGNETIC FIELDS" | Computers, electronic devices, audio and video recordings |
| "THIS END UP" | Computer central processing units, which are shipped upright |
| "ORM-D, Cartridges, Small Arms" | Outside of the live ammunition shipment |
| "INSIDE" and "OUTSIDE" | Faces of laminated glass and faces of the glass in the frame |
| "TOP", "BOTTOM", "LEFT" and "RIGHT" | Part of the frame the glass was taken from |

### 6.4 Incident Scene Safety

- It covers the hazards, precautions and personal protective equipment (PPE) for working common scenes.
- Security personnel must consult environmental and occupational health and safety laws, and waste disposal rules.
- **Application to security:** security managers and heads of investigation units must include personal safety measures in contracts for outsourced investigation services that involve collecting or handling evidence.
- Neglecting the safety of contracted personnel exposes the organization to liability and reputational damage if a contractor is injured.

#### 6.4.1 Routes of Exposure

Anyone who works in or near a contaminated environment must know the routes by which a hazard enters the body and harms it.

| Route | How it occurs | Prevention according to the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Inhalation | The substance enters the respiratory system. It can come as dust, aerosol, smoke, vapor, gas or fume. Solids and liquids are also dangerous if they give off vapors, mists or fumes | Good work practices and adequate ventilation. Respiratory protection is mandatory where there are airborne contaminants. Only certified personnel may use it and work in those areas |
| Skin contact | Direct contact or absorption. Severity depends on the concentration and the exposure time. What is absorbed circulates through the body and causes systemic effects: dizziness, tremors, nausea, blurred vision, liver and kidney damage, shock or collapse | Personal protective equipment: gloves, safety glasses, goggles, face shields and protective clothing |
| Ingestion | The substance enters through the mouth. It can seriously damage the mouth, throat and digestive system | Always wash hands before eating, smoking or applying makeup. Do not bring food, drink or cigarettes into areas that may be contaminated, even if protection is worn |
| Injection | Needle stick or wound from glass, metal or another contaminated sharp object. The contaminant passes directly into the blood and spreads quickly | Take extreme care with sharp objects. Always wear work gloves |

#### 6.4.2 Safety

The book covers five areas: bloodborne pathogens, chemicals, light sources, confined spaces and X-rays.

**Bloodborne-Pathogen Safety**

- In the US, the standards of the Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA, the federal occupational safety and health agency) are in Title 29, section 1910.1030, of the Code of Federal Regulations (CFR): 29 CFR 1910.1030.
- The basis of the standard is the concept of universal precautions.
- It requires treating all blood, body fluid or other potentially infectious material as if it were infected.
- Diseases it cites: hepatitis B virus (HBV), hepatitis C virus (HCV) and human immunodeficiency virus (HIV).
- Measures to avoid direct contact:
  1. Use barriers, such as disposable gloves, coveralls and shoe covers, if contact is possible.
  2. Change gloves if they tear, are punctured or cease to work as a barrier.
  3. Use adequate eye and face protection.
  4. Remove gloves and the rest of the equipment without contaminating the skin or clothing. Wash hands afterward.
  5. Do not eat, drink, smoke or apply makeup where there is blood, fluids or other potentially infectious materials, even if protective equipment is worn.
  6. Do not bend, recap, remove or handle needles or other contaminated sharp objects.
  7. Place contaminated sharp objects in closable, airtight, puncture-resistant containers for transport or disposal. Label them "BIOHAZARD".
  8. Decontaminate equipment after use. Use household bleach diluted 1:10 and prepared each day, 70% isopropyl alcohol or another suitable disinfectant.
  9. Noncorrosive disinfectants are available for sale. Allow enough contact time to fully disinfect.
- In addition, engineering controls and prudent practices reduce or eliminate exposure.
- Examples of engineering controls: puncture-resistant containers for storing and disposing of sharp objects; paint stirrers and long-handled mirrors to find and retrieve evidence from narrow or hidden spaces.

**Chemical Safety**

| Type | Examples | Hazard |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flammable or combustible | Gasoline, acetone, ether | Ignite easily with air and an ignition source, such as a spark or a flame |
| Explosives | Nitroglycerin, nitroglycerin-based dynamite | Over time they deteriorate and become chemically unstable. All are sensitive to heat, shock and friction |
| Pyrophoric | Phosphorus, sodium, barium | Liquids or solids. Ignite on their own in air at below 130 °F (54 °C), without an ignition source |
| Oxidizers | Nitrates, hydrogen peroxide, concentrated sulfuric acid | Release oxygen and fuel combustion. Do not store them with flammables or combustibles, or with substances that accelerate their decomposition |
| Corrosives | (the book gives no examples of substances) | Destroy living tissue and objects such as wood and steel. The damage depends on the concentration and the duration of contact |

Exam note: in the explosives row, the book gives as an example that ether forms peroxides around the mouth of the container. But ether is listed among the flammables, not the explosives.

- In the US, manufacturers, distributors and importers must provide a Safety Data Sheet for each hazardous chemical.
- The sheet gives information on the hazards, how to dispose of it, how to package and ship it, and how to act in an emergency. Other countries also require it.

**Light-Source Safety**

- With ultraviolet light, lasers and other light sources, protect the eyes from direct and indirect exposure.
- Not all laser beams are visible. Direct or reflected light can cause irreversible eye damage.
- Also avoid prolonged skin exposure.
- All personnel nearby must wear glasses suited to that light source.
- Goggles must have enough protective material and fit well, so that no light enters from any angle.
- In the US, they must bear the mark of the American National Standards Institute (ANSI) certifying eye protection.
- Laser glasses must have the optical density suited to the laser's maximum wavelength.

**Confined Space Safety**

- **Confined space:** an enclosed area large enough for a person to enter and work in, but with limited or difficult entry and exit.
- Examples: sewers, open pits, tank cars and vats. They are not designed for continuous occupancy.
- Hazards: toxic gases, explosive or oxygen-deficient atmospheres, electrical hazards and materials that can engulf the person entering.
- They must always be considered dangerous.
- Do not enter until a trained person evaluates the space and, if necessary, an entry permit is issued.
- Monitor the atmosphere continuously with a calibrated direct-reading instrument. It measures oxygen, carbon monoxide, flammable gases and vapors, and toxic contaminants.
- Record the periodic monitor readings.
- Only personnel certified for confined spaces may work inside. Rescue services must be available immediately.
- Mandatory practices:
  1. Never enter before identifying and documenting the atmospheric, engulfment, mechanical and electrical hazards.
  2. Ventilate, without the ventilation equipment interfering with entry, exit or rescue.
  3. Set up barriers that warn unauthorized persons and protect those entering from external hazards.
  4. Maintain constant communication between the entrant and the attendants watching from outside.
  5. Have backup communication ready before entering.
  6. Use the right equipment: self-contained breathing apparatus (SCBA), full-body harness and head protection.
  7. Never attempt a rescue unless you are part of the designated rescue team.
  8. Have personnel certified in first aid and cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) on site. Rescue services must also be available immediately.
- Reference standard: OSHA, Permit-Required Confined Spaces, 29 CFR 1910.146.

**X-Ray Safety**

- Portable handheld X-ray machines are used to see what is inside suspicious packages. At the scene they pose a risk of radiation exposure.
- To work safely:
  1. Shield the device, the suspicious object and the operator.
  2. Remove all nonessential personnel from the X-ray field.
  3. Limit the time personnel spend in the area.
  4. Wear the dosimeters assigned for X-ray radiation.
  5. Have and follow standard operating procedures, with adequate training according to federal and state standards.

#### 6.4.3 Personal Protective Equipment

- At every scene, equipment is chosen based on a hazard-risk assessment done by trained and qualified personnel.
- That assessment identifies the possible contaminants and the hazards of each product.

**Hand Protection**

- The glove is chosen according to the material handled and its hazards. The manufacturer provides the detailed information.

| Glove material | Protects against | Limitations or notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nitrile | Acids, alkaline solutions, hydraulic fluid, photographic solutions, fuels, lubricants, aromatics, petroleum and chlorinated solvents | Some resistance to cuts and snags |
| Neoprene | Oil, grease, acids, solvents, alkalis, bases and most refrigerants | — |
| Polyvinyl chloride (PVC) | Alkalis, oils and limited concentrations of nitric and chromic acid | — |
| Latex (natural rubber) | Mild acids, caustics, detergents, germicides and ketone solutions | Swells and degrades with gasoline or kerosene. Degrades with prolonged excessive heat or direct sun |
| Powder-free, reduced protein | — | Reduce the risk of developing latex allergy. A person who is allergic to latex can usually use nitrile or neoprene |

Glove use guidelines:

1. Check that they have no holes, punctures or tears before putting them on. Remove rings and other objects that could puncture them.
2. With heavily contaminated materials, double glove.
3. Change them if they tear, are punctured or cease to work as a barrier.
4. Remove disposable ones by pulling on the cuffs to turn them inside out. That way the skin and clothing are not contaminated. Throw them in the designated containers and do not reuse them.

**Eye Protection**

- Use safety glasses and goggles when handling biological, chemical and radioactive materials.
- Face shields protect better if there is a risk of splashes or flying debris. They are always used together with safety glasses or goggles.
- Anyone who wears contact lenses must wear safety glasses or goggles. If a chemical splashes the eye, it is hard to remove the lens to flush it, and the contaminant can become trapped behind it.
- Protective glasses are worn over prescription glasses. Prescription safety glasses can also be made.

**Foot Protection**

- Footwear that covers and protects the entire foot is essential.
- Use protective footwear if there is a risk of falling or rolling objects, objects piercing the sole or electrical hazards.
- Standards recognized by OSHA: ASTM International F2412 and F2413 (ASTM is a technical standards body; the book does not explain the acronym).
- In some cases, waterproof shoe covers protect the footwear and prevent carrying contaminants out of the scene.

**Respiratory Protection**

- Some scenes, such as bombings and clandestine labs, produce harmful fumes and other airborne contaminants. There, respiratory protection must be used.
- In the US, whenever respirators are used, compliance with the standard 29 CFR 1910.134, Respiratory Protection, is mandatory.
- Five critical elements for using respirators safely:
  1. A written program.
  2. Training.
  3. Medical evaluation.
  4. Fit testing.
  5. A respirator maintenance program.

**Head Protection**

- Wear a protective helmet at scenes with structural damage, or where there may be.
- Standard recognized by OSHA: ANSI Z89.1-2014, Requirements for Industrial Head Protection.

#### Review Table: Standards Cited in the Chapter

| Topic | Standard or body |
| --- | --- |
| Bloodborne pathogens | OSHA, 29 CFR 1910.1030 |
| Permit-required confined spaces | OSHA, 29 CFR 1910.146 |
| Respiratory protection | 29 CFR 1910.134 |
| Protective footwear | ASTM F2412 and F2413 |
| Protective helmet | ANSI Z89.1-2014 |
| Protective glasses | ANSI compliance mark |
| Shipping of hazardous materials | US Department of Transportation and International Air Transport Association |
| Hazardous waste | Environmental Protection Agency, RCRA ("cradle-to-grave") |
| Chemical sheets | Safety Data Sheets |

Exam note (text versus references):
