- The text cites "ANSI Z89.1-2014, Requirements for Industrial Head Protection". The references cite "ANSI/ISEA Z89.1-2014 R2019, American National Standard for Industrial Head Protection".
- The references cite the footwear standard as "ASTM F2413-1a". Possible typo for "F2413-18a", like the "F2412-18a" that appears next to it.
- The references include 29 CFR 1910.147 (lockout/tagout) and 49 CFR 172.101 (hazardous materials table). The chapter text does not mention them.

## Capítulo 7 — Testimony (págs. 257–266)

**Central idea:** Testimony is the end of a journey that begins with the incident. The security professional's testimony is worth as much as their procedures, reports and preparation are worth. Knowing the trial participants and the opposing attorney's traps also counts. The chapter distinguishes between a fact witness and an expert witness, and cites the three US Supreme Court cases on experts.

### Chapter Introduction — Basic Concepts (pp. 257–258)

- Testimonial evidence is usually a large part of the evidence presented at trial.
- Because of the incidents in their work, security personnel are frequently called to testify.
- Where they may testify (complete list):
  - Regulatory hearings, formal or informal.
  - Interviews with government authorities.
  - Formal sworn statements (depositions).
  - Before a grand jury.
  - Civil or criminal courts of any level.
- They are usually accompanied by their counsel. During the testimony, the attorney may not be able to help them or intervene.
- Testifying in court is intimidating. That is why it is advisable to know the rules and the processes.
- Laws vary between countries, and even within the same country. As an example, the book follows two US federal codes: the Federal Code of Civil Procedures and the Federal Code of Criminal Procedures.
  - Exam note: possible typo in the names. The official names in the US are usually "Federal Rules of Civil/Criminal Procedure".
- Testimony must always be truthful. It must also follow the court's guidelines and the attorney's strategy.

| Aspect | Fact witness | Opinion witness |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other names | — | Expert witness, forensic witness |
| What they testify to | Only what they perceived: what they saw, heard, smelled, touched or tasted | An opinion on the matter |
| Requirement | — | Demonstrate training, education and experience in a specific subject |
| Basis | Their senses | Analysis of the evidence with accepted scientific standards and academic research methods |

- Security personnel can be either.
- **Hearsay:** telling what another person said. It may or may not be admissible.
- Typical exception to hearsay: the **dying declaration**. For example, the last words of a victim who identifies the attacker.

### 7.1 The Journey

- Testimony is the end of a process that began with an earlier incident.
- They may be asked to testify from the very moment up to years later, even if they have already left the job.
- From their first day in the position they must know that the verdict may depend on four things: their observations, reports or findings, plus their demeanor and their ability to explain the facts.

#### 7.1.1 Procedure

- Every security incident must be treated as if it were going to end up in court.
- That requires following established procedures or, at a minimum, consistent personal procedures.
- **Marking evidence:** initials, date and tracking or report number. If possible, always in the same place on the item.
- If the procedure was not followed, they must know why and be ready to explain it.
- Causes of deviation the book gives: the weather, the urgency of the situation and the type of evidence being marked (the book leaves the list open).
- The witness must know well the evidence they say they marked.
- They must examine it before taking the stand for two reasons: its appearance may have changed and it may be presented in a different order than expected.

#### 7.1.2 Report

- Give the attorney all relevant reports and notes to review.
- It is also advisable to explain to the attorney what they will testify to.
- The witness must know the facts well, but without carrying a script of the testimony.

#### 7.1.3 Testimony Preparation

- Preparation begins long before litigation is even considered.
- It begins when recording the facts and impressions of the incident in a report or notes, accurately and on time.
- Written documentation is much more credible than memory, because time and subsequent events weaken and alter memory.
- Shortly before testifying, preparation may include:
  - Visiting the site.
  - Reviewing notes, reports, photos and diagrams.
- The case is reviewed thoroughly with the prosecuting attorney to see its strengths and weaknesses.
- The attorney wants to make sure the testimony supports the case.

| Term | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Parol / "spoken word" | Another name for oral testimonial evidence presented at trial |
| Pre-trial meeting | The opposing parties meet and exchange the information each believes necessary for its position. It is the norm |
| Duces tecum | Request between the parties for all tangible evidence to be produced |
| Interrogatories | Written questions that the parties exchange |
| Prior interviews or interrogations | May be conducted with witnesses, the complainant and the defendant |
| Deposition | Formal sworn statement. It is admissible at trial |

- **Remote deposition:** it used to be done in person. The COVID-19 pandemic made remote ones more common.
- For a remote deposition, one must prepare just as for an in-person one:
  - Dress professionally.
  - Sit in front of a neutral background.
  - Answer as well as possible.

### 7.2 Courtroom Participants

| Participant | Role according to the book |
| --- | --- |
| Judges | Public officials who decide questions of law. They are the final authority for regulating the litigation. In every trial they enforce procedural rules. In a **bench trial** (trial without a jury) they also decide the outcome: guilty or innocent, liable or not |
| Juries | Selected citizens (jurors) who decide based on the facts presented. They are not professionals at judging facts |
| Counsel (attorneys, lawyers) | Advocates hired to represent their client as well as possible, within the procedural rules and with integrity |
| Witnesses | Of fact or of opinion (expert or forensic). Their testimony can support or contradict other evidence |

#### 7.2.1 Judges

- See the table. They decide questions of law, enforce procedure and, in a bench trial, decide the merits.

#### 7.2.2 Juries

- Their names usually come from the voter rolls of the jurisdiction.
- After being sworn, the candidates go through **voir dire**. In it, the attorneys, the judge or both question them to see whether they are fit for that trial.
- Once chosen, the judge instructs them on:
  - What they may consider evidence: relevancy, materiality and competency.
  - Giving different weight to each piece of evidence.
  - Delivering the verdict within the applicable standard of proof.

#### 7.2.3 Counsel

- It may suit the attorney to point out contradictions between what was stated before trial and what was stated at trial.

#### 7.2.4 Witnesses

- Example: someone with a lot of experience in automobile painting may be recognized as an expert in the investigation of an accident.

### 7.3 Testifying

- The employee can improve their effectiveness as a witness by learning about the points in 7.3.1 through 7.3.6.

#### 7.3.1 Pre-Testimony Review

- In any forum, before testifying:
  - Review their notes.
  - Talk with the attorney about their testimony, including their doubts about the weak points of the case.
- Each attorney has their own ideas about:
  - Strategy.
  - How to present witnesses.
  - The form of the answers.
  - What to say on direct examination and what to leave for cross-examination (the one conducted by the opposing attorney).
- The security employee is usually just one piece of the attorney's strategy.
- Some attorneys want short answers without superlatives. Others prefer long, highly descriptive answers.
- In all cases: answer only what is asked.

A good pre-testimony review examines three things:

| # | What to review |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Incident notes and reports |
| 2 | Evidence that is available, accessible and recoverable |
| 3 | Documentary proof of subsequent actions or hearings |

- If physical evidence will be used, the employee should examine it in person at the pre-trial conference.
- It is the only way to verify that the evidence exists in full, as described by the documents and the evidence tags.
- They must be ready to testify about how the evidence was collected and about the chain of custody.

#### 7.3.2 Incident Site and Courtroom

- **Incident site:** visit it, ideally under conditions similar to those of the incident. The book cites: time of day, time of year, lighting and weather.
- Benefits of the visit:
  - Clarifies points in the report.
  - Puts them in perspective.
  - Greatly improves recall.
- **Courtroom:** visit it before testifying. Especially if it is their first time or their first in that country.
- What to observe on the visit:
  - The testimony of other witnesses.
  - The style of the different attorneys.
  - What the judge does.
  - How the jury behaves.
- That way the "mystery" surrounding trials disappears.

#### 7.3.3 Public Place Discussions

- Do not discuss the case in public, or with anyone who is not the attorney on your side.
- If a witness gives opinions in front of other witnesses, it creates two risks:
  - Problems of **impeachment** (discrediting the witness), for example if their words are misinterpreted.
  - Revealing their testimony prematurely to the opposing party.
- Also do not comment or post about the case on social media or news websites.

#### 7.3.4 Demeanor and Attire

| Avoid | Seek |
| --- | --- |
| Stiffness | Being personable |
| Arguing (argumentative) | Being charming |
| Arrogance | Professionalism |
| Evasiveness | Courtesy |
| Indifference (uncaring) | Seriousness and sincerity (earnest) |
| Taking questions personally | Showing knowledge |

- Security personnel must also convey self-confidence.
- **Attire:** suited to the jury and the jurisdiction.
  - The uniform may appeal to the jury or not.
  - A business suit may convey arrogance.
  - Whatever the style: clean, tidy, respectable and without ostentation.

#### 7.3.5 Questioning and Response Techniques

**Opposing counsel's traps, in the deposition and at trial (source: McElheny 2006):**

| Trap | How to respond |
| --- | --- |
| Gauging whether the witness will withstand tough cross-examination | Go in prepared. Remember that they probably know more than the attorney about security response and reporting procedures |
| Summarizing the witness's answer in the attorney's words | Do not answer with a simple "yes". Clarify that the summary is accurate only in very general terms and does not reflect what was said. The attorney's words can change the meaning |
| Skipping the rules of evidence by seeking hearsay, conversations, rumors and opinions | Be aware that it is a trap |
| Asking for the witness's notes | See rules on notes, below |
| Trick questions | Correct the false premises before answering |

- **Rules on notes:**
  - Before testifying, ask the attorney whether to use notes and what to do if opposing counsel asks for them.
  - If notes are used, opposing counsel may be entitled to receive a copy beforehand.
  - Notes taken to the stand must be well organized.
  - If opposing counsel takes them for a moment, do not continue testifying until they are returned.
  - If they put them out of order, politely ask the judge for time to put them in order.
- **Trick question case:** in a case over inadequate security, the attorney asks what the guard did first "when he was working alone that night and Mary signaled to him in the parking lot to report an assault".
  - Perhaps it was daytime, there were several guards or he intervened on his own initiative.
  - If he only answers "I checked whether she was hurt and called an ambulance", he lets the falsehoods pass without clarifying them.
  - Lesson: those falsehoods can influence the jury. They must be corrected.
- **If they do not understand the question:** ask the attorney to speak louder or to clarify it.
- **If they still do not understand it**, they have three options:
  - Say that they are confused.
  - Ask whether they can give a qualified answer (limited and explained).
  - Say that they cannot answer with a yes or a no.
- Never answer the question you think they are asking you.
- The attorneys and the judge may comment. The witness does what the judge says.

**Other rules for answering:**

- Never guess or offer information that has not been requested.
- If they do not know or do not remember, say so or ask whether they may consult their notes.
- Repeating "I don't remember" over and over damages credibility.
- If they are asked things they have no reason to know, their attorney will usually object.
- Do not nod or shake the head until the opposing attorney's question is complete.
- Keep up to date in the field of security. Ignoring a common and relevant practice cited by opposing counsel would make them look uninformed, unprofessional and unconvincing.
- Show confidence, speak clearly and give positive, direct and definitive answers whenever possible.
- They may take their time, but without obviously delaying or denying the obvious.
- If they use technical terms the jury may not know, explain them right away in simple words.

**Jury biases:**

- Juries decide on facts, but they are influenced by their personality, culture and life experience.
- Fact (Anwar 2012): in criminal cases, many studies find that Black jurors convict less than white jurors, especially when the defendant is Black.
- Because of this "cross-race effect", it matters to build good rapport with the jury and show competence.
- Other characteristics that can bias the jury: gender, age, religion, ethnicity and sexual orientation.
- Observe the jury's reactions. If a negative reaction is noticed, adjust the way of speaking.
- Possible causes of a negative reaction:
  - Body language conveys laziness, lack of professionalism or coldness.
  - The testimony is too long or has too much jargon.

**Eye contact (Broda-Bahm 2015):**

| Before | Today |
| --- | --- |
| Sometimes it was advised not to look at the jury | Most experts believe that eye contact keeps the jury attentive |

- While being questioned, look at the attorney.
- While answering, divide your gaze between the jury and the attorney.

**Body language and response strategy (complete list, 9 points):**

1. Look comfortable. Do not slouch, rest the head on the hand, chew gum, touch earrings or mustache, or fidget in the chair.
2. Ask to go to the restroom or for a glass of water if needed.
3. Speak with authority, without arrogance.
4. Answer directly and do not offer anything that is not asked.
5. Admit having met with the attorney.
6. Admit having discussed the case with others, if true and those conversations are not privileged.
7. Stay calm even if opposing counsel seems to lose their composure.
8. Stick to the answer even if the attorney repeats the question many times, each time with more incredulity.
9. Force opposing counsel to ask simple questions, not several questions in one.

- Exam note: in the text the source is spelled "McElheny 2006". In the reference list it appears as "McElhaney, J. W. 2006".

#### 7.3.6 Inconsistencies

- Attorneys are trained to look for inconsistencies and exploit them.
- Four types that can make the jury believe the witness is lying:

| # | Inconsistency between… |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Parts of the same statement by the witness |
| 2 | Different statements by the same witness |
| 3 | The witness's statement and what others said |
| 4 | The witness's statement and other evidence |

### 7.4 Expert Witnesses

- Unlike the fact witness, the expert may give an opinion on what caused the incident or what would have prevented it.
- To be accepted as an expert, they usually need: experience, education, teaching, research and published articles in their field.
- Other countries may define the expert differently.
- In the US, the legal requirements come from three Supreme Court cases:

| Case | Year | What it establishes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc. | 1993 | The judge must verify that scientific testimony is based on sound science before admitting it |
| General Electric Co. v. Joiner | 1997 (the year appears only in the references) | The trial judge may decide what type of scientific testimony the jury hears |
| Kumho Tire v. Carmichael Co. | 1999 (the year appears only in the references) | Extends Daubert: any expert, scientific or not, must be examined before testifying |

- Exam note: the text writes "Kumho Tire v. Carmichael Co.". The references say "Kumho Tire Co., Ltd. v. Carmichael". The text does not give the years of Joiner and Kumho.

| Aspect | Fact witness | Expert |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How they come | Asked or compelled to appear | Hired to give their opinion |
| Hypothetical questions | Not obliged to answer them | Must answer them |

- **Who decides whether someone is an expert:** the judge in the case. The judge decides on the person the attorney proposes and hires.
- Expert = someone with knowledge that a layperson or someone outside a defined field does not usually have.
- In some jurisdictions the judge appoints experts to assist the judge and the jury. In most, each party hires its own.
- **Example, lawsuit for inadequate security with injury from a violent act.** Experts may be hired to:
  - Give an opinion on the who, what, when, where, how and perhaps the why, after reviewing the facts and analyzing relevant data.
  - Purely security topics: premises liability, policies and procedures, personnel, technology, hardware and design, and their relation to the incident.
  - Criminology topics.
- **Advice for the expert:**
  - Accept a case on its merits and because it fits their specialty.
  - Be ready to document their experience and training at trial.
  - Have no obvious conflicts of interest.
  - Gather the relevant information and prepare to have their memory and opinions challenged.
  - This may include a site inspection and a crime analysis (Meyer 2020).
- **Formula for a hypothetical:** say that they understand the scenario, but that those are not the facts of the case, and that if they were they would answer in such a way.
  - That way they answer without applying the hypothetical to the real case.
- Answer directly and briefly. Explaining too much gives opposing counsel more information and opens lines of questioning for which they may not be prepared.

**Chapter close, the complete journey:**

1. It begins with an incident.
2. It depends on procedures and reports.
3. Judges, juries, attorneys and witnesses take part.
4. It requires a prior review.
5. It ends with testimony given knowing the techniques for testifying and the opposing attorney's traps.

### Key Terms

testimonial evidence · fact witness · opinion witness · expert witness · forensic witness · hearsay · dying declaration · deposition · remote deposition · grand jury · regulatory hearing · parol ("spoken word") · pre-trial meeting · duces tecum · interrogatories · complainant · defendant · bench trial · jurors · voter rolls · voir dire · relevancy · materiality · competency · standard of proof · direct examination · cross-examination · pre-testimony review · pre-trial conference · evidence tags · chain of custody · impeachment · demeanor · trick questions · qualified answer · cross-race effect · rapport · privileged discussions · inconsistencies · Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals (1993) · General Electric Co. v. Joiner (1997) · Kumho Tire v. Carmichael (1999) · premises liability · crime analysis · hypothetical questions

## Capítulo 8 — Maximizing the Investigation (págs. 267–292)

**Central idea:** To get the most out of an investigation, force multipliers must be used: online sources, liaison, intelligence and technology. Findings must be communicated in clear, protected reports. In addition, they must serve to improve the investigation process and the organization's security posture. The chapter closes with future trends.

### Chapter Introduction (p. 267)

- The investigative unit (IU) must use all the resources it can gather.
- Force multipliers help the IU find and evaluate information faster.
- During the investigation and afterward, clear and complete reports are needed to communicate activities and findings.
- At the end, managers must do two things:
  - Eliminate the factors that allowed the criminal activity, dangerous conditions or other negative events.
  - Evaluate the investigation process itself in order to improve it.

### 8.1 Investigative Force Multipliers

- The term originates in the military sphere. It is applied to electronic warfare, intelligence operations and perception management.
- **Original US Department of Defense (DoD) definition:** a capability that, when added to and employed by a combat force, significantly increases its combat potential. It thereby improves the probability of accomplishing the mission.
- **Corporate adaptation:** any activity, capability or resource that improves the effectiveness or efficiency of the investigative function.
- **Example:** the "If You See Something, Say Something" campaign.
  - It is a joint initiative of the US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and local police.
  - It is a national awareness program that asks the public to report suspicious or illegal activity.

The four main force multipliers:

| # | Multiplier | Section |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Online information sources | 8.1.1 |
| 2 | Liaison (collaborative relationships) | 8.1.2 |
| 3 | Intelligence information | 8.1.3 |
| 4 | Technology | 8.1.4 |

- All four have costs. They are an investment in the investigative function.
- Both investigation managers and the executives who make decisions must understand this clearly.

#### 8.1.1 Online Information Sources

- The core of every investigation is information.
- Anything that speeds up and makes information more efficient in four tasks is an important multiplier: gathering it, cross-checking it, analyzing it and organizing it.
- To use the Internet well, three things must be understood:
  - Its structure.
  - What can be consulted and what cannot.
  - How to evaluate the reliability of what is found.
- There is a lot of information and also a lot of disinformation. Extreme caution is advisable.

**Internet Structure (iceberg model)**

| Layer | What it contains | Access | % of the total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Surface web or clear web (tip of the iceberg) | The public content found with basic searches: news, shopping, business and government websites, part of social media. These are billions of sites | The only layer indexed by search engines such as Google | About 4% (Ajaeb 2019) |
| Deep web (below the water) | What is behind a login or in internal databases: online databases, social media exchanges, internal company websites, financial records, research catalogs, file storage | Not indexed by search engines because it is not public. That does not make it dangerous | More than 93% |
| Dark web | Network of sites deliberately hidden, for anonymous activities, often dangerous and illegal. Most of it is drug trafficking, arms sales and other illicit exchanges. It is also used to evade censorship in some countries | Only with special browsers, such as Tor | About 3% |

- Exam note: the figures add up to more than 100%. They are 4%, more than 93% and 3%.
- Search engines are useful for starting out, but the real value is in what lies beneath the surface.

**Sources for Online Information and Research**

- What used to take days or weeks is now obtained in minutes. For example, public records, court files or business registries.
- Social media gives a closer and more personal view of the subject's life.
- The book clarifies that its list is not exhaustive. It is a summary of what is most often sought.

**Public Records Databases**

- They are among the most used sources.
- They may include:
  - Criminal and civil court files.
  - Property and land records.
  - Tax liens.
  - Judgments.
- A key step for many investigators: finding out the subject's criminal history.
- State, county, city and municipal courts can be consulted. Results vary widely and not all publish the details online.
- US federal cases: **PACER (Public Access to Court Electronic Records)**, at PACER.gov. It allows searching millions of federal criminal, civil and bankruptcy cases.
- Property, tax liens and civil judgments: at the county assessors' and recorders' offices. Many have online search.
- The key: know which agency holds each record and how much online access it gives.

**Regulatory Databases (US; other countries have equivalents)**

- Searching for regulators' actions against a person or company shows their personal and professional conduct.
- It is an essential step of due diligence.

| Agency | What is sought |
| --- | --- |
| SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission, securities regulator) | Securities violations |
| OFAC (Office of Foreign Assets Control) | Economic sanctions |
| FTC (Federal Trade Commission, commerce) | Legal and enforcement actions |
| EPA (Environmental Protection Agency) | Environmental violations |
| OSHA (Occupational Safety and Health Administration) | Workplace violations |
| FEC (Federal Election Commission, elections) | Political campaign contributions |
| Secretary of State or State Corporations Division | Business and company records in each state |
| State Licensing Boards | Professional licenses and disciplinary sanctions |

**Online News and Media**

- Court files do not always tell the whole story.
- The news, especially local news, covers relevant stories from the area. It provides witness statements and details of the incident.
- It is advisable to know the local and regional media of the area under investigation.
- They are useful for crimes, workplace incidents or a company's reputation.

**Library Resources and Online Archival Research**

- A library card is one of the most versatile tools of the investigator.
- Many public libraries give access to digital resources.
- Their great value is searching news archives. Normal searches usually show only recent news.
- They are useful for earlier periods and for sources that exist only on paper. Many offer digitized press with keyword search for free.
- There are specialists in historical archives. Example: the AIIP (Association of Independent Information Professionals, AIIP.org), an association of experts in information research and retrieval.

**Social Media**

- Almost every investigation can make use of some part of social media.
- It provides information that is nowhere else.
- When records, databases and news fall short, the subject's digital footprint adds another dimension.
- Four out of five Americans use at least one social network.
- The average American has more than eight accounts.
- Settling for a quick search on Facebook can mean losing a lot of information.

Usage data (Pew Research 2019; Statista.com 2020):

| Network | Users worldwide | % of US adults who use it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Facebook | 2,700 million monthly active | Almost 70% |
| Twitter | 353 million monthly active | About 22% |
| Instagram | 1,200 million monthly active | About 37% |
| LinkedIn | 700 million users | About 27% |
| YouTube | 2,000 million monthly active | About 73% |

- Many companies now specialize in online and social media investigations.
- The investigator must understand on each platform: audiences, functions, privacy settings, configurations, advantages and drawbacks.
- It is a skill that changes constantly, as do the techniques for obtaining intelligence from social networks.

**Search Engines**

- Examples from the book: Google, Bing and Yahoo.
- They allow a broad keyword search of the subject.
- They are not authoritative sources. But they find things that do not appear in public records or news, such as company websites, newsletters or event announcements.
- They make it possible to quickly gauge how much information there is about the subject at the outset.
- Caution, for two reasons:
  - Results depend on a complex mix of algorithms, settings, advertising and optimizations, which makes some pages appear higher (Ajaeb 2019).
  - They also depend on the physical location of the person searching.

**Commercial and Subscription-Based Databases**

- Only licensed professional investigators can use them.
- They are not public for two reasons: the sensitive information they contain and the laws that regulate their use.
- They are useful for obtaining background information and summary data on the subject.
- They are not exhaustive, but they give information that does not appear in search engines.
- Examples from the book: TLOxp, idiCORE, Tracers, IRB, Delvepoint and LexisNexis.

**Cautions and Considerations**

| # | Caution | What to do |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Not everything is online. In many jurisdictions records can only be obtained at the courthouse or the clerk's office | Verify everything found in online databases against other records and documents |
| 2 | Information overload. It takes a lot of time and work, and demands an attentive eye and technical skill | Make a plan, a checklist or an outline of what is relevant and of the sources to review |
| 3 | Reliability and accuracy. There are errors even in official databases and court files | Corroborate sources, look for more documents and think about what is missing from the record |
| 4 | Biases | Watch for one's own bias and that of the sources, especially on social media |
| 5 | Special skills are needed, and tools and tactics change with technology, new platforms and privacy | Dedicate budget to continuing training, or partner with specialized firms when it is cheaper |

- IU managers must evaluate their search needs and choose the most effective option for the mission and in cost.
- More information, easier access and a limited budget will lead to greater use of online sources.

#### 8.1.2 Liaison in Investigations

- It is one of the most important multipliers.
- Forms: internal or external, formal or informal, individual or organizational.
- What it allows (Webster University 2020):
  - Leveraging the resources of others.
  - Sharing best practices and lessons learned.
  - Collaborating on specific cases.
  - Better addressing common problems.
  - Sharing information, equipment and facilities. In short, doing more with less.
  - Being more proactive and adding more value to the organization.
- Investigators must be allowed and encouraged to create and maintain their own formal and informal contacts.
- Large police forces have agents all over the world with access to senior officials. Corporate investigators usually do not.
- That is why they must build professional relationships in the community, with:
  - Law enforcement.
  - Government services.
  - Security colleagues.
  - Associations.
- Those relationships yield direct results, or provide facts, evidence and witnesses that lead to them.
- Even if a large network already exists, liaison expands it and makes it more efficient.
- **Infrequent problems:** large-scale fraud, cyberattacks, workplace violence or executive misconduct.
  - Key: coordinate early and closely with internal contacts, such as the general counsel and the head of human resources.
  - External contacts, such as functional experts or consultants, are also useful to use adequate and current investigative protocols.
- **Proactive vs. reactive:** it can occur in a reactive environment (crisis or emergency), but it is above all proactive.
- Being proactive improves the IU's effectiveness and credibility and demonstrates its value.
- Many see it as essential against social problems: illegal drugs, street violence, workplace violence and fraud.
- Police, corporate personnel, security professionals and the legal community, together, better protect assets, solve crimes and prevent losses (Webster University 2020).
- **Philosophical reason:** professionalism. Professionals want to share what they know and learn from others. That strengthens the profession and one's own knowledge.

**How to Establish Liaison**

- Relationships arise from introductions, referrals, contacts (formal and social) and mutual help.
- Professional and civic associations: a great opportunity.
- Encourage security directors, IU managers and investigators to join service, community, social, sports and professional groups.
- **Emergency plan exercises:** a good place to meet those who will provide support in an emergency. Besides the crisis itself, they help to:
  - Review plans.
  - Provide awareness training.
  - Get to know the facilities.
  - Exchange general information.
- **Multilateral forums** worth fostering:
  - Local business and community security associations.
  - Industry security associations (examples from the book: health care, utilities, retail).
  - Local chapters of Infragard or similar organizations.
- If participation has to be justified to senior management: invite a representative of the forum to meet with the executives or train them.
- Professional associations are part of the public-private partnerships between police and corporate security.
- Training courses and professional development also serve to expand the network.
- **Advice from a veteran (Hudak 2005):**
  - Build relationships at different levels of the organizations.
  - On arriving in a community, pay a courtesy visit to the highest-ranking official you can at the agency. Then use that meeting as a reference with others at that agency.
  - Do not limit yourself to the police. Also visit: fire department, emergency services, utility companies, the mayor's office, the postal inspector's office, hospital security directors and any organization useful to the company.
- **Internal contacts:** introductory visit, periodic follow-up visits and exchange of information and favors.
- A crisis or a major investigation is not the time to meet colleagues for the first time.
- Suggested internal contacts (complete list, 12):
  1. Executive assistant and front office staff.
  2. General counsel.
  3. Head of procurement.
  4. Director of human resources.
  5. Facilities manager.
  6. Head of public relations or information, or equivalent.
  7. Director of accounting and finance or chief financial officer (CFO).
  8. Operations manager.
  9. Information technology (IT) manager or chief information officer (CIO).
  10. Director of security or of safety.
  11. Occupational health staff.
  12. Union or collective bargaining unit leaders.
- Depending on the size of the company, it is advisable to have contacts at several levels in each of those functions.
- **International liaison:** it is critical. Abroad, the following weigh heavily:
  - Culture.
  - The laws and rules of the host country.
  - The policies and procedures of the local police.
  - Language.
  - Knowledge of the terrain.
- Local contacts help achieve results and help the team avoid problems and keep a low profile.
- Build international relationships long before a crisis or investigation.
- Many foreign agencies have special units for: counterterrorism, cybercrime, financial crimes, import and export, and organized crime.
- Contact direct counterparts and correspond with them periodically.
- When visiting facilities in other countries, meet with counterpart investigators and learn their policies, procedures and concerns.

**How to Maintain Liaison**

- It is based on a relationship of professional trust. It requires periodic exchange and mutual benefit.

| # | Suggestion |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Each party should obtain some benefit without abusing the other. It must be mutual, though not necessarily a quid pro quo (something in exchange for something) |
| 2 | Respect the legal limits and each agency's rules on the maximum value of liaison gifts. That avoids sanctions and damage to the relationship |
| 3 | Never betray a contact's trust. It usually ends the relationship and marks the investigator and their agency for a long time |
| 4 | Maintain periodic contact even if there is nothing specific to exchange. It shows that the relationship matters and keeps familiarity |

**Joint Task Forces (JTF)**

- They are a highly structured form of liaison.
- They are used for complex and often multi-jurisdictional problems: retail theft, terrorism, cybercrime, human trafficking and serious procurement fraud.

| JTF type | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Case-specific | National Capital Area Sniper Case (2002); Unabomber Task Force |
| Longstanding | The various Joint Terrorism Task Forces; Crimes Against Children Task Force |

**Figure 8.1 — Joint Task Force Resources:**

| # | Resource |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Staffing resources |
| 2 | Intelligence from local law enforcement |
| 3 | Law enforcement databases and public resources |
| 4 | Task force member resources |

- Note: the figure text came out with a broken font. It was reconstructed from the glyph pattern. It should be checked against the book.
- Possible members of a JTF: international, federal and local agencies, industry resources, auditors, analysts, consultants and prosecutors (the book leaves the list open).
- They bring special expertise and cross jurisdictional boundaries.

| Diversity of members: advantages | Diversity of members: drawbacks |
| --- | --- |
| Many benefits and leveraging the strengths of diversity | Misunderstandings, conflicts and inefficiencies. They must be managed to offset them |

Four problems to watch for in a JTF:

| Problem | What it is | Solution |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parochialism (localism, self-interest) | Members arrive with their own agendas, personal or organizational. It causes conflict, open or subtle. It reduces effectiveness, limits results and erodes the trust of the public or of executives | JTF management |
| Protection of sensitive information | Each member sees differently what is sensitive and for how long. There are several chains of command, different policies, pressure to report to different audiences and different secure communications | Tailored OPSEC (operations security) plan, for physical and electronic information |
| Data management | The book only mentions it | — |
| Reporting chains | Their confusion spreads fragmented and apparently contradictory information about progress, successes, needs and relationships | Well-defined reporting chains, in the JTF and in each member agency |

- Exam note: the book lists four problems, but develops only three. "Data management" has no explanation.
- **Corporate application:** this advice is useful for:
  - Multidisciplinary teams or integrated product teams.
  - Collaborations among several organizations.
  - Companies and NGOs that work with police or intelligence JTFs, or that support them.
- **Summary:** liaison ranges from informal face-to-face conversations to detailed international agreements. It is always a great multiplier.
- Paraphrased quote from a former Special Agent in Charge of the largest US Air Force mainframe systems fraud detachment (Smith 1997): good liaison is like a good informant program. Without it you cannot be proactive.

#### 8.1.3 Intelligence Information

- It is a key multiplier, but in companies an organized approach to managing it is usually lacking.
- The IU manager must create a **formal intelligence management program**. It must have policies, procedures and systems to (complete list, 9):
  1. Define general and specific objectives.
  2. Gather intelligence information.
  3. Integrate and analyze the information.
  4. Organize and store the information.
  5. Share it, internally and externally, when appropriate.
  6. Retrieve the information.
  7. Control access to the data.
  8. Control access to information about methods and sources.
  9. Evaluate and improve the program periodically.
- Within the program:
  - Set general and specific objectives, with the priority topics.
  - Define strategies for obtaining and using the information.
  - Sometimes, assign intelligence requirements to specific investigators or locations.
- The possible sources depend on the type of investigation and the IU's functional focus.
- Experienced investigators detect and make better use of sources. These skills are taught in almost all basic and advanced courses.
- **Basic maxim:** "check your own files first".
  - It is common to overlook valuable internal sources, especially in large, complex or multi-site organizations.
- **Case management software:** very useful if it meets three conditions:
  - Supports exhibits and photos.
  - Allows link analysis.
  - Is on the organization's network or in the cloud, accessible to the whole IU.
- Using off-the-shelf commercial programs, many customizable, is cheaper than building them from scratch.
- **Types of sources by duration:**

| Type | Characteristics |
| --- | --- |
| One-time source of unique relevance | It may be the most important. It may be used only once, but it provides critical data to solve the case |
| Continuing long-term sources | Liaison contacts, controlled informants with more potential and confidential sources |
| Investigator's personal sources | They provide certain information regularly. They must be shared with the IU, but contact is always coordinated through the investigator who developed them |

- **Protection of methods and sources:** it is key in every intelligence program. Some are very sensitive.
- Only legal methods and sources should be used.
- Create a system of code names or numbers to hide the identity of persons, organizations or technical sources.
- That system must reduce the risk of exposure in two ways:
  - Accidental disclosure.
  - Legal processes, such as discovery.
- It is even more important if exposing the source would cause great embarrassment or a physical risk to persons or organizations.
- Sources vary according to the IU's functional focus, geographic location and industry.
- **Common intelligence sources (complete list, 21):**
  1. News media.
  2. Online sources.
  3. Internal files and historical records.
  4. Local court files and records (province, state, county, city).
  5. Criminal justice agency files.
  6. IT department records and audit trails.
  7. Hiring and procurement records.
  8. Accounts payable and receivable records.
  9. Operations and production records.
  10. Filings with regulators and regulatory bodies.
  11. Tax returns.
  12. Vehicle records.
  13. Insurers and claims history.
  14. Workplace safety and incident records.
  15. Inventory management systems.
  16. Human resources data.
  17. Personnel evaluations, awards and disciplinary files.
  18. Payroll and travel records.
  19. Medical and financial records.
  20. Security files.
  21. Licenses, permits and other local government records.
- **Legal warning:** medical, financial and other personal data records are protected by local, national and international rules.
- If obtained illegally, the information is useless. In addition, the source, the investigator and the company can be criminally prosecuted.

#### 8.1.4 Technology

- It is a multiplier because it provides four improvements:
  - Faster data analysis and fusion.
  - Better knowledge management.
  - A more predictive approach.
  - More efficient dissemination of information.
- The biggest technology multiplier: data mining and data analytics tools. They make it possible to locate, gather, organize and analyze large volumes of information.

Three ways to take advantage of them (they can be combined according to the IU's needs):

| # | Approach |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Use the functions that standard programs already include, such as basic productivity suites |
| 2 | Buy and install specific mining and analytics suites, some adapted to investigation |
| 3 | Outsource to a company specialized in data mining and analytics |

- These tools are also useful for open-source intelligence (OSINT) projects.
- OSINT is not used only to investigate. It also serves brand and reputation management and competitive intelligence.
- **Training:** virtual reality (VR) environments and collaborative or interactive simulated cases, even with participants from around the world.
- Properly organized, these trainings do not reduce human contact. They facilitate liaison among organizations, specialties and regions.
- **Relationship with IT:** the technological environment demands a close relationship among security, investigators and IT staff.
  - It must start at the top: the top security or investigations executive works with the CIO or equivalent.
  - Then it goes down through the whole organization in both functions.
- Leveraging IT staff to investigate, always within the law, benefits both and generates creative solutions.
- Example: many IT departments do pattern analysis or keystroke monitoring. That can detect insider threats or system abuses.

### 8.2 Investigative Documentation

#### 8.2.1 Report Writing

- **Investigative report:** written document in which the investigator records what was done and the evidence gathered.
- Even if it is not the final report, it can provide leads to other investigators.
- It serves as the basis for later administrative actions, such as a court proceeding or a disciplinary sanction.
- The reader must quickly understand what the investigator did, why, and with what result.
- Even the most careful investigation loses value if it is not reported well.

The five basic steps:

| # | Step |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Gather the facts |
| 2 | Record the facts immediately |
| 3 | Organize the facts |
| 4 | Write the report |
| 5 | Evaluate or correct the report, if needed |

**Types of Reports**

| Type | When and for what purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Initial report | Submitted a few days after the investigation is opened. It describes progress and any pending leads. In a simple case it can also be the final or closing report |
| Progress report (or interim) | A private investigation can last months; a public one, years. It is submitted at fixed intervals with progress and pending leads. In the private sector the normal interval is **30 days** |
| Special report | Documents an action outside the normal course of the investigation. It can be a supplement to the progress report, but it must be readable on its own. Example: a report on a surveillance in an undercover investigation |
| Final report | It must be logical. It is submitted in three cases: (1) the investigation is closed successfully; (2) all leads were followed without success and continuing would not be productive; (3) the person or office that authorized opening the case orders it |

**Elements of the Report**

- The format varies widely between organizations.
- One method is to divide it by topic, such as statements or surveillance logs.
- It may be required on paper or in electronic format. Sometimes a "certified" copy, signed by hand, is requested.
- Whatever the format, it must contain the relevant information obtained and five parts:

| Part | Content and rules |
| --- | --- |
| Administrative information | The report must be understandable without consulting others. It includes: (1) name of the subject of the investigation; (2) case or file number; (3) type of case, if the number does not indicate it; (4) case status, open or closed; (5) reporting investigator; (6) date of the report |
| Summary or synopsis | It goes at the beginning for a quick read. Rule: each important point in the report gets at least one sentence in the summary. Supporting facts are not cited. Conclusions are allowed if they fairly reflect the detail in the body |
| Narrative | It details the work in chronological order, with the facts of the case. Each step goes in a separate paragraph. It must be clear, simple, and brief, with easy vocabulary. Avoid technical terms or, at least, define them. The narrative of the undercover operative is in the **first person** |
| Conclusions | Some organizations ask for conclusions and recommendations. Example: after a fraud, improve controls; after a burglary, more physical security (surveys, alarms, guards, and video surveillance). But the investigator is above all a collector of evidence. The appropriate authority decides on judicial or administrative actions |
| Enclosures | At a minimum: (1) the initial complaint; (2) statements from the complainant and the witnesses; (3) written statements from others interviewed; (4) documentation of the evidence and results of its analysis (physical, documentary, electronic, and technical); (5) surveillance logs, if applicable; (6) photos, audio, or video |

**Distribution and Protection of Investigative Reports**

- The report may have to be disclosed in the court process of discovery.
- In discovery, the unit can be forced to hand over the report and its files to the opposing party.
- For that reason it must be kept free of extraneous and irrelevant material, especially names of people and places unrelated to the case.
- **Protection technique:** give it only to the company's attorney of record, who keeps it.
  - That way it will probably be considered attorney work product and be protected from a subpoena.
  - It is not foolproof. But obtaining it usually requires a court order after a hearing.
- **Qualified privilege:** a well-controlled report does not usually give rise to a defamation lawsuit, even if it contains false data.
  - The exception: proof that the false data was included with malice.
- Even so, avoid opinions and conclusions of the investigator.
- The content should be statements by others or personal observations of the investigator, identified as such.
- Exam note: the book says that some organizations ask for conclusions and recommendations. It then advises not including opinions or conclusions of the investigator.
- **Distribution, the final step of the report:**
  - Only to those with a real need to know.
  - It may contain damaging information about employees or other people, or statements by them.
  - Control copies closely.
  - Whoever receives a copy or keeps a file must have adequate security protection. They must be told that it is sensitive and given handling instructions.
- If the report turns out to contain inaccurate and damaging material, the qualified privilege may be lost for having distributed it more than necessary.
- Many organizations distribute reports only through their in-house or outside attorney, so that they are protected by attorney-client privilege.

| Protection | How it is achieved |
| --- | --- |
| Attorney work product | Give the report only to the company's attorney of record, who keeps it |
| Qualified privilege | Control the report and distribute it only on a need-to-know basis. It is lost with excessive distribution |
| Attorney-client privilege | Distribute only through the in-house or outside attorney |

#### 8.2.2 Other Reporting Formats

- Some purposes or types of investigation use other formats.
- A simple investigation of an incident, done by a security supervisor, may be documented in the incident report itself.
- Preemployment and background investigations may use somewhat different formats, as set by the organization or the authority that requests them.

#### 8.2.3 Testifying and Depositions

- In almost all fields, investigators are called to testify in judicial or administrative proceedings.
- The book refers to Chapter 7.

### 8.3 Leveraging Investigative Results

- The natural end of an investigation is usually:
  - Gathering all the relevant facts.
  - Documenting the findings.
  - Deciding on the disciplinary sanction or restitution.
- But that should not be the end.
- Investigations almost always reveal something valuable. For example, a condition or weakness that:
  - Made it possible to commit a crime.
  - Gave unauthorized access.
  - Created an opportunity for malicious activity.
  - Produced unintended hazards.
  - Allowed another anomaly.
- Even background investigations allow trend analysis:
  - To detect changes in typical "red flags" or in indications of unsuitability.
  - To see how they relate to adjudication standards and whether they need to be updated.
- The organization needs mechanisms to leverage all findings and improve its processes. They should not be wasted.

Two complementary objectives:

| # | Objective | Section |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Improve the organization's investigative processes or capabilities | 8.3.1 |
| 2 | Improve the security posture (security and safety) and the ability to manage current and future risks | 8.3.2 |

#### 8.3.1 Improving the Investigative Process

- According to the software company Forensic Notes (2020), when a workplace investigation ends, human resources and the organization should analyze it. That way they see what to change for the future and what could have been done better.
- Two questions it proposes: do policies need to be updated? Is more training needed?
- Additional questions from the book (complete list, 7):
  1. Are we using our investigative staff and resources efficiently? How can we improve?
  2. Are we using external or subcontracted resources efficiently and effectively? How can we improve?
  3. Was any tool or capability missing? Do we need to buy or budget for new equipment, services, or capabilities?
  4. Did we coordinate well with other departments? The book cites: legal, human resources, CIO, finance, and facilities.
  5. Was information shared with the right areas at the right time?
  6. Did new liaison opportunities, internal or external, or new contacts arise?
  7. Were there leaks, actual or potential, of sensitive information during the investigation? If so, how can they be prevented in the future?

#### 8.3.2 Improving the Organization's Security and Safety Posture

- The aim is to improve the processes by which the organization manages security and safety risk together.
- The strategy is summarized in the ASIS Information Asset Protection guideline.
  - Exam note: the book cites it as "forthcoming," that is, not yet published.
- Every finding that shows a condition, policy, practice, or vulnerability that puts assets at risk must be documented and corrected.
  - This includes anything that could cause or worsen a reputation or brand risk.
  - This achieves continuous improvement in protection and risk management, and mitigates known threats and vulnerabilities.
- **Periodic review of reports**, internal or from outside agencies, to extract useful information on:
  - Security vulnerabilities.
  - Safety hazards.
  - Attack methods, and the adversary's capabilities, intent, and objectives.
  - Insider threats.
  - Criminal intelligence.
- That information serves three purposes:
  - Updating business policies and practices.
  - Improving security procedures.
  - Opening new investigations into matters that had not come to light.
- **Post-investigation review:** in many cases it is advisable. In practice it becomes a root cause analysis, if the report does not already include one.
- **Root cause, according to the ASQ (American Society for Quality):** the factor responsible for a nonconformance. It is the underlying problem that sets in motion the whole chain of cause and effect that ends in the problem or problems.
- The root cause is often what made the investigation necessary.
- **Root cause analysis:** uses approaches, tools, and techniques, from informal to sophisticated, to find the true cause.

| Root cause tool | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| Change analysis | Investigates what changed and when, and how it led to the action: a crime, a security violation, a safety incident, or another loss. The change can be in people, equipment, a vendor, a procedure, a facility, or any other variable (the book leaves it that open) |
| Timelines | A useful tool for change analysis |
| Barrier analysis | Reviews which controls exist, and may have failed, to prevent or detect the problem (ASQ 2020) |

| Investigation | Root cause analysis |
| --- | --- |
| Determines what happened and how | Determines exactly why it happened and how to prevent it from happening again |

- Root cause analysis can lead to (complete list, 4):
  1. Improvements in physical, personnel, information, and cyber security programs.
  2. Better application of enterprise security risk management (ESRM).
  3. Better-informed business decisions.
  4. Improvements in the business and operating practices themselves.
- The results also help to:
  - Restore employee morale after a high-profile incident.
  - Answer questions from employees, the public, and other stakeholders.
  - Explain what corrective measures will be taken and how they will affect operations.
- **The essential point:** correct every failure discovered. What is discovered in a workplace investigation is on record and must be addressed.
- Failing to correct it can lead to future legal action if the same mistakes are repeated (Forensic Notes 2020).

### 8.4 Projections

Four themes that emerge from the history of investigations (seen at the start of the book):

| # | Theme | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The line between public and private investigation is blurring | Seen in Pinkerton and Burns and throughout history. Today, in the early 21st century, the private sector and the police depend more and more on each other |
| 2 | Strong and constant relationship between security and investigation (Webster University 2020) | Examples: the deterrent (security) value of investigating; the investigation function assigned to the security department in many companies; companies that offer both services: Pinkerton, Burns, Wackenhut, Wells Fargo, G4S, and Securitas |
| 3 | Accusations of corruption, frequent in private agencies and in police forces | Ethics must be kept high in reality and in image. Ethics gives credibility, and credibility gives effectiveness. The investigator follows a code of professional conduct. His or her credibility on the witness stand depends on not taking shortcuts against ethical safeguards. An investigator is worth only as much as the case he or she is working now |
| 4 | The "current" problems were already a concern in the past | See the list below |

- Exam note: possible typo. The text says "shortcuts that comprise ethical safeguards." The logical reading would be "compromise" (shortcuts that compromise the safeguards).
- Exam note: the text cites "Webster University 2020" several times (also in 8.1.2), but it does not appear in the chapter's references. The references include "Peterson 2020, The Liaison Advantage," which the text does not cite.

Historical problems that guide the investigation function (complete list, 12):

1. Terrorism (example: England in the 19th century).
2. Burglaries and street crime.
3. Violent crimes.
4. Executive protection needs.
5. Economic and white-collar crime.
6. Corporate asset protection needs.
7. International and cross-border crimes.
8. Major thefts and crimes against cargo or goods in transit.
9. Organized crime networks.
10. Theft of information and business intelligence.
11. Corporate compliance.
12. Competitive intelligence and espionage.

- The tools and techniques of these crimes change, especially with digitalization. But the crimes remain relevant from a risk management standpoint.
- Advances in science, technology, legal theory, and international affairs will continue to have influence.
- Scientific advances will improve:
  - Investigative tools.
  - Evidence collection and analysis techniques.
  - Surveillance capabilities.
  - Database and knowledge management.
  - Information-sharing mechanisms.
- The growing dependence on IT will require IUs to have more IT and computer forensics knowledge, in-house or subcontracted.
- **More self-reliance:** companies, organizations, and communities will increasingly cover their own security and investigation needs.
  - According to the International Foundation of Protection Officers (Davies 2015), because of budget and other limitations, the police and society will expect companies to investigate their own white-collar crimes themselves.
- **Homeland security:** it weighs heavily in public and corporate investigation.
  - Cooperation and information sharing among federal, state, local, and international agencies, and between government and the private sector, is key.
  - The critical infrastructure protection efforts of the late 1990s brought federal agencies and companies closer together. They continue today, because threats are growing.
  - From them came the ISACs (Information Sharing and Analysis Centers), semiformal and organized by key sector.
  - Today these relationships are more productive under the umbrella of homeland security.
  - Examples of public-private partnerships: the FBI's (Federal Bureau of Investigation) Infragard program and the U.S. State Department's OSAC (Overseas Security Advisory Council).
  - These relationships, and the trust they are based on, must continue to grow.

**Trends that will continue in the 21st century (summary from the book):**

1. More investigative responsibilities and capabilities in the private sector.
2. Close relationship between investigation and security risk management or asset protection.
3. Emphasis on ethics and professionalism, also in the professional investigative services industry.
4. Rapid incorporation of new scientific advances.
5. Growing weight of the information sciences: database and knowledge management, and technological mechanisms for sharing information.

- This will affect the prior education, training, requirements, and use of investigative personnel, and the use of outside experts.
- Even so, there will continue to be heavy reliance on traditional techniques:
  - Interviews.
  - Human sources.
  - Physical surveillance.
  - Report writing.

### Key Terms

force multiplier · investigative unit (IU) · "If You See Something, Say Something" · online information sources · surface web / clear web · deep web · dark web · Tor · public records databases · PACER · tax liens · judgments · regulatory databases · due diligence · SEC · OFAC · FTC · EPA · OSHA · FEC · State Licensing Boards · archival research · AIIP · social media footprint · search engines · commercial and subscription-based databases · information overload · liaison · Infragard · multilateral forums · courtesy visit · quid pro quo · liaison gifts · joint task force (JTF) · case-specific vs. longstanding JTF · parochialism · OPSEC · reporting chains · intelligence management program · "check your own files first" · case management software · link analysis · controlled informants · confidential sources · code names · discovery · data mining · data analytics · data fusion · OSINT · keystroke monitoring · insider threats · investigative report · initial / progress / special / final report · administrative information · synopsis · narrative · conclusions · enclosures · attorney of record · attorney work product · subpoena · qualified privilege · defamation · malice · need to know · attorney-client privilege · trend analysis · red flags · adjudication standards · post-investigation review · root cause · root cause analysis · barrier analysis · timelines · ESRM · computer forensics · homeland security · ISAC · OSAC
