### Appendix 2A — Chronological Report

**What it is:** the operative's basic daily report in chronological or timeline format. The operative writes it at the end of the shift.

**Header and fields:**

| Field | Example value |
| --- | --- |
| Issuer | The XYZ Investigation Company / Daily Report |
| Marking | \*\*\* CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION \*\*\* |
| Agent | A1234B (a code, not a name) |
| Assignment | 000-000-000 |
| Day / Date | Thursday, 08/20/20 |
| Closing | ***END OF REPORT*** |

**Entries** (24-hour time, in capital letters, in the third person "OPERATIVE"):

| Time | Event |
| --- | --- |
| 0600 | Arrives and clocks in. |
| 0745 | On a break, sees Ben Richards (employee, suspect #1) and Mike Miller (employee, suspect #2) meet suspiciously. |
| 1000 | Unscheduled break. It is said that slacking is no longer possible because supervisor Chris Watts (employee, suspect #3) is back. Watts returned from vacation on Wednesday 08/19/20. Watts said there would be a 12-hour shift on Friday. |
| 1200 | Clocks out and goes to lunch with Mike Kilner (employee). |
| 1310 | Leaves his area to talk with John (LNU, last name unknown; janitor). |
| 1315 | Returns to his area. It was not break time; the normal break is from 1345 to 1355. |
| 1315 | Buys marijuana from Ronny Sanders (employee, suspect #7). Refers to "Incident Report #7". |
| 1450 | Cleans his area. |
| 1500 | Clocks out and leaves. |
| 1530 | Goes to J.J.'s Bar & Grill and drinks beer with several employees. |
| 1600 | Leaves J.J.'s. |

**What it teaches as a model:**

- The facts go in logical sequence and each incident is found quickly by the time.
- It labels each person (employee, suspect #n) and refers to separate incident reports.
- It uses the third person ("operative"), but in telegraphic style. That is why the book says that going from 2A to 2C would require a lot of "red ink."
- Compare with the rule of avoiding vague terms: "suspiciously" does not describe what was seen.

**Figures:** shift from 0600 to 1500; normal break from 1345 to 1355; 12-hour shift announced for Friday; suspects #1, #2, #3, and #7; Incident Report #7.

**Contradictions:**

- Exam note: 2A says the operative bought marijuana from Ronny Sanders at 13:15. 2B, at that time, says Sanders sold to Mike Lance and the operative only watched.
- Exam note: 2A puts lunch at 12:00; 2B records off the clock from 10:00 to 11:00.
- Exam note: 2A presents the 10:00 event as a talk during an unscheduled break. 2B presents it as an announcement by Watts to the entire production department.
- Exam note: 2A calls Mike Miller "employee, suspect #2." 2B identifies him as a production supervisor.
- Exam note: it says the normal break is from 13:45 to 13:55, but it records a break at 07:45 without marking it as unscheduled.
- Exam note: it refers to an "Incident Report #7," a type of report that the chapter does not describe.
- 2A does not include the absenteeism conversation at 14:10 that is in 2B.

### Appendix 2B — Narrative Report

**What it is:** the operative's daily report in structured narrative format. It is one of the most popular formats. It is chronological, with more detail, a conversational tone, and in the third person.

**Header and fields:**

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Case Name | Diamond Back |
| Case Number | 000-000-000 |
| Date | Thursday, August 20, 2020 |
| On Clock | 6:00 a.m.–3:00 p.m. (8.0) |
| Off Clock | 10:00 a.m.–11:00 a.m. (1.0) |
| Off Clock | 3:00 p.m.–4:00 p.m. (1.0) |
| Off Clock | 0.5 (report) |
| Expenses | Social Activities: $20 for beer and tip |
| Title | OPERATIVE DAILY REPORT |
| Closing | Submitted by: Operative code name / ***End of Report*** |

**Entries** (each is an independent paragraph with a thematic heading):

| Heading | Time and place | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Suspected Drug Activity | Around 7:45 a.m., production area | Ben Richards (insertion operator, temporary) leaves his machine accompanied by Mike Miller (production supervisor). They enter the supply room and close the door for about 3 minutes. Richards returns to his machine and Miller leaves the department. The operative plans to talk with Richards the next day. |
| Message from Management | Around 10:00 a.m., production area | Chris Watts (floor supervisor) announces a mandatory 12-hour shift on Friday, 21-Aug-2020, due to excess orders. He warns that no one should be absent or late. About 15 employees complain. Watts says it is normal to work long hours in the first quarter of the year. |
| False Time Records | Around 1:10 p.m., production area | John (last name unknown, janitor, temporary) is seen punching three time cards. He says they belong to other janitors who were arriving late. No further questions are asked about his attitude. Description: white male, about 22 years old, 6'1", 200 pounds, short brown hair, brown eyes, blue janitor coveralls and white sneakers. |
| Marijuana Sale | Around 1:15 p.m., receiving department | Ronny Sanders (receiving employee) hands 1/16 ounce of "bud" (marijuana) to Mike Lance (forklift operator) for $20, in two $10 bills. Lance puts it in his right pocket and leaves through dock door 11. Sanders says Lance buys from him at least once a week at work. Tony Lucas (receiving manager) calls Sanders to dock door 14 and the conversation is cut off. |
| Absenteeism | Around 2:10 p.m., production area | John says he will call in sick on Saturday, 22-Aug-2020, to go elk hunting with friends who are not employees. He says he is often absent. His physical description is repeated. |
| Sabotage | Around 3:30 p.m., J.J.'s Bar & Grill (Cedar Springs, Colorado); dated "Thursday, 26-Aug-2005" | Kenny Sanchez (truck driver) says that he and two other drivers often puncture tires so they do not have to go to certain destinations. He has done it 3 times in 12 months. He changes the subject when Randy Clark (shipping manager) sits down. |

**What it teaches as a model:**

- Third person ("the operative") or passive voice ("was observed"): it can be edited without having to be authenticated again.
- Each paragraph is independent and carries a thematic heading: it can be grouped and ordered by topic in the summary.
- Each paragraph opens with the date, approximate time, and place. It answers who, what, when, where, how, and why.
- Complete physical descriptions and concrete data (quantity, price, bills, doors): it avoids vague terms.
- It records hours on and off the clock, report-writing time, and expenses. That is useful for billing.
- It captures facts unrelated to the objective (false time punches, absenteeism, sabotage): this is the unexpected information that planning must anticipate.
- Each entry ends by saying that no further information was obtained.

**Figures:**

- Schedule: 8.0 hours on the clock (6:00 to 15:00 is 9 hours, minus 1.0 off the clock). Two off-clock blocks of 1.0 hour. 0.5 hours for the report. Expense of $20.
- Events: 12-hour shift announced; about 15 employees complaining; about 3 minutes in the room; three cards; John about 22 years old, 6'1", and 200 pounds.
- Marijuana sale: 1/16 ounce for $20 in two $10 bills; dock doors 11 and 14; purchases "at least once a week."
- Sabotage: 3 punctures in 12 months; two other drivers.

**Contradictions:**

- Exam note: the "Sabotage" entry is dated "Thursday, August 26, 2005"; the report is from Thursday, 20-Aug-2020. It is a typo in the example.
- Exam note: Watts says it is normal to work long hours "in the first quarter of the year," but the report is from August.
- Exam note: according to 2B, the operative saw a sale from Sanders to Lance. According to 2A, the operative bought from Sanders.
- Exam note: it marks off the clock from 10:00 to 11:00 a.m. But "Message from Management" places the operative in the production area at 10:00. And 2A puts lunch at 12:00.
- Exam note: the text says that some investigators begin each paragraph with an alphanumeric code. 2B uses thematic headings, not codes.
- Exam note: the case is called "Diamond Back" with number 000-000-000. In 2C and 2D it is "Diamond-Back" with number 000-000-0000.

### Appendix 2C — Investigative Summary Report

**What it is:** the summary that the project manager prepares for the client. It replaces the daily reports. It gathers the information from the dailies and arranges it by topic, in the third person.

**Structure and fields:**

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Addressee | The Very Big XYZ Company, Any Street, Any Town, USA 55555 |
| Reference | Re: Diamond-Back, 000-000-0000 |
| Title | INVESTIGATIVE SUMMARY REPORT / \*\*\* CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION \*\*\* |
| Facility | Cedar Springs facility |
| Period | From Monday 17-Aug-2020 to Friday 21-Aug-2020 |
| Scope | Sale of cocaine and "talk" about marijuana use and sale. Also physical and workplace security issues (security/safety), employee misconduct, and company-employee relations. |
| Results block | "To date our efforts have yielded" and "Additionally, we have identified" |
| Thematic sections | COCAINE SALE; SUSPECTED DRUG ACTIVITY |
| Closing | ***End of Report*** |

**Cumulative results:**

| Item | Number |
| --- | --- |
| Cocaine purchases during working hours and on company premises | 12 |
| Cocaine purchases during working hours but off company premises | 6 |
| Marijuana purchases during working hours and on company premises | 7 |
| Employees who use cocaine during working hours and on company premises | 16 |
| Employees who use marijuana during working hours and on company premises | 12 |

**Cocaine Sale (Tuesday 18-Aug-2020): sequence and chain of custody**

| Time | Event |
| --- | --- |
| Around 11:35 | In the rail assembly area, Ben Richards (insertion operator, temporary) signals to the operative to go out through the door behind the "rail blaster." He takes a cigar wrapper out of his left shirt pocket and empties out a large white rock wrapped in cellophane. He says it is so pure it can be smoked in a pipe as is. He says it is better than the "stuff" (cocaine) he sold him on Friday 7-Aug-2020. |
| Around 11:40 | The operative takes possession of the cocaine. |
| Around 11:45 | He puts it in an evidence bag left to him by Special Agent Kenny Berger. He locks it in the evidence box in the trunk of his car. He goes to First State Bank to meet Berger. |
| Around 12:00 | He hands over the locked box. Berger opens it, takes out the bag, inspects it, and gives him a receipt (Exhibit A). He returns the box to him open. He lets him photograph the package after examining it (Exhibit C). Berger keeps the only key to the box. |
| Around 12:10 | The operative leaves the bank parking lot and returns to the plant. |
| Around 12:35 | In the rail assembly area he pays Richards $300 and closes the purchase. He uses three $100 bills from the Colorado State Police Narcotics Task Force. Serial numbers: AB67547008I, AL04508146D, and AJ35735194A (Exhibit B). |

**Suspected Drug Activity:**

- Thursday 13-Aug-2020, around 11:00 a.m., rail assembly. Richards says that Chris Watts (floor supervisor) uses crystal methamphetamine because it leaves the body in two or three days.
- He adds that when Watts cut his hand at work and was given stitches, he was not given a drug test like other injured workers.
- Thursday 20-Aug-2020, around 7:45 a.m. It repeats almost word for word the "Suspected Drug Activity" entry from 2B: Richards and Miller about 3 minutes in the supply room. It removes the sentence about the operative's follow-up.

**What it teaches as a model:**

- It reorders the information by topic, not by day. That makes it more readable for the team.
- The third-person text of the dailies carries over into the summary without being rewritten (compare with 2B).
- It begins with totals in figures that are useful for decision-making.
- It shows how to coordinate a drug purchase with the police: evidence bag, locked box, handover to the agent, receipt, photo, sole key, and bills with recorded serial numbers.
- It applies the guidelines "coordinate with law enforcement" and "avoid actual drug use": the operative does not smoke it.
- It uses Exhibits (A, B, and C) as evidentiary enclosures.
- Compare with the guideline "buy down, not up": the example does not give the weight, only speaks of a "large rock" for $300.

**Contradictions:**

- Exam note: the stated period is from 17 to 21 August, but it includes an event from 13 August.
- Exam note: it covers five working days, but the text says the typical summary is made after 10 consecutive days of work.
- Exam note: the introduction speaks only of "talk" about marijuana, but the totals include 7 marijuana purchases.
- Exam note: 2D describes the same cocaine purchase dated Tuesday 11-Aug-2020; 2C says Tuesday 18-Aug-2020.
- Exam note: the case number has 10 digits (000-000-0000), versus 9 in 2A and 2B.
- Nuance: the text (2.6.2) says the file name must not carry a code name or case number. The sample reports do show them in the header; the rule is about the file name.

### Appendix 2D — Special Report

**What it is:** a report on a special or out-of-the-ordinary event. It has no fixed format. According to the text, it was made easily from 2C. If it may serve as criminal evidence, the operative reviews and signs it.

**Structure and fields:**

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Addressee | The Very Big XYZ Company, Any Street, Any Town, USA 55555 |
| Reference | Re: Diamond-Back, 000-000-0000 |
| Title | SPECIAL REPORT #16 |
| Body | Chronological account of the cocaine purchase |
| Closing | ***End of Report*** + signature line "Confidential Informant #1" and "Date" |

**Content:**

- It is the same event, with the same sequence of times, as the "Cocaine Sale" section of 2C: 11:35, 11:40, 11:45, 12:00, 12:10, and 12:35.
- Same $300 in three $100 bills, same serial numbers, and same Exhibits A, B, and C.
- Changes compared with 2C:
  - Date: Tuesday 11-Aug-2020 (in 2C, 18-Aug-2020).
  - The operative is called "Confidential Informant #1" instead of "the operative."
  - It adds the description of Richards: Caucasian male, 5'11", 200 pounds, shoulder-length brown hair, blue eyes, and a large blue dragon tattoo on the left forearm.
  - It includes a line for the informant's signature and date.

**What it teaches as a model:**

- A special event is documented separately, ready to be used as evidence.
- It is put together by reusing the third-person text of the summary.
- The operative signs it, here as a confidential informant, when it may be criminal evidence.
- It includes a complete physical description of the suspect, useful for identifying him.
- Special reports are numbered in sequence (#16).

**Contradictions:**

- Exam note: the same event has a different date than in 2C (11 versus 18 August 2020).
- Exam note: the text says 2D comes "easily" from 2C, but it changes the date, changes the operative's name, and adds the description.
- Exam note: the chapter does not explain why the operative comes to be called "Confidential Informant #1."

### Appendix 3A — Sample Due Diligence Investigation Checklist

Suggested checklist for acquiring a company (22 categories).

| Category | Items |
| --- | --- |
| Corporate Organization | Articles of incorporation · Bylaws · Recent changes in corporate structure · Shareholder list (shares outstanding and percentage of each, plus stock option or stock appreciation rights plans) · Parent, subsidiaries, and affiliates · Shareholder agreements · Minutes of board meetings |
| Business Information | Product offering · Depreciation method · Patents · Management information system |
| Business Plan | Most recent five-year business plan · Previous business plan |
| Capital Expenditures | Last five years · Gross five-year projection · Detailed list of priorities, with all possible analysis |
| Competition | By product line: name, address, phone, company size, size of the competing unit, market share, competitive advantages and disadvantages · Industry publications: name, address, and phone numbers |
| Contracts | Main contracts by product line, with their terms |
| Debt and Leases | Lender · Terms · Interest rate · Payment schedule |
| Employee Relations | Unions: name of the union, name of the local president, address, phone, and copy of the contract |
| Environmental Liabilities | Description · Environmental studies · Projected five-year remediation cost · Projected five-year compliance cost |
| Equipment | List · Age · Appraisal (orderly liquidation value and replacement value) |
| Financial Statements | Five years of audited statements: income statement, balance sheet, cash flow, and changes in equity · Internal monthly statements for the current year · Most recent five-year projections · Monthly sales projections with seasonality |
| Information Technology & Security | IT policies and procedures · Electronic records retention policies · Cybersecurity infrastructure · Other |
| Insurance | Property · Liability · Workers' compensation · Other |
| Last Corporate Transactions | Description of each · Purchase and sale agreement for each |
| Litigation | Current: description and possible damages · Potential: description and possible damages |
| Management | Organization chart · Résumés · Ownership interest · Compensation and contracts · Performance evaluation criteria · Profit-sharing policies |
| Marketing | Pricing strategy · Patents · Distribution channels · Promotion tactics · Customer base (top 10 by product line, with their volume) · Market share by product line |
| Operating Data | Accounts receivable: turnover or days, aging, credit control and policy, seasonality · Inventory: turnover or days, obsolescence policy, sources of supply, valuation method · Backlog: by product line, five-year history, current, seasonal issues |
| Pension Plans | Funding status · Balance sheet treatment · Projected ten-year cash expense |
| Property | Description · Recent acquisitions or spin-offs · Expansion plans |
| Retiree Medical Benefits Liabilities | Funding status · Balance sheet treatment · Projected ten-year cash expense |
| Tax Status | Historical tax rate · Net operating losses |

- Exam note: "Patents" appears twice, in Business Information and in Marketing.

### Appendix 4A — Sample Application Form

This is a sample form from a security guard company in the U.S. Header: "Please Print Clearly."

| Section | Fields |
| --- | --- |
| General information | Name (last, first, middle) · Date · Name changes? · Date you can start · Position applied for · Current address (street, city, state, ZIP code) · Time at that address · Phone · Can you legally work in the U.S.? (Yes/No) · Are you 18 or older? Over 21? · Have you worked for us before? (Yes/No) · How did you hear about us? · Experience, skills, or qualities of special value for the position · Pay you need at the start ($ per hour) |
| How to contact you? | Email · Home phone · Cell phone · Message phone no. 1 (check if it belongs to a friend, neighbor, or relative) |
| Education | Three "High School" lines, each with city and state, years attended, and "Did you graduate?" |
| Military service | Were you in the U.S. Armed Forces? (Yes/No) · Branch · Dates · Do you have the DD-214? (Yes/No; the book does not explain what it is) · Did you receive military training useful for the position? |
| Physical requirements of the security job | The job involves walking, standing, lifting, climbing ladders and stairs, and prolonged moderate physical activity. Also night and weekend work in bad weather, close attention to detail, and quick and effective response to emergencies. The applicant is asked to indicate any reason why he or she cannot meet them. |
| Previous employment | All positions in the last 10 years, from the most recent to the oldest. Includes temporary jobs, contract work, and unpaid volunteering. Use another sheet if needed. Explain all gaps. Columns: employer · phone/address · from (mm/dd/yy) · to (mm/dd/yy) · position · reason for leaving · May we contact this employer? (Yes/No). Six lines. |
| Personal references | Three references who are NOT former employers or relatives: name and occupation · email · years known · phone |
| Consent for background investigation | The company does a rigorous check of all candidates. It may include tests of all the requirements for the guard license and for the job: criminal history, credit, drugs, driving records (DMV), Social Security number verification, references, and physical condition. All in accordance with the law and contracts with clients. The candidate gives consent: name, signature, and date. |
| Criminal history | Note: they are requested only because the licensing department requires it for the guard license. They are not used against the applicant unless they leave him or her without a license. Question: do you have open criminal cases or were you ever convicted? Includes pleading guilty or being found guilty, even with a deferred or suspended sentence already completed (Yes/No). If the answer is yes: details and official court documents (court, offense code, classification, plea or verdict, sentence, and final disposition). Explanation, name, signature, and date. |
| Driver's license and identification | Name as it appears on the license (first, middle, last) · License number · State · Expiration date · Address on the license · Social Security number · Email · Date of birth · Name and current address |
| Application Agreement | Certifies that the data is correct to the best of his or her knowledge · Falsifying it is grounds for dismissal · "At will" employment: the company or the employee can end it at any time, with or without cause or notice, paying only what has already been earned · Only the chairman of the board or the CEO can agree otherwise, in writing and signed · Authorizes contacting references about work habits, skills, and professional conduct, unless otherwise indicated · Authorizes the background investigation by the company and its agents: employment, education, driving history, criminal history (as required by the license), and public records · Signature and date · Application reviewer |

Contradictions with the chapter text:

| Topic | What the chapter says | What the form does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FCRA authorization | The notice and written authorization must be separate from the application. | It puts the consent and authorization inside the application. |
| Ban-the-Box | Many places prohibit asking about criminal history on the application until a conditional offer. | It asks about convictions on the application itself, justifying it by the guard license. |
| Scope of the criminal question | Convictions, guilty pleas, and nolo contendere, over a period (for example 7 or 10 years). | It asks about any conviction "ever," with no time limit or mention of nolo contendere. It does include deferred or suspended sentences. |
| A conviction alone does not disqualify | The application must say that a conviction is not an automatic rejection. | It says it will not be used against the applicant unless it prevents the license. This matches only in part. |
| Omissions and falsehoods | The application must say that omitting or falsifying something material ends the process when it is discovered. | It only says that falsifying is grounds for dismissal. It does not mention omissions. |
| Scope of the authorization | It must also be valid for future screenings for retention, promotion, or change of position. | It does not mention this. |
| Addresses | It asks for all previous addresses in the period under review. | It only asks for the current address, how long the applicant has lived there, and the one on the license. |
| Education | For each level: school and address, program, dates, final status, degree, grade average, and reason for withdrawal. | Only three high school lines, with years attended and whether the applicant graduated. There is no higher education. |
| Licenses and certifications | They must be requested and verified with the issuer. | There is no section, except for the driver's license. |
| Data on each job | Starting and final position, and name and phone of the last direct supervisor. | A single position field and no supervisor. The 10-year period does fit the text (5, 7, or 10). |
| References | At least three, known for a year or more, not relatives. Work and home addresses, and how they know the applicant. A former boss is an excellent reference. | It excludes former employers. It does not ask for addresses, or how they know the applicant, or a minimum of one year. It adds the occupation. |
| Military service | Period, branch, type of discharge, and rank at discharge. Ask whether the discharge was honorable or dishonorable. | It asks for branch, dates, whether the applicant has the DD-214, and training. It does not ask for type of discharge or rank. |
| Restricted questions | In the U.S., Australia, and Canada, certain questions about age, disability, and other data are prohibited. | It asks whether the applicant is 18 or 21, the date of birth, and whether he or she can meet physical requirements. |

### Appendix 6A — Crime Lab Organizations

**Criteria for choosing a laboratory or a provider**

- Assess the credibility, reputation, and reliability of the laboratories and of the personnel or companies that process and examine the evidence.
- Also assess the provider's specialty, capabilities, or area of expertise.
- Vet potential providers well before they are needed.
- In the middle of an investigation there is usually no time to investigate, do due diligence, and choose a provider.
- One way to find a professional laboratory is through an accreditation or professional organization.

**Organizations cited in the book (9)**

| # | Organization | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | American Academy of Forensic Sciences | Apply science to law |
| 2 | American Board of Forensic Entomology | Promote the use of insect evidence to clarify matters of legal interest, often a crime |
| 3 | American Society of Crime Laboratory Directors | Pursue excellence in forensic science through leadership and innovation |
| 4 | Association for Crime Scene Reconstruction | Encourage the exchange of information and procedures useful for reconstructing crime scenes |
| 5 | Canadian Society of Forensic Science | Maintain professional standards and promote the study of forensic science |
| 6 | The Chartered Society of Forensic Sciences | United Kingdom organization that applies science to the cause of justice |
| 7 | International Association for Identification | Support those who work in forensic identification, investigation, and scientific examination of physical evidence |
| 8 | International Association of Arson Investigators | Promote fire investigation standards |
| 9 | International Crime Scene Investigators Association | Assist police who process crime scenes |

### Appendix 6B — Sample Policy on Computer Evidence

**Part 1. Seizure and Disconnection of Computer Equipment**

1. Isolate the system, photograph it, and document how its computer, telephone, and power connections were found. Confirm that it is not deleting data and look for physical traps.
2. Always wear latex gloves when handling computers and related equipment in a criminal investigation.
3. Do not use fingerprint powder on the equipment.
4. If the computer is off, leave it off.
5. If it is on, do not touch the keyboard or let anyone touch it. Note the applications open on the screen. If necessary, the mouse can be moved to wake the screen, but do NOT open files or applications.
6. On standalone equipment, do not shut down the system the normal way. Unplug first from the back of the machine and then from the power outlet.
   - a. If it is a laptop that is on, remove the battery first and then disconnect it from power.
   - b. On networked systems, a computer crimes investigator from the department will come to help.
7. Photograph the back of the computer to record the cable connections.
8. Label all cables, indicating which cable goes to which port on each device.
9. Photograph and document all connected devices, such as printers.
10. Note anything unusual on the computer or on nearby objects, such as loose screws.
11. Disconnect all cables and connected devices.
12. Remove the storage media and treat them as evidence.
13. Put evidence tape over the disk drive openings and over the case. If someone uses a drive or opens the case, the tape will reveal it. Put initials and date on the edge of the tape. That way the drives are preserved until an exact copy is made for analysis, and the original disk is secured as evidence.

**Part 2. Seizure, Packaging, and Transport of Computer Items or Devices**

1. Seize other objects that store information: DVDs, CDs, flash memory (drives or cards), disks, tapes, or cartridges.
2. Take into account mobile phones, data bank watches, personal digital assistants, pagers, and caller ID devices.
3. Search the area for written-down passwords.
4. Seize the devices connected to the computer: monitors, keyboards, mice, printers, external drives, cameras, modems, dongles, and power cables.
5. Seize the software and its manuals.
6. Pack the computers in their original boxes with their foam pieces, if any. If not, use other suitable boxes with bubble wrap or solid foam. Do not use packing peanuts or shredded paper, which generate static electricity and dust.
7. Put loose storage media in paper boxes or bags. Do not use plastic bags, because they can generate static electricity.
8. Mark each item on the item itself to identify it.
9. Seal each package and put initials and date.
10. Transport the evidence carefully and with these precautions:
    - a. Carry it inside the passenger compartment of the vehicle. Do not put it in the trunk next to a radio transmitter, which emits radio frequency energy and can damage the media.
    - b. Consider turning off the vehicle's radio during transport.
    - c. Do not expose the evidence to extreme heat or cold.
    - d. Photograph the scene after removing the evidence.
11. Note the equipment that was not seized.

Exam note:

- Step 8 of Part 2 asks to mark each item on the item itself. The chapter asks to try marking the container first, and for firearms it says "do not mark the item."
- Step 2 of Part 1 requires latex gloves. The chapter allows latex or cotton gloves.
- Point 6.b (networked systems) appears within the standalone equipment step, although it deals with a different case.
- Point 10.d (photograph the scene after removing the evidence) appears among the transport precautions, although it is not one of them.
- This policy seizes all peripherals, whereas the chapter asks to send to the laboratory only the central units and the media. Seizing is one thing and sending is another.

### Appendix — Standards in Security (pp. 294–298)

- Pending: p. 294 has no extractable text. It is probably the appendix cover page. It should be checked in the book to make sure it contains nothing else.

#### Definition and Principles

- **Standard:** a set of criteria, guidelines, and best practices that improves the quality and reliability of products, services, or processes.
- Thanks to national and international standards, many parts, processes, and systems work regardless of who creates or runs them, who uses them, or where.
- They are created to meet a market need or fill a gap in industry standards.
- They are coordinated through accredited standards developing organizations (SDOs). SDOs bring together stakeholders from the private and public sectors to identify best practices.
- They are the basis of innovation and competitiveness. They provide a common language that drives national and international trade.
- **Four development principles:**

| # | Principle |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Consensus |
| 2 | Openness |
| 3 | Due process |
| 4 | Transparency |

- In security, standards help nations, communities, societies, organizations, and individuals protect their assets.
- They also improve their resilience to natural and human-caused threats.

#### Types of Standards

| Type | Features | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Voluntary standards | Voluntary but widely adopted. Some are integrated into the regulatory framework of several countries | ISO (International Organization for Standardization) and national standards institutes. In the U.S., trade or professional associations such as ASIS International (security management standards) |
| Statutory or regulatory standards | Mandatory by law. Formal authorities can enforce them | — |
| Mixed standards | The line blurs when a voluntary standard is incorporated into laws or regulations. This happens in fire, construction, and life safety. At other times the standard remains voluntary in theory, but mandatory in practice | Insurers set the availability and cost of casualty insurance according to the use of approved materials or standardized practices. Contracts include standards as a requirement |

- Contracts show that the adoption of standards increasingly depends on what the client asks for (client driven).
- **ISO areas relevant to asset protection:**
  - Facility management.
  - Health, environment, and occupational safety.
  - Risk management.
  - Security and resilience.
  - IT and information security.
- **Other bodies that set standards:**

| Body | Scope |
| --- | --- |
| NFPA (National Fire Protection Association, fire protection) | Not all of its standards are voluntary. Some are incorporated into regulations, such as building codes |
| UL (Underwriters Laboratories) | Several standards on security equipment: locks, alarms, and access control systems |
| Illuminating Engineering Society | Lighting standards and practices |
| Electronic Industries Association | Electronic components and products |
| ASIS International | Broad library of security management standards and guidelines (asisonline.org, standards-guidelines section) |

#### Benefits of Standards

According to the book, standards make the security professional's work easier. They can fulfill one or all of these functions (complete list, 9):

1. Put best practices and processes in writing (codify them), and share lessons learned.
2. Provide tools to assess threats, risks, vulnerabilities, criticalities, and impacts.
3. Define measurement methods.
4. Document equipment performance requirements, to ensure its effectiveness and safety.
5. Set design requirements so that devices, systems, and infrastructure withstand threats.
6. Define effective methods to identify people.
7. Increase knowledge and competence.
8. Improve information exchange between jurisdictions and interoperability.
9. Provide consistency in services.

#### Management System Standards

- They help organizations improve how they deliver services and run processes.
- They are widely accepted and used in many fields and disciplines.
- The best known:

| Standard | Topic |
| --- | --- |
| ISO 9001 | Quality management systems |
| ISO 14001 | Environmental management systems |

- The management systems approach calls for analyzing the requirements of the organization and its stakeholders. Then, defining the processes that lead to success.
- A management system provides the framework for continual improvement. This raises the probability of achieving strategic, operational, tactical, and reputational objectives.
- At the same time it improves the organization's resilience.

#### The PDCA Cycle (Plan-Do-Check-Act)

- It is the operating principle of management system standards.
- It is a structured method for solving problems, centered on continual improvement.

| Phase | According to the text | According to Figure A.1 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plan | Identify and analyze the organization's problems and assets, and rank them by importance | Define and analyze an issue and its context |
| Do | Prioritize the next steps, make a detailed action plan, and execute it | Devise a solution, make a detailed action plan, and execute it systematically |
| Check | Verify whether the solutions give results consistent with the plan | Confirm the results against the plan and identify deviations and problems |
| Act | If the solutions solve the problems, standardize them across the organization, review the list of problems, and define the new ones. Here the cycle starts again | Standardize the solution, review and define the next issues |

#### Figure A.1 and Its Source

- **Title and source:** "Security and Resilience in Organizations and their Supply Chains – Requirements with Guidance," ASIS ORM.1-2017.
- Elements of the figure:

| Block | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Input | Stakeholders and Interested Parties → Requirements and Expectations |
| Center | Organizational Resilience Management Systems, with the PLAN-DO-CHECK-ACT MODEL cycle |
| Output | Stakeholders and Interested Parties → Managed Risk |
| Phases within the cycle | The four in the "Figure A.1" column of the table above |

- Note: the text of the figure came out with a broken font. It was reconstructed from the glyph pattern. It should be checked against the book.

#### Contradictions

- Exam note: in the **Plan** phase, the text speaks of identifying problems and assets and ranking them by importance. The figure speaks of defining and analyzing an issue and its context.
- Exam note: in the **Do** phase, the text begins with "prioritize the next steps." The figure begins with "devise a solution" and adds executing it "systematically."
- Exam note: in the **Check** phase, the figure adds "identify deviations and problems," which the text does not mention.
- Exam note: the text presents PDCA in general, for any management system standard. The figure is the version applied to organizational resilience from the ASIS ORM.1-2017 standard, which the text does not name.

#### Key Terms

standard · standards developing organizations (SDOs) · consensus · openness · due process · transparency · resilience · voluntary standards · statutory/regulatory standards · mixed standards · ISO · NFPA · UL · Illuminating Engineering Society · Electronic Industries Association · casualty insurance · client driven · management system standards · ISO 9001 · ISO 14001 · continual improvement · Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA) · ASIS ORM.1-2017 · Organizational Resilience Management Systems · stakeholders and interested parties · managed risk

#### Review Questions

1. What are the four principles with which standards are developed? → Consensus, openness, due process, and transparency.
2. What distinguishes a voluntary standard from a statutory or regulatory one? → The statutory one is mandatory by law and is enforced by formal authorities. The voluntary one is not.
3. Give an example of a standard that is voluntary in theory but mandatory in practice. → Insurers make casualty insurance conditional on the use of approved materials, or contracts require the standard.
4. What are the two best-known management system standards? → ISO 9001 (quality) and ISO 14001 (environmental).
5. What is PDCA? → The operating principle of management system standards: solving problems in a structured way with continual improvement.
6. What standard is Figure A.1 from? → ASIS ORM.1-2017, Security and Resilience in Organizations and their Supply Chains – Requirements with Guidance.
